Module 1·About 1 week + daily useMust

Linux and the command line

15 topics · about 1.5 h of reading

Almost every Java application you write will end up running on a Linux server: an AWS EC2 machine, a Docker container, a Kubernetes pod, or a build agent in CI. Your laptop may run Windows, but production does not. This module teaches you the Linux command line well enough to log in to a server, find your app, read its logs, fix a crash, deploy a new JAR and keep it running after you log out.

Everything later on this page builds on it. Docker (Module 3) is Linux underneath. GitHub Actions (Module 4) runs your steps in Linux shells. Cloud deployment (Module 5) starts with an SSH login. The networking ideas in Module 2 use the same tools you meet here (ss, curl). Operating-system theory (processes, files, memory) is explained in CS Fundamentals · Module 1; here we stay practical.

Plan for daily practice: open a Linux terminal every day, even for a ten-minute task. Typing the commands is the only way they stick.

After this module you will be able to:

  • Set up Ubuntu (or WSL2 on Windows) and move around the filesystem confidently.
  • Read and change file permissions, and explain why an SSH key must be 600.
  • List processes and ports, stop a stuck program, and fix "address already in use".
  • Run a Spring Boot JAR as a systemd service and read its logs with journalctl.
  • Search and summarise logs with grep, awk, sort and uniq.
  • Install software with apt, set environment variables, and log in with SSH keys.
  • Write a safe bash script, schedule it with cron, and test an API with curl.

Why servers run Linux: kernel, distributions, shells and WSL2

Linux is a free operating-system kernel that powers most servers and containers; you use it through a shell (usually bash) inside a terminal, and on Windows 11 you get a real Ubuntu with wsl --install.

Why servers run Linux

When you rent a virtual machine from AWS, Azure or Google Cloud, the default choice is Linux. The reasons are practical, not fashion:

  • Cost. Linux is free. Windows Server needs a licence, which is added to your cloud bill.
  • Light and stable. A Linux server runs happily with 1 GB of RAM and stays up for months without a reboot.
  • Everything is text. Configuration is in plain files, and every task can be scripted. That is what makes automation (CI/CD, Docker, Terraform) possible.
  • Remote by default. You manage a server over SSH with no graphical desktop. A server has no screen, so there is nothing to click.
  • The ecosystem. Docker, Kubernetes, Nginx, PostgreSQL, Kafka and the JVM were built and tested on Linux first.

Kernel versus distribution

The kernel is the core of the operating system. It talks to the hardware, schedules processes, manages memory and files. "Linux" strictly means this kernel. A distribution (distro) packages the kernel with a package manager, system tools, default settings and a release schedule. Two distros run the same kernel family but differ in how you install software.

DistributionFamilyPackage toolWhere you meet it
Ubuntu (LTS releases)DebianaptMost tutorials, EC2, WSL
DebianDebianaptBase of many Docker images
Amazon LinuxRed Hat stylednf / yumDefault AWS images
RHEL, Rocky, AlmaLinuxRed Hatdnf / yumLarge enterprises, banks
AlpineIndependentapkVery small Docker images

These notes use Ubuntu. An LTS (long-term support) release gets security updates for years, so it is the safe choice for servers. The ideas (files, permissions, processes, SSH) are the same on every distro; only the install command changes.

Terminal, shell and bash

Terminal
The window where you type. It only shows text and sends your keystrokes.
Shell
The program inside the terminal that reads your command, runs it and prints the result. Common shells are bash (default on Ubuntu and most servers), zsh (default on macOS) and sh (minimal; used by many scripts and Alpine).
Prompt
The text before your cursor, for example ubuntu@web-1:~$ = user ubuntu on host web-1, in the home folder ~. $ means a normal user; # means root.

Learn bash. Scripts, CI pipelines and Dockerfiles are all written for it.

$ whoami
ubuntu
$ hostname
web-1
$ echo $SHELL
/bin/bash
$ cat /etc/os-release | head -3
PRETTY_NAME="Ubuntu 24.04 LTS"
NAME="Ubuntu"
VERSION_ID="24.04"
$ uname -r
6.8.0-1012-aws

uname -r prints the kernel version; /etc/os-release tells you the distro. Your numbers will differ — these are sample outputs.

Installing WSL2 on Windows 11

WSL2 (Windows Subsystem for Linux) runs a real Linux kernel in a lightweight virtual machine inside Windows. You get Ubuntu in a terminal, with no dual-boot. Open PowerShell as Administrator:

PS> wsl --install
PS> wsl --list --online
PS> wsl --install -d Ubuntu
PS> wsl -l -v
  NAME      STATE           VERSION
* Ubuntu    Running         2

wsl --install turns on the needed Windows features and installs Ubuntu by default; restart when asked. On first launch Ubuntu asks you to create a Linux username and password. That password is for sudo inside WSL, separate from your Windows password. Check that the VERSION column says 2.

Windows files from WSL, and back

You wantHow
Open a Windows folder from WSLcd /mnt/c/Users/asha/Downloads (drive C: appears as /mnt/c)
Open a WSL folder in Windows Explorerexplorer.exe . from the Linux folder, or type \\wsl$\Ubuntu\home\you in Explorer's address bar
Open a WSL project in VS Codecode . inside the folder (VS Code's WSL extension connects)
Run a Windows program from WSLType its name with .exe, e.g. notepad.exe notes.txt

Tip: keep your projects inside the Linux filesystem

Work in ~/projects, not in /mnt/c/.... Reading thousands of small files (a Maven build, node_modules) through /mnt/c is much slower, and file permissions behave oddly there. Access the files from Windows through the \\wsl$ path when you need to.

Git Bash versus WSL

Git BashWSL2 (Ubuntu)
What it isBash and common tools compiled for Windows, installed with GitA real Linux system
Linux fidelityPartial: no apt, systemctl, real permissionsFull: same as a server
Good forGit commands, light bash scriptsLearning Linux, Docker, building like production
Paths/c/Users/.../mnt/c/Users/...

Use WSL2 for this module. Git Bash is fine for daily Git work, but it will teach you habits that do not match a server.

Mistake: copying Windows line endings into scripts

A script saved in Notepad with CRLF line endings fails on Linux with bad interpreter: /bin/bash^M or $'\r': command not found. Save shell scripts with LF endings (VS Code shows CRLF/LF in the status bar), or convert with dos2unix script.sh after sudo apt install dos2unix.

Edge cases

  • If wsl --install only prints help text, WSL is already present; use wsl --install -d Ubuntu to add a distro.
  • WSL needs virtualisation enabled in BIOS/UEFI. The error mentions "virtualization" when it is off.
  • Linux is case-sensitive: App.java and app.java are different files. This is the classic "works on my Windows laptop, fails on the server" bug.

Interview: "Why do most production servers run Linux?"

  • Free, stable, light, scriptable, remote-first; the tools of the cloud era were built on it.
  • Know the difference: the kernel is the core; a distribution adds packages and defaults.
  • Mention that you practise on Ubuntu or WSL2 and can work without a GUI.

The filesystem: hierarchy, paths and ls -la

Linux has one tree starting at /; know where config (/etc), logs (/var/log) and your files (/home) live, and read ls -la column by column.

One tree, no drive letters

Windows has C: and D:. Linux has a single tree that starts at the root directory, written /. Other disks are mounted into the tree at some folder. Every file on the machine has a place below /.

DirectoryWhat lives thereExample
/homeOne folder per user/home/ubuntu/projects
/etcSystem-wide configuration (text files)/etc/nginx/nginx.conf
/var/logLogs from the system and services/var/log/syslog, /var/log/nginx/
/optSelf-contained third-party apps/opt/billing/billing.jar
/usr/binCommands installed by packages/usr/bin/java (often a link)
/tmpScratch space for anyoneUpload staging, temporary dumps
/dev/nullA special file that discards everything written to itcmd > /dev/null

Absolute and relative paths

An absolute path starts with / and means the same thing from anywhere: /etc/nginx/nginx.conf. A relative path does not start with / and is read from your current directory. Three shortcuts help:

  • ~ is your home directory (/home/ubuntu). ~/logs is always the same place.
  • . is the current directory. ./run.sh runs the script in this folder.
  • .. is the parent directory. cd .. goes up one level.
$ pwd
/home/ubuntu
$ cd /var/log
$ pwd
/var/log
$ cd ../lib
$ pwd
/var/lib
$ cd ~
$ pwd
/home/ubuntu
$ cd -
/var/lib

cd - jumps back to the previous directory. cd alone goes home.

Hidden files

A file whose name starts with a dot is hidden: .bashrc, .ssh, .gitignore. Plain ls skips them; ls -a shows them. Configuration for your user lives in such "dotfiles".

Reading ls -la column by column

$ ls -la
total 24
drwxr-xr-x  4 ubuntu ubuntu 4096 Nov 11 10:02 .
drwxr-x--- 12 ubuntu ubuntu 4096 Nov 11 09:58 ..
-rw-------  1 ubuntu ubuntu  412 Nov 10 18:30 .bash_history
-rwxr-xr-x  1 ubuntu ubuntu  120 Nov 11 10:02 deploy.sh
drwxr-xr-x  2 ubuntu ubuntu 4096 Nov 11 10:00 logs
-rw-r--r--  1 ubuntu ubuntu   42 Nov 11 10:01 notes.txt

Take the line -rwxr-xr-x 1 ubuntu ubuntu 120 Nov 11 10:02 deploy.sh:

  1. Type and permissions (-rwxr-xr-x). The first character is the type: - regular file, d directory, l symbolic link. The next nine characters are three groups of rwx for owner, group and others (see Permissions).
  2. Link count (1). Number of hard links. For a directory it is roughly 2 plus the number of sub-directories. You rarely need it.
  3. Owner (ubuntu). The user who owns the file.
  4. Group (ubuntu). The group that owns the file.
  5. Size (120) in bytes. Add -h (ls -lah) for 4.0K, 2.1M.
  6. Modified time (Nov 11 10:02). For files older than about six months it shows the year instead of the time.
  7. Name (deploy.sh). A link shows name -> target.

The first two lines, . and .., are the directory itself and its parent. total 24 is the disk blocks used by the listed files, not a file count.

Globbing: wildcards the shell expands

Before a command runs, the shell expands patterns called globs into file names. The command never sees the star; it sees the list.

PatternMatchesExample
*Any characters (including none)ls *.log
?Exactly one characterls app-?.log → app-1.log
[abc], [0-9]One character from the set or rangels app-[1-3].log
{a,b}Brace expansion (not a glob, but handy)mkdir -p logs/{2025,2026}
 
$ ls
app-1.log  app-2.log  app-10.log  notes.txt
$ ls app-?.log
app-1.log  app-2.log
$ ls app-*.log
app-10.log  app-1.log  app-2.log
$ echo *.txt
notes.txt

The star does not match hidden files, and it does not cross /.

Rule: quote names that contain spaces

The shell splits on spaces. cd My Projects passes two arguments. Write cd "My Projects", or better, never use spaces in file names on servers — use my-projects or my_projects.

Edge cases

  • If no file matches a glob, bash passes the pattern as plain text: ls *.xyz prints ls: cannot access '*.xyz': No such file or directory.
  • /home/ubuntu is not /home/ubuntu/ to a human, but to the system both are the same directory. A trailing slash matters only for a few tools such as rsync.
  • Symbolic links: /usr/bin/java is usually a link to a JDK folder. ls -l shows -> and the target.

Interview: "Where would you look for logs and config on a Linux server?"

  • Logs in /var/log (and journalctl for systemd services); config in /etc; apps you install by hand in /opt.
  • Explain absolute versus relative paths and the meaning of ~, ., ...

Everyday file commands: ls, cp, mv, rm, find, less and more

Create, copy, move, delete, view and find files with a small set of commands — and treat rm -rf with respect, because Linux has no recycle bin.

The core commands

CommandPurposeCommon use
pwdPrint the current directorypwd
lsList filesls -lah, ls -lt (newest first)
cdChange directorycd /opt/billing
mkdirMake a directorymkdir -p app/logs/archive
touchCreate an empty file or update its timetouch notes.txt
cpCopycp a.txt b.txt, cp -r dir dir2
mvMove or renamemv old.txt new.txt
rmDeleterm file, rm -r dir
catPrint a whole filecat application.yml
lessScroll through a fileless app.log
head / tailFirst / last linestail -n 50 app.log
 
$ mkdir -p demo/app/logs
$ cd demo
$ touch app/logs/app.log app/README.md
$ cp app/README.md app/README.bak
$ mv app/README.bak app/README.old
$ cp -r app app-copy
$ ls -R
.:
app  app-copy

./app:
README.md  README.old  logs

./app/logs:
app.log
...
$ rm app/README.old
$ rm -r app-copy

mkdir -p creates all missing parent folders and does not complain if the folder exists. cp -r (recursive) is required for folders. mv is both "move" and "rename": moving to the same folder with a new name is a rename.

The danger of rm -rf

rm deletes immediately and permanently. There is no trash. -r means recursive (go into directories); -f means force (no questions, ignore missing files). Together, rm -rf will wipe a whole tree without asking. Real incidents come from an empty variable: rm -rf "$APP_DIR"/* becomes rm -rf /* when APP_DIR is unset.

Rule: look before you delete

  • Run ls with the same pattern first, then edit the command into rm.
  • Use rm -ri (interactive) when unsure; it asks for each file.
  • Never run rm -rf with a variable you have not checked. In scripts use set -u (see Bash scripting).
  • Never type sudo rm -rf / or rm -rf /*. Ever.

Viewing files

cat prints everything; fine for short files, bad for a 2 GB log. For long files use less, which opens a pager. Its keys are the same ones man uses:

Key in lessAction
Space / bPage down / page up
↓ ↑ or j kLine down / up
g / GGo to start / end of file
/error then n / NSearch forward; next / previous match
FFollow the end of the file live (like tail -f); Ctrl+C to stop
qQuit
 
$ head -n 3 app.log
2026-11-11T10:00:01.120+05:30  INFO 4120 --- [main] c.e.BillingApplication : Starting
2026-11-11T10:00:03.456+05:30  INFO 4120 --- [main] c.e.BillingApplication : Started in 2.4s
2026-11-11T10:05:10.002+05:30  WARN 4120 --- [io-8080-exec-1] c.e.OrderService : Slow query
$ tail -n 1 app.log
2026-11-11T10:09:44.870+05:30 ERROR 4120 --- [io-8080-exec-3] c.e.PaymentClient : Timeout

tail -f app.log keeps the file open and prints new lines as they arrive; it is the standard way to watch a log live (Text tools).

Finding files with find

find walks a directory tree and tests each file. The shape is find WHERE TESTS ACTION.

$ find /var/log -name "*.log"
/var/log/dpkg.log
/var/log/nginx/access.log
$ find . -type d -name logs
./app/logs
$ find /opt/billing -type f -mtime -1
/opt/billing/billing.jar
$ find /tmp -type f -size +100M
/tmp/dump.sql
$ find . -name "*.tmp" -delete
TestMeaning
-name "*.log"Name matches the pattern (quote it so the shell does not expand it); -iname ignores case
-type f / -type dRegular files / directories
-mtime -1Modified within the last 1 day; -mtime +7 = older than 7 days
-size +100MBigger than 100 megabytes
-delete / -exec cmd {} \;Delete matches / run a command on each match

Getting help: which, man and --help

$ which java
/usr/bin/java
$ type cd
cd is a shell builtin
$ ls --help | head -3
Usage: ls [OPTION]... [FILE]...
List information about the FILEs (the current directory by default).
$ man tail

which shows which program runs when you type a name — the first thing to check when "the wrong Java" starts. man tail opens the manual page in less (quit with q). command --help is a shorter summary. Shell builtins such as cd have no man page of their own; use help cd.

Tip: speed up the terminal

  • Tab completes file and command names; press it twice to list options.
  • ↑ recalls earlier commands; Ctrl+R searches your history.
  • Ctrl+C stops a running command; Ctrl+L clears the screen; Ctrl+A / Ctrl+E jump to the start / end of the line.
  • !! repeats the last command: sudo !! retries it as root.

Mistake: cp and mv silently overwrite

cp a.txt b.txt replaces b.txt without a warning. Use cp -i or mv -i to be asked first, and cp -n to never overwrite. Also remember: mv file dir moves into the folder if dir exists, but renames file to dir if it does not.

Errors you will see

  • No such file or directory — a wrong path, a typo, or a case difference.
  • Permission denied — you lack the right on the file or folder (next article).
  • cp: -r not specified; omitting directory 'x' — add -r.
  • rm: cannot remove 'x': Is a directory — add -r (or use rmdir for an empty folder).
  • Argument list too long — a glob expanded to too many files; use find ... -delete or xargs.

Interview: "How do you find large files or recent changes on a server?"

  • find / -type f -size +500M 2>/dev/null for big files; du -sh * to see which folder is big; find -mtime -1 for what changed today.
  • Mention that 2>/dev/null hides "permission denied" noise.

Users, groups, permissions, chmod, chown and sudo

Every file has an owner, a group and three permission sets (read, write, execute for user, group, others); chmod changes the bits, chown changes the owner, and sudo runs one command as root.

Why permissions exist

Linux is multi-user. Your web server, your database and you should not be able to read each other's secrets or delete each other's files. Every file therefore records who owns it and who may do what. If a compromised app runs as a low-privilege user, the attacker cannot touch the rest of the system. This is the base of server security.

Reading rwx

From the ls -l output: -rwxr-xr-- has a type character and three groups of three.

BitOn a fileOn a directory
r read (4)View the contentsList the names inside (ls)
w write (2)Change the contentsCreate, rename and delete files inside
x execute (1)Run it as a program or scriptEnter it (cd) and access files by name

The system checks in order: if you are the owner, the owner bits apply; else if you are in the group, the group bits apply; else the "others" bits apply. Root ignores these checks.

Octal notation

Add the values r=4, w=2, x=1 for each group. rwx = 7, r-x = 5, rw- = 6, r-- = 4, --- = 0. Three digits give owner, group, others.

OctalSymbolicTypical use
755rwxr-xr-xPrograms, scripts, directories everyone may enter
644rw-r--r--Normal files, config others may read
600rw-------Private files: SSH private keys, .env secrets
700rwx------Private directories, e.g. ~/.ssh
777rwxrwxrwxEveryone can do everything. Almost never correct

chmod: change the mode

Two styles. Octal sets all nine bits at once. Symbolic changes some bits: who (u user/owner, g group, o others, a all), an operator (+, -, =), and the bits.

$ ls -l deploy.sh
-rw-r--r-- 1 ubuntu ubuntu 120 Nov 11 10:02 deploy.sh
$ ./deploy.sh
bash: ./deploy.sh: Permission denied
$ chmod +x deploy.sh
$ ls -l deploy.sh
-rwxr-xr-x 1 ubuntu ubuntu 120 Nov 11 10:02 deploy.sh
$ chmod 600 secrets.env
$ chmod u+x,g-w,o-rwx deploy.sh
$ chmod -R 755 public/
$ ls -l secrets.env deploy.sh
-rw------- 1 ubuntu ubuntu  64 Nov 11 10:03 secrets.env
-rwxr-x--- 1 ubuntu ubuntu 120 Nov 11 10:02 deploy.sh

-R applies the change to a whole tree. Be careful: giving 755 recursively to a tree also makes every data file executable. Use find -type d -exec chmod 755 and find -type f -exec chmod 644 to treat folders and files separately.

chown: change the owner

$ sudo chown billing:billing /opt/billing/billing.jar
$ sudo chown -R billing:billing /opt/billing
$ sudo chown ubuntu app.log
$ ls -l /opt/billing/billing.jar
-rw-r--r-- 1 billing billing 58123456 Nov 11 10:10 /opt/billing/billing.jar

The format is user:group. Only root can give a file to another user, so you need sudo. Common reason: you copied a JAR as ubuntu but the service runs as billing and cannot write its own log folder.

Users, groups and sudo

$ whoami
ubuntu
$ id
uid=1000(ubuntu) gid=1000(ubuntu) groups=1000(ubuntu),27(sudo),999(docker)
$ sudo adduser deploy
$ sudo usermod -aG sudo deploy
$ sudo usermod -aG docker deploy
$ groups deploy
deploy : deploy sudo docker
  • root is the all-powerful user (UID 0). It can read, change and delete anything. You should almost never log in as root.
  • sudo ("superuser do") runs one command as root after asking for your own password (on EC2 Ubuntu the default user has passwordless sudo). Members of the sudo group on Ubuntu may use it. sudo -i opens a root shell; avoid leaving it open.
  • adduser creates a user with a home folder (the friendly Debian/Ubuntu command). usermod -aG GROUP USER adds the user to a group. Always include -a: without it, you replace all the user's groups.
  • Adding a user to docker lets them run Docker without sudo, but it is effectively root-level power. The user must log out and in for new groups to apply.
  • Service accounts: create a system user with no login for each app, for example sudo adduser --system --group billing (checked on Ubuntu/Debian).

Why SSH keys must be 600

An SSH private key is a password in a file. If other users could read it, they could log in as you. So the SSH client refuses to use a key that is readable by others:

$ ssh -i mykey.pem ubuntu@203.0.113.10
@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@
@         WARNING: UNPROTECTED PRIVATE KEY FILE!          @
@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@@
Permissions 0644 for 'mykey.pem' are too open.
$ chmod 600 mykey.pem
$ ssh -i mykey.pem ubuntu@203.0.113.10

On the server, ~/.ssh should be 700 and authorized_keys 600, or the SSH server may ignore it.

Mistake: fixing "Permission denied" with chmod 777

chmod 777 makes every user and every compromised process able to modify the file. It hides the real problem (wrong owner, or missing x). Find out who needs access, then use chown or the minimum bits, such as chmod 750.

Mistake: sudo for everything

sudo mvn package or sudo npm install creates root-owned files in your project, and later builds fail with permission errors. Run builds as yourself. Use sudo only for things that really need root: apt, systemctl, editing /etc.

Edge cases

  • To delete a file you need w on the directory, not on the file.
  • A directory without x cannot be entered even if you can read its name.
  • A shell script needs x to run as ./script.sh, but bash script.sh needs only r.
  • sudo echo "x" > /etc/file fails: the redirection is done by your shell, not by sudo. Use echo "x" | sudo tee /etc/file (add -a to append).
  • Special bits (setuid, sticky bit on /tmp) exist; you can ignore them for now.
  • On WSL, files under /mnt/c may show 777-like permissions regardless of chmod.

Interview: "What does chmod 755 mean? Why is chmod 777 bad?"

  • 7 = rwx for the owner; 5 = r-x for group and others. Owner full control, others read and run.
  • 777 lets any user or process change or replace the file — a security hole and usually a sign of a wrong owner.
  • Add: SSH private keys need 600; use least privilege, run services as a non-root user.

Processes: ps, top, signals, kill and background jobs

A process is a running program with a PID; list them with ps aux or top, stop them politely with kill (signal 15) and only use kill -9 as a last resort.

What a process is

When you run java -jar app.jar, Linux starts a process: your program loaded in memory, with its own PID (process id), an owner, and a parent process. Your shell is a process too, and it is the parent of every command you run from it. The very first process is systemd (PID 1), which starts the services of the machine. For the theory (scheduling, memory, threads) see CS Fundamentals · Module 1.

Listing processes: ps

$ ps aux | head -3
USER   PID %CPU %MEM    VSZ   RSS TTY   STAT START  TIME COMMAND
root     1  0.0  0.4 167680 11520 ?     Ss   09:00  0:03 /sbin/init
root     2  0.0  0.0      0     0 ?     S    09:00  0:00 [kthreadd]
$ ps aux | grep billing
billing 4120  3.1 12.5 3456788 502340 ?  Sl   10:00  1:12 /usr/bin/java -jar billing.jar
ubuntu  4388  0.0  0.0   9032   720 pts/0 S+  10:20  0:00 grep --color=auto billing
$ pgrep -f billing.jar
4120
$ ps -p 4120 -o pid,ppid,etime,cmd
    PID    PPID     ELAPSED CMD
   4120       1       20:11 /usr/bin/java -jar billing.jar

Columns: USER owner; PID; %CPU and %MEM; RSS real memory in KB (here about 490 MB); STAT state (S sleeping, R running, Z zombie, l multi-threaded); COMMAND. Note that the grep itself shows up in its own result. Use pgrep -f to avoid this, or ps aux | grep [b]illing. ps aux uses BSD-style options (no dash) and shows all users' processes.

Live view: top and htop

$ top
top - 10:21:05 up 1:21,  1 user,  load average: 0.35, 0.28, 0.20
Tasks: 112 total,   1 running, 111 sleeping,   0 stopped,   0 zombie
%Cpu(s):  4.0 us,  1.0 sy,  0.0 ni, 94.5 id,  0.3 wa
MiB Mem :   1948.4 total,    210.2 free,   1120.5 used,    617.7 buff/cache
MiB Swap:      0.0 total,      0.0 free,      0.0 used.    650.1 avail Mem

    PID USER   PR  NI    VIRT    RES    SHR S  %CPU  %MEM  COMMAND
   4120 billing 20   0 3456788 502340  24160 S   3.1  25.2  java

The load average is the average number of runnable processes over 1, 5 and 15 minutes; compare it to your CPU count. Inside top: P sorts by CPU, M by memory, k asks for a PID to kill, q quits. htop is a friendlier version (sudo apt install htop) with colours, mouse and a tree view.

Signals: asking a process to stop

A signal is a small message the kernel delivers to a process. kill does not necessarily "kill": it sends a signal, and by default it sends SIGTERM.

SignalNumberMeaningCan the app handle it?
SIGTERM15"Please shut down" (default)Yes: cleanup, close connections
SIGINT2Interrupt; what Ctrl+C sendsYes
SIGKILL9"Die now"; the kernel ends itNo: cannot be caught
SIGHUP1Terminal closed; some daemons reload configYes
 
$ kill 4120
$ ps -p 4120
    PID TTY          TIME CMD
$ kill -9 4120
$ pkill -f billing.jar
$ killall nginx

Always try kill -15 (same as plain kill) first. A Spring Boot app handles SIGTERM by running its shutdown hooks: it stops accepting requests, finishes work in progress and closes its database pool. kill -9 skips all of that and can leave half-written files or open transactions. Use it only when the process ignores SIGTERM for a long time. A process that exited after SIGTERM reports exit code 143 (128 + 15); after SIGKILL, 137 (128 + 9). Both numbers show up in Docker and Kubernetes too.

pkill -f pattern matches the full command line; killall name matches the program name. Check with pgrep -af pattern first so you do not kill the wrong thing.

Background jobs

A command normally owns the terminal until it ends. You can move work to the background:

$ java -jar billing.jar &
[1] 4120
$ jobs
[1]+  Running                 java -jar billing.jar &
$ fg %1
java -jar billing.jar
^Z
[1]+  Stopped                 java -jar billing.jar
$ bg %1
[1]+ java -jar billing.jar &
$ nohup java -jar billing.jar > app.log 2>&1 &
[1] 4150
  • cmd & starts in the background. jobs lists this shell's jobs.
  • Ctrl+Z pauses the foreground job; bg resumes it in the background, fg brings it back.
  • When you close the terminal or the SSH session, the shell sends SIGHUP to its jobs and they die. nohup ("no hang up") makes the command ignore SIGHUP so it survives logout. Output goes to nohup.out unless you redirect it as shown.

Convention: nohup is for quick tests, systemd is for real services

A nohup process does not restart after a crash or a reboot, has no standard log handling and is hard to manage. For anything that must stay up, write a service unit (next article). tmux is another useful tool: a session that keeps running after you disconnect.

Mistake: kill -9 as the first move

Reaching for kill -9 every time means your app never runs its shutdown hooks. You may see lost messages, locked rows or corrupted files. Try kill, wait 10-30 seconds, check with ps, and only then escalate.

Edge cases

  • kill: (4120) - Operation not permitted — the process belongs to another user. Use sudo kill.
  • A zombie (Z) process has already exited but its parent has not collected its status. You cannot kill it; fix or restart the parent.
  • A process in state D (uninterruptible sleep, usually stuck on disk or NFS) ignores even kill -9 until the I/O returns.
  • The JVM may be killed by the kernel's OOM killer when the machine runs out of memory. The process just disappears; look for "Out of memory: Killed process" with dmesg or journalctl -k.

Interview: "Difference between kill -15 and kill -9?"

  • 15 (SIGTERM) asks the process to stop and lets it clean up; 9 (SIGKILL) cannot be caught or ignored, the kernel removes the process immediately.
  • Use 15 first; 9 only for a hung process. Exit codes 143 and 137.
  • Bonus: how nohup and systemd keep a process alive after logout.

systemd: running a Spring Boot JAR as a service

systemd starts services at boot, restarts them when they crash and collects their logs; you describe your app in a small unit file and control it with systemctl and journalctl.

What systemd does

systemd is the service manager on Ubuntu, Debian, Amazon Linux and RHEL. It is PID 1. A unit is something it manages; the most common is a service (a long-running program). For your Spring Boot JAR, systemd gives you what nohup cannot:

  • Start at boot, and start after the network is up.
  • Restart automatically after a crash.
  • Run as a limited user, with environment variables and a working directory.
  • Capture everything the app prints (stdout and stderr) in the journal.
  • A single, standard interface: start, stop, status.

systemctl commands

CommandWhat it does
sudo systemctl start billingStart now
sudo systemctl stop billingStop now (sends SIGTERM)
sudo systemctl restart billingStop then start
systemctl status billingState, PID, last log lines
sudo systemctl enable billingStart automatically at boot
sudo systemctl enable --now billingEnable and start in one step
sudo systemctl disable billingDo not start at boot
sudo systemctl daemon-reloadRe-read unit files after you edit them
systemctl is-active billingPrints active or inactive

start and enable are different: start affects the current boot, enable affects future boots. You usually want both.

A complete unit file for a Spring Boot JAR

First prepare a user, a folder and the files:

$ sudo adduser --system --group --no-create-home billing
$ sudo mkdir -p /opt/billing /etc/billing
$ sudo cp target/billing-1.0.0.jar /opt/billing/billing.jar
$ sudo chown -R billing:billing /opt/billing
$ sudo nano /etc/billing/billing.env
$ sudo chown root:billing /etc/billing/billing.env
$ sudo chmod 640 /etc/billing/billing.env

The environment file holds secrets, one KEY=value per line, no export:

SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod
SERVER_PORT=8080
SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL=jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/billing
SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME=billing
SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD=change-me

Now the unit file, created with sudo nano /etc/systemd/system/billing.service:

[Unit]
Description=Billing service (Spring Boot)
After=network.target

[Service]
Type=simple
User=billing
Group=billing
WorkingDirectory=/opt/billing
EnvironmentFile=/etc/billing/billing.env
ExecStart=/usr/bin/java -Xms256m -Xmx512m -jar /opt/billing/billing.jar
SuccessExitStatus=143
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5
TimeoutStopSec=30
SyslogIdentifier=billing

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target
After=network.target
Start this service after basic networking is up.
User / Group
Run as the unprivileged billing account, not root.
WorkingDirectory
The folder the process starts in; relative paths like ./config are resolved here.
EnvironmentFile
Loads variables from a file, so passwords stay out of the unit file and out of Git.
ExecStart
The command. Use full paths: systemd does not use your shell's PATH. Check with which java. Heap options go before -jar.
SuccessExitStatus=143
A JVM stopped by SIGTERM exits with 143. This tells systemd that is a normal stop, not a failure.
Restart=on-failure, RestartSec=5
Restart after a crash or non-success exit, waiting five seconds. Restart=always also restarts after a clean exit.
WantedBy=multi-user.target
What enable hooks into: start in normal multi-user boot.
$ sudo systemctl daemon-reload
$ sudo systemctl enable --now billing
Created symlink /etc/systemd/system/multi-user.target.wants/billing.service
$ systemctl status billing
● billing.service - Billing service (Spring Boot)
     Loaded: loaded (/etc/systemd/system/billing.service; enabled; preset: enabled)
     Active: active (running) since Tue 2026-11-11 10:30:12 IST; 8s ago
   Main PID: 5201 (java)
      Tasks: 38 (limit: 2300)
     Memory: 310.2M
     CGroup: /system.slice/billing.service
             └─5201 /usr/bin/java -Xms256m -Xmx512m -jar /opt/billing/billing.jar

Reading logs with journalctl

Anything your app writes to the console goes to the journal. Read it with journalctl:

$ journalctl -u billing -f
$ journalctl -u billing -n 100 --no-pager
$ journalctl -u billing --since "1 hour ago"
$ journalctl -u billing --since "2026-11-11 10:00" --until "2026-11-11 10:30"
$ journalctl -u billing -p err
$ journalctl -u billing -b
OptionMeaning
-u NAMEOnly this unit
-fFollow: show new lines live (Ctrl+C to stop)
-n 100The last 100 lines
--since / --untilTime window
-p errOnly priority err and worse
-bOnly the current boot

Pipe it for analysis: journalctl -u billing --since today --no-pager | grep ERROR.

Deploying a new version

$ sudo cp billing-1.0.1.jar /opt/billing/billing.jar
$ sudo chown billing:billing /opt/billing/billing.jar
$ sudo systemctl restart billing
$ systemctl is-active billing
active

If you edit the unit file itself, run sudo systemctl daemon-reload first; changing only the JAR or the env file needs just a restart.

Mistake: forgetting daemon-reload, or using relative paths

After editing billing.service, systemd warns: The unit file changed on disk. Run 'systemctl daemon-reload'. And ExecStart=java -jar billing.jar fails with Executable path is not absolute. Use /usr/bin/java and a full JAR path.

Why a service fails: status codes you will see

  • status=203/EXEC — systemd could not run ExecStart: wrong path to java or the JAR, or the file is not readable by the service user.
  • status=217/USER — the User= account does not exist.
  • status=1/FAILURE — the app started and exited; read journalctl -u billing -n 100 (often a database connection or "port already in use").
  • Restart loop: Active: activating (auto-restart). After too many quick restarts systemd gives up with start-limit-hit; fix the cause, then systemctl reset-failed billing.
  • Spring Boot also supports building an "executable" JAR; the explicit java -jar in ExecStart is clearer and works with all versions.

In real projects

On a single VM, many teams run the app exactly like this behind Nginx (Nginx config). With containers, Docker or Kubernetes replaces systemd (Module 3), but the ideas are the same: a restart policy, a non-root user, config from environment variables, logs to stdout.

Interview: "How do you keep your Spring Boot app running after you close SSH?"

  • Create a systemd unit, enable --now it; it survives logout and reboots and restarts on crash with Restart=on-failure.
  • Mention a dedicated user, EnvironmentFile for secrets and journalctl -u for logs. nohup or & are only quick tricks.

Ports on a server: ss, lsof and "address already in use"

A program listens on a port to accept connections; ss -tulpn shows who listens where, and "address already in use" means another process already owns that port.

What listening means

An IP address finds a machine; a port (0-65535) finds a program on it. A server program listens on one address and port, such as 0.0.0.0:8080. When a client connects, the kernel hands the connection to that program. Only one process can listen on a given address and port at a time. Ports below 1024 are "privileged": only root (or a process given special rights) may bind to them, which is why apps listen on 8080 and Nginx on 80 and 443. Full networking ideas are in Module 2.

ss -tulpn

$ sudo ss -tulpn
Netid State  Recv-Q Send-Q Local Address:Port  Peer Address:Port Process
tcp   LISTEN 0      4096   0.0.0.0:22          0.0.0.0:*         users:(("sshd",pid=801,fd=3))
tcp   LISTEN 0      511    0.0.0.0:80          0.0.0.0:*         users:(("nginx",pid=1100,fd=6))
tcp   LISTEN 0      200    127.0.0.1:5432      0.0.0.0:*         users:(("postgres",pid=950,fd=5))
tcp   LISTEN 0      100    *:8080              *:*               users:(("java",pid=5201,fd=9))
udp   UNCONN 0      0      127.0.0.53%lo:53    0.0.0.0:*         users:(("systemd-resolve",pid=620,fd=13))
FlagMeaning
-t / -uTCP / UDP sockets
-lOnly listening sockets
-pShow the process (needs sudo to see other users' processes)
-nNumbers, do not translate names (faster, clearer)

Read the Local Address column carefully:

  • 0.0.0.0:80 or *:8080 — listens on all network interfaces. Reachable from other machines (if the firewall allows).
  • 127.0.0.1:5432 — listens on localhost only. Only programs on the same machine can connect. This is the right setting for a database that only your app uses.

Many "my app is not reachable" problems are exactly this: the app listens on 127.0.0.1 only (see the checklist).

lsof and other ways to check a port

$ sudo lsof -i :8080
COMMAND  PID    USER   FD   TYPE DEVICE SIZE/OFF NODE NAME
java    5201 billing    9u  IPv6  45678      0t0  TCP *:8080 (LISTEN)
$ sudo ss -tulpn | grep :8080
tcp LISTEN 0 100 *:8080 *:* users:(("java",pid=5201,fd=9))
$ curl -I http://localhost:8080/actuator/health
HTTP/1.1 200
$ nc -zv localhost 8080
Connection to localhost (127.0.0.1) 8080 port [tcp/http-alt] succeeded!

lsof ("list open files") works because in Linux a network socket is a file. lsof -i :PORT is the shortest way to answer "who is using this port?". netstat is the older tool; ss replaces it and is installed by default.

Fixing "address already in use"

When you start a second copy of your app, or an old one is still running, Spring Boot fails at startup:

***************************
APPLICATION FAILED TO START
***************************

Description:

Web server failed to start. Port 8080 was already in use.

Action:

Identify and stop the process that's listening on port 8080 or configure this
application to listen on another port.
  1. Find the owner: sudo lsof -i :8080 or sudo ss -tulpn | grep :8080.
  2. Decide: is it your old app, a leftover from a crash, or something else important?
  3. Stop it politely: kill PID, or sudo systemctl stop billing if it is a service. Escalate to kill -9 PID only if needed.
  4. Start your app again. Or choose another port: java -jar app.jar --server.port=8081 (or the environment variable SERVER_PORT=8081).

On Windows the same job is netstat -ano | findstr :8080 then taskkill /PID 1234 /F.

Tip: a quick one-liner

Free a port you know is a stuck dev process: kill $(lsof -t -i :8080). lsof -t prints only the PIDs. Make sure the port really is yours before you run it.

Edge cases

  • Permission denied when binding port 80 or 443 as a normal user — ports below 1024 need root. Run the app on 8080 and let Nginx (running as a service) listen on 80/443.
  • After a crash, a port can stay in TIME_WAIT for about a minute; most servers set the socket option so a restart still works.
  • The same port may be used for TCP and UDP by two different programs: they are separate.
  • Inside Docker, ss shows the container's own network, not the host's.

Interview: "Your app says port 8080 is in use. What do you do?"

  • Find the PID with lsof -i :8080 or ss -tulpn; check it is safe; stop it with kill; restart. Or change server.port.
  • Explain what "listening" means and why 127.0.0.1 versus 0.0.0.0 matters.

Text tools: pipes, redirection, grep and tail -f

Linux tools each do one small job on text; pipes (|) connect them and redirection (>, >>, 2>&1) sends output to files, so a one-line pipeline can answer questions about gigabytes of logs.

Standard streams

Every process has three channels: stdin (0, input), stdout (1, normal output) and stderr (2, error messages). By default stdin is your keyboard and both outputs go to your screen. Redirection and pipes change where they go.

Redirection and pipes

SyntaxMeaning
cmd > fileWrite stdout to the file, replacing it
cmd >> fileAppend stdout to the file
cmd < fileRead stdin from the file
cmd 2> err.txtWrite only stderr to a file
cmd > out.txt 2>&1Send stderr to the same place as stdout (both into out.txt)
cmd > /dev/null 2>&1Throw away all output
cmd1 | cmd2Pipe: stdout of cmd1 becomes stdin of cmd2
cmd | tee fileShow on screen and save to a file
cmd1 && cmd2 / cmd1 || cmd2Run cmd2 only if cmd1 succeeded / failed

Order matters: 2>&1 means "make 2 point where 1 points now", so it must come after > file.

$ echo "build started" > build.log
$ echo "step 2 done" >> build.log
$ cat build.log
build started
step 2 done
$ ls /nonexistent 2> errors.txt
$ cat errors.txt
ls: cannot access '/nonexistent': No such file or directory
$ mvn package > build.log 2>&1
$ sort < names.txt | head -3
$ mkdir release && cp app.jar release/ || echo "failed"

grep: search text

grep prints the lines that match a pattern. It is the tool you will use most on servers.

OptionMeaning
-iIgnore case
-nShow line numbers
-rSearch a directory recursively
-vInvert: lines that do not match
-cOnly count matching lines
-EExtended regular expressions: a|b, +, ?, ()
-A 3 / -B 3 / -C 3Show 3 lines After / Before / around each match
-lOnly list names of files that match
-wMatch whole words only
 
$ grep ERROR app.log
2026-11-11T10:09:44.870+05:30 ERROR 4120 --- [io-8080-exec-3] c.e.PaymentClient : Timeout
2026-11-11T10:11:02.115+05:30 ERROR 4120 --- [io-8080-exec-5] c.e.OrderService : Order 991 failed
$ grep -c ERROR app.log
2
$ grep -n -i "timeout" app.log
5:2026-11-11T10:09:44.870+05:30 ERROR 4120 --- [io-8080-exec-3] c.e.PaymentClient : Timeout
$ grep -B1 -A2 "Order 991" app.log
$ grep -E "ERROR|WARN" app.log | grep -v "Slow query"
$ grep -rn "DataSource" /opt/billing/config/
$ grep -rl "password" /etc/nginx/ 2>/dev/null

-A/-B are very useful for Java logs: the line with ERROR is followed by a stack trace, so grep -A 15 "ERROR" prints the exception under each error. Quote patterns so the shell does not interpret *, | or $.

Watching logs live

$ tail -f /opt/billing/logs/app.log
$ tail -n 200 -f app.log
$ tail -f app.log | grep --line-buffered ERROR
$ tail -F app.log
$ less +F app.log
  • tail -f follows the file. -F also follows through log rotation, when the file is renamed and recreated; prefer it on production logs.
  • When you pipe tail -f into grep, add --line-buffered or the output may appear late.
  • less +F starts in follow mode; press Ctrl+C to stop following and scroll or search (/), then F to follow again. It is better than tail -f when you need to go back and look.
  • For a systemd service use journalctl -u NAME -f.

Small helpers: wc, sort, uniq, cut, tr

$ wc -l app.log
1240 app.log
$ grep -c ERROR app.log
18
$ cut -d: -f1,3 /etc/passwd | head -2
root:0
daemon:1
$ echo "Hello" | tr 'a-z' 'A-Z'
HELLO
$ sort names.txt | uniq
$ sort names.txt | uniq -c | sort -rn | head
ToolJob
wc -lCount lines (-w words, -c bytes)
sortSort lines; -n numeric, -r reverse, -k2 by column 2, -u unique
uniq -cCollapse adjacent equal lines and count them — so always sort first
cut -d, -f2Take field 2 with delimiter ,
tr a-z A-ZTranslate or delete characters (tr -d '\r' strips Windows line endings)
head / tailFirst / last lines

Mistake: truncating a file by redirecting onto itself

sort data.txt > data.txt empties the file before sort reads it. Write to a new file, then mv it back, or use sort -o data.txt data.txt. A single > overwrites; when in doubt use >>.

Mistake: uniq without sort

uniq only merges neighbours. uniq -c on unsorted input gives many partial counts. The standard idiom is sort | uniq -c | sort -rn.

Edge cases

  • grep exit code: 0 if something matched, 1 if nothing matched, 2 on error. In a script with set -e, a "no match" stops the script.
  • Binary file: Binary file x matches. Use grep -a to treat it as text.
  • Compressed rotated logs: zgrep ERROR app.log.2.gz searches without unzipping.
  • Basic grep treats +, | and () literally; add -E for regex features.

Interview: "How do you debug a production issue from logs?"

  • tail -f or journalctl -f to watch live; grep -n ERROR with -A context to see stack traces; narrow to the time window.
  • Summarise with sort | uniq -c | sort -rn (next article).
  • Explain stdout, stderr and what 2>&1 does.

Log analysis with awk, sed, sort, uniq and xargs

Combine grep, awk, sort and uniq -c to count errors, rank URLs and find the slowest requests in seconds; use sed for quick edits and xargs to run a command per line.

When production misbehaves, nobody opens a 500 MB log in an editor. You ask questions with a pipeline: how many errors? which URL fails most? which client is noisy? how slow is the slowest request? This article shows the patterns on two realistic logs.

awk in five minutes

awk reads text line by line, splits each line into fields (by whitespace, by default) and runs your code. $1 is the first field, $NF the last, $0 the whole line, NR the line number. The shape is awk 'CONDITION { ACTION }' file; BEGIN and END blocks run before the first and after the last line.

$ echo "alice 30 mumbai" | awk '{print $1, $3}'
alice mumbai
$ echo "a,b,c" | awk -F, '{print $2}'
b
$ awk 'NR <= 2' names.txt
$ awk '$2 > 100 {print $1}' data.txt
$ awk '{s += $2} END {print s}' data.txt

-F, sets the field separator. Conditions such as $7 ~ /^5/ use a regular expression match; ==, >, && work as in Java.

Example 1: an Nginx access log with request time

Suppose you added a log format that ends with the request time in seconds ($request_time), so each line looks like this:

203.0.113.7 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:30 +0530] "GET /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 200 512 0.043
203.0.113.7 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:31 +0530] "GET /api/orders/42 HTTP/1.1" 200 230 0.021
198.51.100.4 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:33 +0530] "POST /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 201 180 0.350
198.51.100.4 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:40 +0530] "GET /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 500 95 1.920
192.0.2.9 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:42 +0530] "GET /api/products HTTP/1.1" 200 2048 0.087
203.0.113.7 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:50 +0530] "GET /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 200 512 0.040
192.0.2.9 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:51 +0530] "GET /api/orders/99 HTTP/1.1" 404 60 0.012
198.51.100.4 [11/Nov/2026:10:15:58 +0530] "POST /api/orders HTTP/1.1" 500 95 2.410

Splitting on whitespace gives: $1 client IP, $2 $3 the time, $4 the method (with a leading quote), $5 the path, $6 the protocol, $7 the status, $8 bytes, $9 request time.

$ wc -l access.log
8 access.log
$ awk '{print $7}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn
      4 200
      2 500
      1 404
      1 201
$ awk '$7 ~ /^5/ {print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c
      2 198.51.100.4
$ awk '{print $9, $5}' access.log | sort -rn | head -3
2.410 /api/orders
1.920 /api/orders
0.350 /api/orders
$ awk '{s += $9; n++} END {printf "average %.3f s over %d requests\n", s/n, n}' access.log
average 0.610 s over 8 requests
$ awk '{print $5}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -3
      4 /api/orders
      1 /api/products
      1 /api/orders/99

The first line of the status count is the pattern to memorise: ... | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn = "count each distinct value, biggest first". It works for IPs, URLs, status codes, user ids, exception names. The 500s all come from one client IP on /api/orders and are also the slowest requests: a good lead.

Example 2: a Spring Boot application log

Default Spring Boot log lines have the shape TIMESTAMP LEVEL PID --- [thread] logger : message. Questions you ask the app log:

$ grep -c " ERROR " app.log
18
$ grep " ERROR " app.log | awk '{print $1}' | cut -c1-16 | sort | uniq -c
      2 2026-11-11T09:5
     14 2026-11-11T10:0
      2 2026-11-11T10:1
$ grep -E "Exception|Error" app.log | sed -E 's/.*(java\.[a-zA-Z.]+Exception).*/\1/' \
    | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -3
     11 java.net.SocketTimeoutException
      4 java.sql.SQLTransientConnectionException
      1 java.lang.NullPointerException
$ grep -A 15 "OrderService : Order 991 failed" app.log | head -20

The second command cuts the timestamp to ten-minute buckets (first 16 characters) to see when errors spike. The third pulls the exception class name out of each line and ranks them. The numbers shown are sample output. A trailing backslash continues the command on the next line.

sed: edit streams

sed is a stream editor. Its most common use is substitution: s/old/new/ (first match per line) and s/old/new/g (all matches).

$ echo "port=8080" | sed 's/8080/9090/'
port=9090
$ sed -n '100,120p' app.log
$ sed '/^#/d' application.properties
$ sed -i.bak 's/localhost/db.internal/' application.properties
$ sed -n '/10:09:4/,/10:09:5/p' app.log
  • sed -n '100,120p' prints only lines 100-120 (a quick way to view a part of a big file).
  • sed '/^#/d' deletes comment lines.
  • sed -i edits the file in place; -i.bak keeps a backup copy first. Test without -i before you change a file.
  • The last example prints a time window between two patterns.

xargs: turn lines into arguments

Some commands take arguments, not stdin. xargs reads lines from a pipe and puts them on the command line.

$ find . -name "*.tmp" | xargs rm
$ find . -name "*.log" -print0 | xargs -0 grep -l "OutOfMemory"
$ grep -rl "TODO" src/ | xargs wc -l
$ echo "a b c" | xargs -n 1 echo item
item a
item b
item c

If file names can contain spaces, use -print0 with xargs -0 (null separators). find ... -exec cmd {} + does a similar job without xargs.

Putting it together: a 30-second triage

  1. Is it up? systemctl status billing and ss -tulpn | grep 8080.
  2. When did it fail? journalctl -u billing --since "1 hour ago" | grep -n ERROR | head.
  3. What is the top error? The sed + uniq -c pipeline above.
  4. Context: grep -B 3 -A 15 "first error text" app.log.
  5. Who and what is affected? Group the access log by IP, URL and status.

Tip: build pipelines one stage at a time

Run the first command alone, check the output, add | next, check again. When a pipeline gives a strange answer, you can see exactly which stage broke it. Save the good ones as shell aliases or small scripts.

Mistake: wrong column numbers

Log formats put spaces inside quotes ("GET /api HTTP/1.1") or in the date, so $7 in one log is $9 in another. Print the fields first with head -1 access.log | awk '{for (i=1; i<=NF; i++) print i, $i}' and use what you see.

Edge cases

  • Sorting numbers needs sort -n; plain sort is alphabetical, so 10 comes before 9.
  • A locale can change sort order; use LC_ALL=C sort for strict byte order and a faster sort on huge files.
  • sed -i differs on macOS (needs -i ''). On Linux and WSL the examples here work as written.
  • Multi-line stack traces break "one line = one event" analysis. Count only the first line of each event (the one with a timestamp), or use grep -A for context.

Interview: "How would you find the top 5 IPs hitting your server?"

  • awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -5
  • Explain each stage: extract the field, group equal values, count, order, limit.
  • Similar for slow requests: sort on the time column with sort -k9 -nr.

Installing software with apt, Java setup and environment variables

apt update refreshes the package list, apt install installs from it; environment variables such as JAVA_HOME and PATH configure programs and are set in ~/.bashrc, systemd env files or .env files.

Package managers

On Ubuntu you do not download installers from websites. A package manager installs signed software from trusted repositories, resolves dependencies, and updates everything with one command. On Ubuntu and Debian it is apt.

CommandWhat it does
sudo apt updateDownload the latest list of available packages and versions. Installs nothing.
sudo apt upgradeInstall newer versions of packages you already have.
sudo apt install nginxInstall a package and its dependencies.
sudo apt remove nginxRemove the program, keep its config files.
sudo apt purge nginxRemove the program and its config.
sudo apt autoremoveRemove dependencies nobody needs now.
apt search keywordSearch package names and descriptions.
apt show nginxDetails of a package; apt list --installed lists what you have.

The classic mistake: apt update and apt upgrade are different. update only refreshes the catalogue; upgrade changes software. Run update before install on a fresh machine, or you may get Unable to locate package or an old version.

$ sudo apt update
Hit:1 http://archive.ubuntu.com/ubuntu noble InRelease
Reading package lists... Done
$ sudo apt install -y nginx htop curl unzip
$ nginx -v
nginx version: nginx/1.24.0 (Ubuntu)
$ apt search openjdk-21 | head -3

-y answers "yes" automatically; use it in scripts. Red Hat family systems use dnf install/yum install instead; Alpine uses apk add.

Installing Java

$ sudo apt update
$ apt search openjdk | grep -E "^openjdk-[0-9]+-jdk/"
$ sudo apt install -y openjdk-21-jdk
$ java -version
openjdk version "21.0.5" 2024-10-15
OpenJDK Runtime Environment (build 21.0.5+11-Ubuntu-1ubuntu1)
OpenJDK 64-Bit Server VM (build 21.0.5+11-Ubuntu-1ubuntu1, mixed mode, sharing)
$ javac -version
javac 21.0.5
$ readlink -f $(which java)
/usr/lib/jvm/java-21-openjdk-amd64/bin/java

Package names include the Java version (openjdk-17-jdk, openjdk-21-jdk); use apt search to see what your Ubuntu release offers. The version numbers above are sample output. On a server that only runs an app, a JRE (-jre-headless) is enough; on a build machine you need the JDK. Temurin (Eclipse Adoptium) is another common distribution; it provides its own apt repository (see adoptium.net for the current instructions). If several Java versions are installed, choose the default with sudo update-alternatives --config java.

Environment variables

An environment variable is a named value that every program started from a shell inherits. Apps read them for configuration: database URLs, passwords, profiles, ports. This is how the "twelve-factor" approach keeps config out of code. Spring Boot maps them automatically: the variable SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL sets spring.datasource.url, and SERVER_PORT sets server.port.

$ NAME=asha
$ echo $NAME
asha
$ bash -c 'echo "child sees: $NAME"'
child sees:
$ export NAME=asha
$ bash -c 'echo "child sees: $NAME"'
child sees: asha
$ printenv HOME
/home/ubuntu
$ env | grep JAVA
JAVA_HOME=/usr/lib/jvm/java-21-openjdk-amd64
$ unset NAME
$ SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod java -jar app.jar

A plain assignment (NAME=asha) is local to the current shell. export marks it for child processes, such as java. A variable placed before a command (VAR=x cmd) lives only for that command.

PATH and JAVA_HOME

PATH is a colon-separated list of directories. When you type java, the shell searches them in order for an executable with that name. JAVA_HOME is a convention: it points to the JDK folder, and tools such as Maven and Gradle read it.

# append these lines to ~/.bashrc
export JAVA_HOME=/usr/lib/jvm/java-21-openjdk-amd64
export PATH="$JAVA_HOME/bin:$PATH"
export PATH="$HOME/bin:$PATH"
$ source ~/.bashrc
$ echo $JAVA_HOME
/usr/lib/jvm/java-21-openjdk-amd64
$ echo $PATH
/usr/lib/jvm/java-21-openjdk-amd64/bin:/home/ubuntu/bin:/usr/local/sbin:/usr/local/bin:...

Put your folder first (NEW:$PATH) to override a system program; put it last to keep the system one as the default. Never write PATH="..." without including the old $PATH, or you will lose ls, cd and everything else.

.bashrc, .profile and .env files

FileRead whenUse for
~/.bashrcEach new interactive bash terminalAliases, prompt, PATH, JAVA_HOME for interactive use
~/.profileLogin shells (SSH login, console)Variables for your login session; Ubuntu's default .profile also loads .bashrc
/etc/environmentSystem-wide, at loginSimple KEY=value lines for all users
EnvironmentFile= in a unitWhen a service startsVariables for a systemd service (not read from .bashrc!)

A .env file is a plain text file of KEY=value lines, common in projects and Docker. To load it into your shell, source it with auto-export on:

$ cat .env
DB_HOST=localhost
DB_PASSWORD=s3cret
$ set -a
$ source .env
$ set +a
$ echo $DB_HOST
localhost

set -a exports every variable defined while it is on. Treat .env as a secret: chmod 600 .env and add it to .gitignore.

Mistake: expecting a systemd service to see your .bashrc

A service starts without your shell, so JAVA_HOME from .bashrc is not set. Give it variables with Environment= or EnvironmentFile= (systemd article) and use full paths. Same for cron jobs, which run with a tiny PATH.

Mistake: spaces around = and unquoted values

NAME = asha fails (NAME: command not found). Write NAME=asha. Quote values with spaces: GREETING="hello world".

Rule: no secrets in code, Git, or command lines

Passwords passed as command arguments show up in ps aux and shell history. Put them in a file with 600 or 640 permissions, or in a secret manager (Module 5).

Edge cases

  • E: Could not get lock /var/lib/dpkg/lock-frontend — another apt process is running (often automatic updates on a fresh EC2). Wait a minute and retry; do not delete the lock file.
  • E: Unable to locate package x — run apt update, check the spelling and the Ubuntu release.
  • The repository version of a tool can be old. For the latest versions, use the vendor's own repository or a container image, and check the vendor documentation.
  • Environment changes in .bashrc need a new terminal or source ~/.bashrc.

Interview: "What is the difference between apt update and apt upgrade?"

  • update refreshes the package index; upgrade installs newer versions of installed packages.
  • Environment variables: how you pass config to a Spring Boot app, what export does, and why a service does not read .bashrc.

SSH: keys, config, EC2, scp, rsync and hardening

SSH gives you an encrypted shell on a remote machine; use a key pair instead of a password, save hosts in ~/.ssh/config, copy files with scp or rsync, and turn off password and root login on servers.

What SSH is

SSH (Secure Shell) lets you run commands on a remote machine as if you sat in front of it, over an encrypted connection, on TCP port 22. It also carries file copies (scp, rsync) and port forwards. Every Linux server you manage — EC2, a VPS, a Raspberry Pi — is reached this way.

$ ssh ubuntu@203.0.113.10
The authenticity of host '203.0.113.10 (203.0.113.10)' can't be established.
ED25519 key fingerprint is SHA256:abc...xyz.
Are you sure you want to continue connecting (yes/no/[fingerprint])? yes
Warning: Permanently added '203.0.113.10' (ED25519) to the list of known hosts.
ubuntu@203.0.113.10's password:
$ ssh -p 2222 ubuntu@server.example.com
$ ssh ubuntu@203.0.113.10 "uptime"
 10:40:01 up 3 days,  2:11,  1 user,  load average: 0.05, 0.03, 0.01
$ exit

The first connection asks you to trust the server's host key; the client saves it in ~/.ssh/known_hosts and warns loudly if it ever changes (which can mean a rebuilt server or a man-in-the-middle attack). Adding a command after the host runs it and returns.

How key-based authentication works

Passwords can be guessed by bots; a key cannot. You make a key pair: a private key that never leaves your machine, and a public key you copy to the server's ~/.ssh/authorized_keys.

The server only checks the signature with the public key. Even a stolen public key is useless without the private one. Protect the private key (chmod 600), and consider a passphrase that encrypts it on disk.

Create and install a key

$ ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "asha@laptop"
Generating public/private ed25519 key pair.
Enter file in which to save the key (/home/asha/.ssh/id_ed25519):
Enter passphrase (empty for no passphrase):
Your identification has been saved in /home/asha/.ssh/id_ed25519
Your public key has been saved in /home/asha/.ssh/id_ed25519.pub
$ ls ~/.ssh
id_ed25519  id_ed25519.pub  known_hosts
$ ssh-copy-id ubuntu@203.0.113.10
$ ssh ubuntu@203.0.113.10
Welcome to Ubuntu 24.04 LTS

ed25519 is the modern, short and fast key type; id_ed25519 is private, .pub is public (safe to share, for example in GitHub settings). ssh-copy-id appends your public key to the server's authorized_keys using your password once.

Connect to AWS EC2 with a .pem key

When you launch an EC2 instance, AWS lets you create a key pair and downloads the private key as a .pem file (you can download it only once). AWS puts the public half on the instance for the default user. The default user depends on the image: ubuntu for Ubuntu, ec2-user for Amazon Linux.

  1. Move the file somewhere safe: mv ~/Downloads/billing-key.pem ~/.ssh/
  2. Restrict it: chmod 600 ~/.ssh/billing-key.pem
  3. Find the instance's public IP or DNS name in the EC2 console.
  4. Make sure the security group allows inbound TCP 22 from your IP (Firewalls).
  5. Connect: ssh -i ~/.ssh/billing-key.pem ubuntu@203.0.113.10
$ ssh -i ~/.ssh/billing-key.pem ubuntu@ec2-203-0-113-10.ap-south-1.compute.amazonaws.com
ubuntu@ip-172-31-5-20:~$ hostname
ip-172-31-5-20
ubuntu@ip-172-31-5-20:~$ sudo apt update

Errors and meanings: Permission denied (publickey) = wrong user name or wrong key; Connection timed out = the security group, firewall or IP is wrong (the packets never arrive); Connection refused = the machine answered but nothing listens on that port.

~/.ssh/config: host aliases

Host billing
    HostName 203.0.113.10
    User ubuntu
    IdentityFile ~/.ssh/billing-key.pem
    Port 22

Host *
    ServerAliveInterval 60
$ ssh billing
$ scp app.jar billing:/tmp/
$ ssh billing "systemctl is-active billing"
active

The alias works for ssh, scp, rsync and Git over SSH. ServerAliveInterval sends a keep-alive so idle sessions are not dropped. Keep the file at 600.

Copying files: scp and rsync

$ scp target/billing-1.0.1.jar billing:/tmp/
billing-1.0.1.jar            100%   56MB   9.1MB/s   00:06
$ scp -i ~/.ssh/billing-key.pem notes.txt ubuntu@203.0.113.10:/home/ubuntu/
$ scp billing:/var/log/nginx/access.log .
$ scp -r config/ billing:/tmp/config
$ scp -P 2222 file.txt user@host:/tmp/

The form is scp SOURCE DESTINATION; a remote path is host:path. Note: scp uses uppercase -P for the port, ssh uses lowercase -p. rsync is better for repeated copies, because it sends only what changed:

$ rsync -avz --progress dist/ billing:/var/www/app/
$ rsync -avz --delete dist/ billing:/var/www/app/
$ rsync -avzn dist/ billing:/var/www/app/
$ rsync -avz -e "ssh -i ~/.ssh/billing-key.pem" ./backup/ ubuntu@203.0.113.10:/backup/

-a archive (keeps permissions and times), -v verbose, -z compress, --delete removes files on the target that no longer exist in the source, -n is a dry run (shows what would happen). A trailing slash on the source matters: dist/ copies the contents, dist copies the folder itself.

Port forwarding (brief)

An SSH tunnel forwards a local port to a port on the server, through the encrypted connection. It lets you reach a database that listens only on 127.0.0.1 of the server, without opening it to the internet.

$ ssh -L 5433:localhost:5432 billing
$ psql -h localhost -p 5433 -U billing billing

While the SSH session is open, localhost:5433 on your laptop connects to PostgreSQL on the server. Add -N to open the tunnel without a shell.

Hardening basics

Any server with a public IP is probed by bots within minutes. After you can log in with a key, tighten /etc/ssh/sshd_config:

PasswordAuthentication no
PermitRootLogin no
PubkeyAuthentication yes
$ sudo sshd -t
$ sudo systemctl reload ssh

On Ubuntu the service is called ssh; on Red Hat style systems it is sshd. Keep your current session open and test a second login in a new terminal before closing the first one, so a typo cannot lock you out. Also: allow port 22 only from your IP in the firewall; keep the system updated; use fail2ban if you want automatic blocking; never share a private key between people (add each person's public key instead).

Mistake: committing or sharing a private key

A .pem or id_ed25519 in Git, a chat or an email means anyone can log in. If it happens, remove the public key from authorized_keys / replace the EC2 key pair and create a new one. Only the .pub file is safe to share.

Edge cases

  • WARNING: REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED! — the server's host key differs from known_hosts. If you rebuilt the server, remove the old line with ssh-keygen -R 203.0.113.10. If you did not, stop and investigate.
  • A stopped and restarted EC2 instance usually gets a new public IP unless you attach an Elastic IP; update your config.
  • On WSL, a key stored under /mnt/c keeps wide permissions and SSH refuses it. Copy it into ~/.ssh in the Linux filesystem and run chmod 600.
  • Windows 10/11 also ships an OpenSSH client in PowerShell, with the same commands.

Interview: "How does SSH key authentication work, and how do you secure SSH?"

  • Public key on the server, private key on the client; the client proves it holds the private key by signing a challenge; the key itself is never sent.
  • Hardening: keys only, no root login, restrict port 22 to known IPs, updates, optionally fail2ban and a non-default setup behind a bastion host.
  • Say that private keys must be 600 and never committed.

Editing in the terminal: nano and vim survival

On a server you edit files in the terminal: nano is simple and shows its keys at the bottom; vim is on every server and has modes, so learn the few keys to open, edit, save and quit without panic.

A server has no VS Code. When a config file needs a one-line change at 2 a.m., you open it with an editor that is already installed. You do not need to be fast; you need to be safe and sure.

nano: the easy editor

Run nano file (use sudo nano for files you do not own, such as those in /etc). You type directly; commands are shown at the bottom. ^ means Ctrl.

KeysAction
Ctrl+O, then EnterWrite out (save)
Ctrl+XExit (asks to save if there are changes)
Ctrl+WSearch
Ctrl+K / Ctrl+UCut the current line / paste it
Ctrl+_Go to a line number
Alt+UUndo
 
$ sudo nano /etc/billing/billing.env
# change a value, then Ctrl+O, Enter, Ctrl+X
$ sudo systemctl restart billing

vim: why bother?

vim (or its older form vi) is installed on almost every Linux machine, including minimal containers and recovery shells. git commit may open it, and crontab -e may open it. You must be able to leave it. People get "stuck in vim" because it starts in normal mode, where keys are commands, not text.

The modes

  • Normal mode (default): move, delete, copy, search, undo.
  • Insert mode: type text like any editor. Enter with i; leave with Esc.
  • Command mode: start with :, then type a command and press Enter (save, quit, replace).

Lost? Press Esc two or three times. You are then in normal mode.

Survival keys

Keys (Normal mode)Action
i / a / oInsert before cursor / after cursor / on a new line below
EscBack to normal mode
:wWrite (save)
:qQuit (fails if there are unsaved changes)
:wq or ZZSave and quit
:q!Quit and throw away changes
h j k l or arrow keysLeft, down, up, right
gg / GTop / bottom of the file
/text then nSearch; next match (N previous)
ddDelete (cut) the current line
yy then pCopy a line, paste below
u / Ctrl+RUndo / redo
:set numberShow line numbers
:%s/old/new/gReplace every old with new in the file
  1. Open: vim application.properties. You are in normal mode.
  2. Search for the setting: type /server.port and press Enter.
  3. Move to the right place and press i to start editing. Change the value.
  4. Press Esc.
  5. Type :wq and press Enter. Changed your mind? :q!.

Tip: choose your default editor

If a tool opens an editor you do not like, set it: export EDITOR=nano in ~/.bashrc, or run sudo update-alternatives --config editor. Git has git config --global core.editor nano. For bigger work, edit locally and copy the file with scp, or use the VS Code Remote-SSH extension.

Mistake: typing text in normal mode, or editing a root file without sudo

Typing letters in normal mode runs commands and can wreck the file (undo with u). And if you open /etc/... without sudo, vim lets you edit but :w fails with E45: 'readonly' option is set or Can't open file for writing. Quit with :q! and reopen with sudo vim.

Edge cases

  • A leftover file .file.swp means vim was closed badly or another session has the file open. Vim offers to recover; delete the swap file only when you are sure.
  • Pasting into a terminal editor can mangle indentation (auto-indent). In vim use :set paste first; in YAML, spaces matter, so check the result.
  • YAML and Makefiles are indentation sensitive; tabs can break them (YAML forbids tabs).

Interview: "How do you exit vim?"

  • Press Esc, then :wq to save and quit, or :q! to quit without saving.
  • Show you know the three modes; that earns the point more than the keys do.

Bash scripting: variables, conditions, loops, functions and exit codes

A bash script is a text file of commands with a shebang line; add variables, if, loops and functions, check exit codes ($?), and start every serious script with set -euo pipefail.

Your first script

Any series of commands you type twice deserves a script. Create a file, add the shebang (the first line, which tells Linux which interpreter runs the file), make it executable and run it.

#!/usr/bin/env bash
echo "Hello from $(hostname) on $(date +%F)"
$ chmod +x hello.sh
$ ./hello.sh
Hello from web-1 on 2026-11-11
$ bash hello.sh
Hello from web-1 on 2026-11-11

$(command) is command substitution: it runs the command and inserts its output. #!/usr/bin/env bash finds bash on the PATH (portable); #!/bin/bash is also common.

Variables and quoting

#!/usr/bin/env bash
APP_NAME="billing"
VERSION=1.0.1
JAR="${APP_NAME}-${VERSION}.jar"
echo "Deploying $JAR"
echo 'Single quotes: no $expansion here'
FILES=$(ls | wc -l)
echo "There are $FILES entries"
echo "Home is ${HOME}, user is ${USER:-unknown}"
  • No spaces around =. Read a variable with $NAME; use ${NAME} when letters follow it.
  • Double quotes expand variables; single quotes do not. Always quote variables: "$FILE". Unquoted, a value with spaces splits into several words.
  • ${VAR:-default} uses a default when VAR is unset or empty; ${VAR:?message} stops with an error if it is unset.

Arguments

VariableMeaning
$0The script's name
$1, $2, ...First, second ... argument
$#Number of arguments
"$@"All arguments, each kept as a separate word (use the quotes)
$?Exit code of the last command
$$PID of the script itself
 
#!/usr/bin/env bash
if [[ $# -lt 1 ]]; then
    echo "Usage: $0 <name> [more names]" >&2
    exit 1
fi
echo "First: $1, total: $#"
for arg in "$@"; do
    echo " - $arg"
done
$ ./args.sh asha ravi
First: asha, total: 2
 - asha
 - ravi
$ ./args.sh
Usage: ./args.sh <name> [more names]

>&2 sends the message to stderr, which is the right place for errors.

Exit codes

Every command ends with an exit code: 0 means success, any other number means failure. $? holds the last one. Conditions, &&, || and CI pipelines all use it.

$ ls /tmp > /dev/null; echo $?
0
$ ls /nonexistent 2> /dev/null; echo $?
2
$ grep -q ERROR app.log && echo "errors found" || echo "clean"
clean

In your own scripts end with exit 0 for success and exit 1 (or another small number) for failure; CI (Module 4) marks a step as failed when the script exits non-zero.

if, test and [[ ]]

if [[ -f "$JAR" ]]; then
    echo "jar exists"
elif [[ -d "$JAR" ]]; then
    echo "it is a directory"
else
    echo "missing"
fi

if [[ "$ENV" == "prod" && $COUNT -gt 3 ]]; then echo "big prod run"; fi
if grep -q "ERROR" app.log; then echo "has errors"; fi
if ! command -v java > /dev/null; then echo "java not installed"; fi
TestTrue when
-f path / -d path / -e pathRegular file / directory / anything exists
-r / -w / -x pathReadable / writable / executable
-z "$s" / -n "$s"String is empty / not empty
"$a" == "$b", !=String equal / not equal
$a -eq $b, -ne -lt -le -gt -geNumber comparison

[[ ]] is the modern bash form (safer with spaces, supports && and || inside). [ ] and test are the older POSIX forms that work in plain sh too. Needs spaces inside the brackets: [[ -f $x ]], not [[-f $x]]. if can also test any command directly, as with grep -q.

Loops

for f in *.log; do
    echo "$f has $(wc -l < "$f") lines"
done

for i in {1..5}; do echo "attempt $i"; done

count=0
while [[ $count -lt 3 ]]; do
    echo "count=$count"
    count=$((count + 1))
done

while read -r line; do
    echo "host: $line"
done < hosts.txt

$(( ... )) is arithmetic. The while read -r form reads a file line by line. break and continue work as in Java.

Functions

log() {
    echo "[$(date +%T)] $*"
}

check_port() {
    local port="$1"
    if ss -tln | grep -q ":${port} "; then
        return 0
    fi
    return 1
}

log "Checking the app"
if check_port 8080; then log "8080 is listening"; else log "8080 is down"; fi

Functions take arguments as $1, $2; return sets the exit status (0-255), and printed text is the "return value". Use local for variables, or they become global.

Read input and make scripts safe

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

read -r -p "Deploy to which environment (staging/prod)? " ENV
case "$ENV" in
    staging|prod) echo "Deploying to $ENV" ;;
    *) echo "Unknown environment: $ENV" >&2; exit 1 ;;
esac

cleanup() { rm -f /tmp/deploy.lock; }
trap cleanup EXIT
set -e
Exit immediately when a command fails (non-zero exit).
set -u
Treat an unset variable as an error. It protects rm -rf "$DIR/" from running with an empty DIR.
set -o pipefail
A pipeline fails if any stage fails, not only the last. Without it, false | true counts as success.
trap cleanup EXIT
Run a function when the script ends, even on error: delete temp files, release locks.
case
Match a value against patterns; like switch in Java.

Rule: start scripts with a shebang and set -euo pipefail

Without them, bash keeps going after an error. A failed cd followed by rm -rf * deletes the wrong folder. Add them to every script that changes anything.

Mistake: unquoted variables and spaces around =

Wrong: breaks on spaces, wrong assignment

NAME = "my app"
rm -rf $DIR/*
if [ $ENV == prod ]; then echo ok; fi

Right: quoted, no spaces around =

NAME="my app"
rm -rf "${DIR:?}"/*
if [[ "$ENV" == "prod" ]]; then echo ok; fi

${DIR:?} aborts the script when DIR is empty or unset — a cheap safety net.

Edge cases

  • With set -e, a grep that finds nothing (exit 1) stops the script. Guard it: grep -q x file || true or put it in an if.
  • Commands in an if condition or before || are exempt from set -e.
  • A script run as sh script.sh uses a smaller shell on Ubuntu (dash), so [[ ]] and arrays fail. Run it with bash or ./script.sh.
  • A script run through cron or systemd has a small PATH and no ~/.bashrc; use absolute paths.
  • CRLF line endings break the shebang (see Linux basics).

Interview: "What does set -euo pipefail do?"

  • -e stop on error, -u error on unset variables, pipefail fail if any command in a pipe fails.
  • Together they make a script fail fast instead of continuing in a broken state. Mention trap for cleanup and quoting variables.

A real deploy and backup script, with cron

A deploy script copies the new JAR, restarts the service, checks the health endpoint and rolls back if it fails; cron runs scheduled jobs such as a nightly database backup.

Deploy script

This script runs on the server. You upload the JAR to /tmp first, then run it. It keeps the previous JAR, restarts the systemd service from the earlier article, waits for Spring Boot Actuator's health endpoint and rolls back if the new version does not become healthy.

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Usage: ./deploy.sh /tmp/billing-1.0.1.jar
set -euo pipefail

APP_DIR="/opt/billing"
SERVICE="billing"
HEALTH_URL="http://localhost:8080/actuator/health"
NEW_JAR="${1:?Usage: $0 path-to-new-jar}"

log() { echo "[$(date '+%F %T')] $*"; }

[[ -f "$NEW_JAR" ]] || { log "File not found: $NEW_JAR" >&2; exit 1; }

log "Backing up current jar"
if [[ -f "$APP_DIR/billing.jar" ]]; then
    cp "$APP_DIR/billing.jar" "$APP_DIR/billing.jar.previous"
fi

log "Installing new jar"
cp "$NEW_JAR" "$APP_DIR/billing.jar"
chown billing:billing "$APP_DIR/billing.jar"

log "Restarting $SERVICE"
systemctl restart "$SERVICE"

log "Waiting for health check"
for attempt in {1..30}; do
    if curl -fsS "$HEALTH_URL" 2>/dev/null | grep -q '"status":"UP"'; then
        log "Healthy after $attempt checks. Deploy OK."
        exit 0
    fi
    sleep 2
done

log "Health check failed, rolling back" >&2
if [[ -f "$APP_DIR/billing.jar.previous" ]]; then
    cp "$APP_DIR/billing.jar.previous" "$APP_DIR/billing.jar"
    systemctl restart "$SERVICE"
fi
journalctl -u "$SERVICE" -n 30 --no-pager >&2
exit 1

Walk through it:

  1. set -euo pipefail and ${1:?...} make the script stop if a step fails or the JAR argument is missing.
  2. It checks the new file exists before touching anything.
  3. It keeps billing.jar.previous as the rollback copy.
  4. curl -fsS: -f fails on HTTP errors, -s is silent, -S still shows errors. The loop tries for about a minute (30 times, 2 seconds apart), long enough for Spring Boot to start.
  5. On failure it restores the old JAR, restarts, prints the last log lines and exits with 1 so whoever called the script (a person or CI) sees the failure.
$ mvn -q package -DskipTests
$ scp target/billing-1.0.1.jar billing:/tmp/
$ ssh billing "sudo /opt/billing/deploy.sh /tmp/billing-1.0.1.jar"
[2026-11-11 10:55:01] Backing up current jar
[2026-11-11 10:55:01] Installing new jar
[2026-11-11 10:55:01] Restarting billing
[2026-11-11 10:55:02] Waiting for health check
[2026-11-11 10:55:12] Healthy after 5 checks. Deploy OK.

The health endpoint needs spring-boot-starter-actuator in the project; the exact JSON your app returns can differ, so adjust the grep. Module 5 and Module 4 automate this exact flow in a GitHub Actions pipeline.

Backup script

#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail

BACKUP_DIR="/var/backups/billing"
DB_NAME="billing"
KEEP_DAYS=7
STAMP="$(date +%F_%H%M)"
FILE="$BACKUP_DIR/${DB_NAME}_${STAMP}.sql.gz"

mkdir -p "$BACKUP_DIR"
pg_dump "$DB_NAME" | gzip > "$FILE"
echo "Backup written: $FILE ($(du -h "$FILE" | cut -f1))"

find "$BACKUP_DIR" -name "${DB_NAME}_*.sql.gz" -mtime +"$KEEP_DAYS" -delete
echo "Old backups removed"

pg_dump writes the database as SQL; gzip compresses it. The find -mtime +7 -delete line removes files older than seven days so the disk does not fill up. For PostgreSQL the script runs as a user that may connect to the database (for example postgres through its own crontab, or credentials from a ~/.pgpass file). A backup you have never restored is not a backup; test a restore.

cron: scheduled jobs

cron runs commands on a schedule. Each user has a crontab:

$ crontab -e
$ crontab -l
0 2 * * * /opt/scripts/backup-db.sh >> /var/log/backup.log 2>&1
*/15 * * * * curl -fsS http://localhost:8080/actuator/health > /dev/null || echo "down"
ScheduleMeaning
0 2 * * *Every day at 02:00
*/15 * * * *Every 15 minutes
30 9 * * 1-509:30 on weekdays (Mon-Fri)
0 0 1 * *Midnight on the first day of each month
@rebootOnce, at boot (a shortcut instead of five fields)

Mistake: cron jobs that work in the terminal but not in cron

  • cron runs with a minimal PATH and no .bashrc. Use absolute paths (/usr/bin/pg_dump) and set variables in the script.
  • Without redirection you will not see errors. Always append >> file.log 2>&1.
  • In a crontab, a % character means a newline; escape it as \% (for example in date +%F). Putting the logic into a script avoids this.
