A container is an ordinary Linux process that the kernel isolates (namespaces) and limits (cgroups), started from an image that holds the app and all its dependencies — so it runs the same everywhere, starts in seconds and uses far less memory than a virtual machine.

The "works on my machine" problem

You build a Spring Boot app on Windows with Java 21. The server has Java 17, a different PATH, an older PostgreSQL client, and no one remembers which environment variables were set. The app crashes in production but runs fine on your laptop. Every difference between environments — OS, Java version, libraries, config — is a possible bug.

A container fixes this by shipping the environment with the app. The unit you deploy is no longer "a JAR, plus instructions for the server"; it is "an image that already contains the JRE, the JAR and the settings". Anyone who runs that image gets the same result. Other benefits:

  • Consistency. Dev, test and production run the same image.
  • Isolation. Two apps that need Java 17 and Java 21 live side by side on one machine.
  • Fast start. A container starts in about a second plus your app's own start-up time.
  • Easy setup. A new teammate runs docker compose up and has PostgreSQL, Redis and your app — no installers.
  • Easy deployment. Every cloud platform (AWS ECS, Kubernetes, Render, Railway, Cloud Run) runs containers. Image in, running service out.

Containers versus virtual machines

A virtual machine (VM) emulates a whole computer. A hypervisor (VMware, VirtualBox, Hyper-V, KVM) splits the physical machine into several VMs, and each VM runs its own full operating system with its own kernel. Containers skip that: all containers on a host share one kernel, and the kernel keeps them apart.

Virtual machineContainer
ContainsFull guest OS plus appApp plus its libraries only
KernelIts ownShared with the host
SizeGigabytesTens to a few hundred MB
Start timeTens of seconds to minutesUsually about a second
IsolationStrong (separate kernels)Good, but a kernel bug can affect all
Typical useRunning different OSes, strong tenant isolationPackaging and running one service

They are not enemies. In the cloud, containers usually run inside VMs: an EC2 instance is a VM, and you run Docker on it.

What a container really is

A container is not a tiny VM; it is a normal Linux process with two kernel features applied. Namespaces give the process its own view of the system: its own process IDs (it thinks it is PID 1), its own network interfaces and ports, its own hostname, its own mounted filesystem. cgroups (control groups) limit how much CPU, memory and I/O the process may use. Add a layered filesystem built from the image, and you have a container. That is why you can see a container's Java process in ps on the host, and why a container needs a Linux kernel.

Docker's architecture

Client
The docker command you type. It only sends requests.
Daemon
dockerd, the background service that builds images and runs containers.
Image
A read-only template: the files and start-up command for a container.
Container
A running (or stopped) instance of an image.
Registry
A server that stores images: Docker Hub, GitHub Container Registry, AWS ECR.

Docker Desktop on Windows (WSL2 backend)

Containers need a Linux kernel, and Windows does not have one. Docker Desktop solves this by running a small Linux environment through WSL2 (Windows Subsystem for Linux 2) and placing the daemon inside it. You type docker in PowerShell or a WSL terminal and it works as on Linux. Install Docker Desktop, keep "Use the WSL 2 based engine" ticked in Settings → General, and check with docker version and docker run hello-world.

  • Files inside the WSL2 filesystem (\\wsl$\Ubuntu\home\...) are much faster for bind mounts than files on C:\. Keep big projects there if builds feel slow.
  • WSL2 can take a lot of RAM. Limit it in %UserProfile%\.wslconfig ( [wsl2] section, memory=6GB).
  • Docker Desktop has licensing terms for larger companies; on a Linux server you install the free Docker Engine only. Podman is a compatible alternative.

Edge cases: containers are not a security boundary like a VM

  • A Windows or macOS "container" is a Linux container running in a hidden Linux VM.
  • Because the kernel is shared, an image built for ARM (Apple M-series, AWS Graviton) will not run on an x86 server unless it is built for both. Check the architecture if you see exec format error.
  • Running as root inside a container is risky; see the Dockerfile article.

Interview: "What is the difference between a container and a VM?"

  • A VM virtualises hardware and runs a full guest OS with its own kernel; a container virtualises the OS: processes share the host kernel and are isolated with namespaces and limited with cgroups.
  • Containers are lighter, start faster and pack denser; VMs isolate more strongly. In practice containers run on VMs.

Images, layers, tags and the container lifecycle

An image is a stack of read-only layers plus start-up metadata; a container is that stack with one thin writable layer on top. Use specific tags (never latest in production), and pick a small, official base image such as eclipse-temurin for Java.

Image, container, registry

Think of an image as a class and a container as an object created from it. You can start ten containers from one image. The image never changes; each container gets its own writable layer, which is thrown away when the container is removed. That is why data written inside a container disappears — unless you use a volume.

A registry stores images so other machines can download them. Docker Hub (hub.docker.com) is the default public registry. An image name has the form registry/namespace/repository:tag, and most parts have defaults:

Written asMeans
postgresdocker.io/library/postgres:latest (an official image)
postgres:16The official PostgreSQL image, tag 16
asha/shop-api:1.2.0User asha's repository on Docker Hub
ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:1.2.0GitHub Container Registry

Layers and the build cache

Each instruction in a Dockerfile that changes the filesystem (RUN, COPY, ADD) creates a layer: a set of file changes. An image is a stack of layers. Layers are identified by a hash of their content, so they are shared: ten images built on the same eclipse-temurin:21-jre store that base only once on disk and download it once.

When you build, Docker reuses a cached layer if the instruction and its inputs are identical. The moment one layer changes, every layer after it is rebuilt. This is the most important performance rule in Docker: put things that change rarely (dependencies) before things that change often (your source code). The Dockerfile article shows how.

Tags and digests

A tag is a movable label on an image: postgres:16, eclipse-temurin:21-jre. If you do not write a tag, Docker uses latest, which is just a name, not "the newest safe version". Tags can be moved: postgres:16 points to 16.x today and a newer 16.y next month. A digest is the immutable SHA-256 of the image content: postgres@sha256:…. It always means exactly the same bytes.

ReferenceStabilityUse for
app:latestChanges without warningQuick local experiments only
app:1.2.0Stable if you never overwrite itReleases
app:3f9c2ab (git SHA)Unique per commitCI builds and deploys
app@sha256:…ImmutableStrict reproducibility

Official images and choosing a Java base image

Official images (no username, such as postgres, redis, nginx, eclipse-temurin) are curated, documented and regularly updated. Prefer them over random user images. For Java use eclipse-temurin, the Eclipse Adoptium build of OpenJDK. Its tags are named by version and type:

  • eclipse-temurin:21-jdk — full JDK, for compiling. Large.
  • eclipse-temurin:21-jre — runtime only. Right for running a built JAR. The same pattern exists for 25 (the newest LTS) and 17.
  • Variants such as 21-jre-alpine (Alpine Linux base) or 21-jre-jammy (a named Ubuntu release). Check the image's page on Docker Hub for the exact tag list.
BaseProsCons
Debian/Ubuntu (default tags)Uses glibc, most compatible, easy debugging toolsBigger
AlpineVery small baseUses musl libc; some native libraries (or JNI code) misbehave; fewer tools
Distroless (Google)No shell or package manager; small attack surfaceHard to debug with exec; needs care

A sensible default for a Spring Boot service: eclipse-temurin:21-jre. Choose Alpine only when image size really matters and you have tested the app on it.

The container lifecycle

A container lives only as long as its main process (PID 1). When your Java process exits, the container exits. A stopped container still exists, with its writable layer and logs, until you remove it with docker rm.

Rule: pin a version; never rely on latest in production

With FROM eclipse-temurin:latest your next build may silently switch to a new Java major version. Use a version tag in Dockerfiles and compose files, and tag your own images with a version or git SHA.

Mistake: storing data in the container's writable layer

Uploads, database files or logs written inside a container vanish on docker rm and on redeploys. Use volumes, an external database, or object storage.

Interview: "Image versus container? What are layers?"

  • Image: read-only template; container: a running instance with a writable layer.
  • Layers: each filesystem-changing instruction creates one; they are cached and shared. Order instructions so rarely changing ones come first.

Docker commands: run, ps, logs, exec, stop, rm and friends

Learn docker run and its flags, then ps, logs -f, exec -it, stop, rm, images and system prune; these ten commands cover nearly all daily Docker work.

Your first container

$ docker run -d --name pg -p 5432:5432 \
    -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=devpass -e POSTGRES_DB=shop \
    postgres:16
Unable to find image 'postgres:16' locally
16: Pulling from library/postgres
...
d3c1f6a0b2e4f8a7c5b9e1d2a3f4b5c6d7e8f9a0b1c2d3e4f5a6b7c8d9e0f1a2
$ docker ps
CONTAINER ID   IMAGE         COMMAND                  STATUS         PORTS                    NAMES
d3c1f6a0b2e4   postgres:16   "docker-entrypoint.s…"   Up 5 seconds   0.0.0.0:5432->5432/tcp   pg

Docker looked for postgres:16 locally, did not find it, pulled it from Docker Hub, created a container named pg, started it in the background and mapped port 5432 of your machine to port 5432 inside. Your Spring Boot app on the host can now use jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/shop.

docker run flags you must know

FlagMeaningExample
-dDetached: run in the background and print the container iddocker run -d nginx
-p host:containerPublish a container port on a host port-p 8081:8080
--nameGive the container a fixed name instead of a random one--name shop-api
-e KEY=valueSet an environment variable-e SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod
--env-fileRead many variables from a file--env-file .env
-vMount a volume or a host folder-v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
--rmDelete the container automatically when it exitsdocker run --rm -it ubuntu bash
--networkAttach to a named network--network shop-net
--restartRestart policy: no, on-failure, always, unless-stopped--restart unless-stopped
-itInteractive terminal (-i keeps stdin open, -t allocates a TTY)docker run -it alpine sh
-m, --cpusMemory and CPU limits (cgroups)-m 512m --cpus 1

Everything after the image name is the command that overrides the image's default command: docker run --rm ubuntu ls /.

Port mapping reads left to right as host:container. In -p 8081:8080 a browser uses localhost:8081 and the app inside still listens on 8080. Bind only to your own machine with -p 127.0.0.1:5432:5432; plain -p 5432:5432 listens on all interfaces, which is risky on a public server.

Inspecting and debugging

$ docker ps -a                       # all containers, also stopped ones
$ docker logs pg                     # print the logs
$ docker logs -f --tail 100 pg       # follow, starting from the last 100 lines
$ docker exec -it pg psql -U postgres -d shop     # open psql inside the container
$ docker exec -it pg sh              # a shell inside it (bash if the image has it)
$ docker inspect pg                  # full JSON: IP address, mounts, env, state
$ docker inspect -f '{{.State.ExitCode}}' pg      # one field
$ docker stats                       # live CPU and memory per container
$ docker top pg                      # processes inside
$ docker cp pg:/etc/hostname ./      # copy a file out of a container

exec starts a new process inside a running container; it is how you "log in". docker run -it starts a new container. Do not confuse them.

Stopping and removing

CommandWhat it does
docker stop pgSends SIGTERM so the app can shut down gracefully; after 10 seconds (default) it sends SIGKILL
docker kill pgSends SIGKILL at once; no graceful shutdown
docker start pg / restart pgStart a stopped container / stop and start
docker rm pgDelete a stopped container (-f to force a running one)
docker rm -f $(docker ps -aq)Remove every container (bash); be careful

A Spring Boot app handles SIGTERM by finishing in-flight requests if you enable server.shutdown=graceful. This works only if the Java process is PID 1 or receives the signal (see ENTRYPOINT exec form in the next article).

Images and cleanup

$ docker images                      # list local images
$ docker pull eclipse-temurin:21-jre # download without running
$ docker rmi postgres:16             # delete an image (no container may use it)
$ docker tag shop-api:1.0 asha/shop-api:1.0
$ docker push asha/shop-api:1.0
$ docker system df                   # how much space Docker uses
$ docker system prune                # remove stopped containers, dangling images, unused networks
$ docker system prune -a --volumes   # also unused images AND unused volumes (dangerous)

Disks fill up with old images and containers quickly, especially on CI servers and laptops. docker system prune is safe for most work; the --volumes flag deletes volumes that no container uses, which can include your database data.

Restart policies

By default a container that crashes stays down. --restart on-failure restarts it when it exits with an error; unless-stopped restarts it also after a server reboot unless you stopped it by hand; always is similar but also restarts a container you stopped, after the daemon restarts. For a server, unless-stopped is the usual choice.

Tip: name things and clean as you go

Use --name so commands are readable, and --rm for throwaway containers (docker run --rm -it eclipse-temurin:21-jdk jshell gives you a JShell with no installation).

Mistake: "port is already allocated" and "name already in use"

  • Bind for 0.0.0.0:5432 failed: port is already allocated — a local PostgreSQL or another container already uses that host port. Use -p 5433:5432 or stop the other one.
  • The container name "/pg" is already in use — remove the old container (docker rm pg) or choose another name.

Edge cases

  • -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=... on the command line is stored in your shell history and visible in docker inspect; use --env-file for anything real.
  • The official PostgreSQL image only reads POSTGRES_PASSWORD and POSTGRES_DB when the data directory is empty. Changing them later has no effect on an existing volume.
  • On Windows PowerShell, line continuation is the backtick (`), not \; Git Bash and WSL use \.

Interview: "docker stop versus docker kill? exec versus run?"

  • stop sends SIGTERM, waits (10 s default), then SIGKILL; kill sends SIGKILL at once.
  • run creates and starts a new container from an image; exec runs an extra process inside an existing running container.

Dockerfile instructions, ENTRYPOINT vs CMD and best practices

A Dockerfile is a recipe of instructions that builds an image layer by layer; order the instructions so dependencies are cached, run as a non-root user, keep images small, and never put secrets inside.

What a Dockerfile is

A Dockerfile is a text file (named exactly Dockerfile) with one instruction per step. docker build -t shop-api:1.0 . reads it, runs the steps in order, and stores the result as an image. The final . is the build context: the folder whose files COPY may use. Docker sends the whole context to the daemon, so keep it small with a .dockerignore.

The instructions

InstructionPurposeExample
FROMBase image; starts a build stageFROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre
WORKDIRSet (and create) the working directory for later stepsWORKDIR /app
COPYCopy files from the build context (or another stage) into the imageCOPY target/app.jar app.jar
ADDLike COPY, plus unpacks local tar files and fetches URLs; prefer COPYADD site.tar.gz /var/www
RUNRun a command at build time; creates a layerRUN ./mvnw -B package
ENVSet an environment variable, kept in the image and at runtimeENV TZ=Asia/Kolkata
ARGBuild-time variable, passed with --build-arg; not available at runtimeARG APP_VERSION=dev
EXPOSEDocuments the port the app listens on; does not publish itEXPOSE 8080
USERRun later steps and the container as this userUSER spring
ENTRYPOINTThe executable that always runsENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]
CMDDefault arguments (or default command); easy to overrideCMD ["--spring.profiles.active=prod"]
HEALTHCHECKCommand Docker runs to decide if the container is healthyHEALTHCHECK CMD curl -f http://localhost:8080/actuator/health

Lines starting with # are comments. RUN, ENTRYPOINT and CMD each have two forms:

  • Exec form ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"] (JSON array). Runs the program directly as PID 1, so it receives SIGTERM from docker stop. Use this for ENTRYPOINT and CMD.
  • Shell form java -jar app.jar. Docker wraps it in /bin/sh -c, the shell becomes PID 1, and signals may never reach Java, so shutdown is not graceful.

ENTRYPOINT versus CMD

ENTRYPOINT is "what this container is" and CMD is "default arguments". When both exist, Docker runs ENTRYPOINT followed by CMD. docker run image extra args replaces CMD but not ENTRYPOINT (override it with --entrypoint).

Dockerfiledocker run imageRuns
CMD ["java","-jar","app.jar"](nothing)java -jar app.jar
CMD ["java","-jar","app.jar"]shsh (CMD replaced; the app is gone)
ENTRYPOINT ["java","-jar","app.jar"](nothing)java -jar app.jar
ENTRYPOINT ["java","-jar","app.jar"]--server.port=9000java -jar app.jar --server.port=9000

For a Spring Boot service use ENTRYPOINT in exec form; that way extra arguments become Spring command-line arguments, and nobody starts a shell by accident.

.dockerignore

A .dockerignore file lists paths excluded from the build context. It makes builds faster, keeps secrets out, and stops stale files from invalidating the cache.

.git
.idea
.vscode
target
node_modules
*.log
.env
.env.*
Dockerfile
compose.yaml
README.md

Best practices

  1. Order layers for the cache. Copy pom.xml and download dependencies before copying src. Dependencies change rarely, source changes on every commit; so the slow download layer stays cached.
  2. Use multi-stage builds. Compile with the JDK and Maven in one stage; ship only the JRE and the JAR in the final stage (next article).
  3. Run as a non-root user. If an attacker breaks into the app, they are not root inside the container.
  4. One main process per container. The app in one, the database in another, a cache in a third. Compose joins them.
  5. Keep images small. A JRE base, no build tools, no caches; fewer packages mean less to patch and faster pulls.
  6. Pin versions. eclipse-temurin:21-jre, not latest; pin Maven and Node versions too.
  7. No secrets in the image. Every layer is readable by anyone who can pull the image, even if a later step deletes the file.
  8. Combine related RUN steps with && and clean up in the same step (for example apt-get install … && rm -rf /var/lib/apt/lists/*). Deleting in a later layer does not shrink the image.
  9. Use exec form for ENTRYPOINT/CMD, and add a healthcheck where the platform uses it.

Wrong: source copied first, cache never helps

FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-21
WORKDIR /build
COPY . .
RUN mvn -B package
ENTRYPOINT java -jar target/app.jar

Right: dependencies cached, exec form

FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-21 AS build
WORKDIR /build
COPY pom.xml .
RUN mvn -B dependency:go-offline
COPY src src
RUN mvn -B package -DskipTests
# ...runtime stage copies only the JAR

In the wrong version any edit to any file invalidates the COPY . . layer, so Maven downloads all dependencies again on every build, and the final image carries Maven, the JDK and the source.

Rule: ARG and ENV values are not secrets

Build args and environment variables baked into an image can be read with docker history and docker inspect. Pass real secrets at runtime (see Environment variables and secrets) or use BuildKit secret mounts for build-time needs.

Edge cases

  • EXPOSE changes nothing at runtime. Without -p (or compose ports) the host cannot reach the port.
  • CMD or ENTRYPOINT given twice: only the last counts.
  • ARG declared before FROM is usable only in FROM lines; redeclare it inside the stage to use it there.
  • ADD auto-extracts local archives and downloads URLs, which surprises people; it is the reason to prefer COPY.

Interview: "How do you make Docker builds fast and images small?"

  • Order instructions by change frequency so the build cache works; copy the dependency manifest first.
  • Multi-stage build, JRE base image, .dockerignore, combine and clean RUN steps.
  • Non-root user, pinned tags, no secrets.

A complete multi-stage Dockerfile for Spring Boot, line by line

Stage one uses a Maven + JDK image to download dependencies and build the JAR; stage two starts from a small JRE image, copies only the JAR, switches to a non-root user and starts the app with an exec-form ENTRYPOINT — typically a few hundred MB smaller than a single-stage image.

Why two stages

Building needs Maven, the JDK, your source and about 200 MB of downloaded dependencies. Running needs only a JRE and one JAR. A multi-stage build uses several FROM lines in one Dockerfile; each starts a fresh stage, and COPY --from= takes chosen files from an earlier stage. Everything else in the earlier stage is dropped from the final image.

The Dockerfile

Project layout: pom.xml, src/, and optionally the Maven wrapper (mvnw, .mvn/). This version uses the Maven image, so it does not need the wrapper.

# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1

# ---------- Stage 1: build ----------
FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-21 AS build
WORKDIR /build

# Copy only the dependency manifest first, so this layer is cached
COPY pom.xml .
RUN mvn -B -q dependency:go-offline

# Now copy the source and build the JAR
COPY src ./src
RUN mvn -B -q package -DskipTests

# ---------- Stage 2: runtime ----------
FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre
WORKDIR /app

# Create a non-root user and group
RUN groupadd --system spring && useradd --system --gid spring --no-create-home spring

# Take only the built JAR from stage 1
COPY --from=build /build/target/*.jar app.jar

USER spring
EXPOSE 8080

# Tune the JVM for containers (see the JVM article)
ENV JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS="-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0"

HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --start-period=40s --retries=3 \
  CMD curl -fs http://localhost:8080/actuator/health || exit 1

ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]

Every line explained

# syntax=docker/dockerfile:1
Optional first line that selects the current stable Dockerfile syntax for BuildKit (the default builder). It enables newer features such as RUN --mount.
FROM maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-21 AS build
The base for stage 1: Maven 3.9 on Temurin JDK 21. AS build names the stage so we can copy from it later.
WORKDIR /build
Creates /build and makes it the current folder. All following relative paths start here.
COPY pom.xml .
Copies just the POM. If the POM has not changed, this layer and the next stay cached.
RUN mvn -B -q dependency:go-offline
Downloads dependencies and plugins into the image's ~/.m2 as its own layer. -B is batch mode (no colours or progress bars) and -q is quiet. Some plugins are only resolved when they run, so a rare "missing artifact" can still appear later; it only costs speed, not correctness.
COPY src ./src
Brings in your code. From here down, layers are rebuilt on every code change.
RUN mvn -B -q package -DskipTests
Compiles and builds the executable Spring Boot JAR into target/. Tests are skipped here because CI runs them in an earlier step; running them inside the image build would need a database.
FROM eclipse-temurin:21-jre
Starts stage 2 from a JRE-only image. Nothing from stage 1 comes along automatically.
RUN groupadd … && useradd …
Creates a system user spring. These are the Debian/Ubuntu commands; on Alpine you would use addgroup/adduser.
COPY --from=build /build/target/*.jar app.jar
Copies the JAR from the build stage and renames it app.jar. Keep exactly one JAR in target/ (the glob fails or misbehaves if a -plain.jar is also produced, e.g. by the Gradle jar task; Maven's Boot plugin produces one executable JAR).
USER spring
From now on, and at runtime, the process runs as spring, not root.
EXPOSE 8080
Documentation: the app listens on 8080.
ENV JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS=…
The JVM reads this variable at start-up and applies the flags.
HEALTHCHECK …
Docker runs the command inside the container every 30 s. start-period gives Spring time to boot. It needs curl and the Actuator health endpoint; the Temurin Ubuntu-based images include curl, but Alpine and distroless images do not.
ENTRYPOINT ["java", "-jar", "app.jar"]
Exec form: Java is PID 1 and gets SIGTERM on docker stop.

Build and run

$ docker build -t shop-api:1.0 .
[+] Building 94.2s (13/13) FINISHED
$ docker images shop-api
REPOSITORY   TAG   IMAGE ID       CREATED          SIZE
shop-api     1.0   5a1b2c3d4e5f   10 seconds ago   310MB
$ docker run -d --name shop-api -p 8080:8080 \
    -e SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod shop-api:1.0
$ curl -s localhost:8080/actuator/health
{"status":"UP"}
$ docker build -t shop-api:1.0 .       # second build after a code change
[+] Building 12.8s (13/13) FINISHED     # dependency layer came from the cache

The sizes and times above are examples; yours will differ. The point to notice is the second build: the dependency layer is cached, so only the compile and copy steps run.

Optional: layered JARs for faster pulls

A Spring Boot JAR is mostly third-party libraries that rarely change, plus a small part that is your code. Spring Boot can split the JAR into layers (dependencies, application classes) that Docker caches separately, so a code change re-pushes only a few KB instead of the whole fat JAR. You extract the layers in the build stage with java -Djarmode=tools -jar app.jar extract --layers (Spring Boot 3.3 and later; older versions use -Djarmode=layertools with extract) and copy each folder with its own COPY. This is an optimisation; start with the simple version above and see the Spring Boot reference ("Efficient container images") before you add it.

Java versions

Replace 21 with 17 or 25 to match your project's java.version. The build and runtime stages must use the same major version (or the runtime must be newer), otherwise you get UnsupportedClassVersionError.

Mistake: building the JAR on the host and forgetting to rebuild

A Dockerfile that does COPY target/app.jar uses whatever is in target/ on your machine, possibly an old build, and breaks in CI where target/ does not exist. The multi-stage version builds inside Docker, so the result does not depend on your laptop.

Errors you will see

  • Error: Unable to access jarfile app.jar — the JAR was not copied to the path the ENTRYPOINT expects, or the glob matched nothing.
  • no main manifest attribute — a plain (non-Boot) JAR was copied; build with the spring-boot-maven-plugin repackage goal.
  • UnsupportedClassVersionError — compiled with a newer JDK than the runtime image.
  • Container becomes "unhealthy" — curl missing, Actuator not on the classpath, or the app needs more than start-period to start.

Interview: "Walk me through a Dockerfile for a Spring Boot app."

  • Two stages: Maven/JDK builds the JAR (POM and dependencies first for caching); a JRE image runs it.
  • COPY --from=build, non-root USER, exec-form ENTRYPOINT, EXPOSE 8080, healthcheck; secrets come from the environment at runtime.

The JVM in containers, Buildpacks and Jib

Modern JVMs read the container's memory and CPU limits; set the heap as a percentage with -XX:MaxRAMPercentage, always set a container memory limit, and consider Spring Boot's Buildpacks or Jib when you want an image without writing a Dockerfile.

Container-aware JVM

A container limit (for example -m 512m) is enforced by the kernel through cgroups. Old JVMs (Java 8 before update 191) looked at the host memory and sized the heap for 16 GB while the container had 512 MB, and the kernel killed the container. Modern JVMs (Java 10+, and 8u191+) are container-aware by default (-XX:+UseContainerSupport is on): they read the cgroup limits for memory and CPU count.

By default the maximum heap is about 25% of the container memory — safe but often too low. Control it with a percentage so the setting follows the limit:

FlagMeaning
-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0Max heap as a percentage of container memory (default 25)
-XX:InitialRAMPercentage=50.0Initial heap size as a percentage
-Xmx512mFixed maximum heap; does not follow the container limit
-XX:ActiveProcessorCount=2Override the CPU count the JVM believes it has
-XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryErrorExit on OOM so the platform restarts the container

Why not 100%? The JVM also needs memory outside the heap: metaspace, thread stacks, the code cache, direct buffers and garbage collector structures. A container with a 1 GB limit and MaxRAMPercentage=75 gets a heap up to 768 MB, leaving about 256 MB for everything else. For very small containers (under 512 MB) use a lower percentage, around 50–60.

$ docker run -d --name shop-api -m 1g --cpus 2 -p 8080:8080 \
    -e JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS="-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0 -XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError" \
    shop-api:1.0
$ docker logs shop-api | head -3
Picked up JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS: -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0 -XX:+ExitOnOutOfMemoryError
$ docker stats --no-stream shop-api
CONTAINER ID   NAME       CPU %   MEM USAGE / LIMIT   MEM %
8c1d2e3f4a5b   shop-api   0.6%    412MiB / 1GiB       40.23%

JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS is read by every JVM start, so it also affects tools like jcmd that run in the same container (they print the "Picked up" line). That is harmless.

Other container tips for the JVM

  • Always set a memory limit (-m, or deploy.resources.limits / mem_limit in compose, or the platform setting). Without one, the JVM sees the whole host.
  • CPU limits change the GC and thread pools. With under 2 CPUs the JVM may choose the Serial GC. If start-up is slow, give it more than one CPU while it boots.
  • Start-up time. A Spring Boot app often needs 5–20 s to start; set the healthcheck start-period and the platform's readiness delay accordingly.
  • Heap dumps and logs. Write logs to stdout (the default), not to files inside the container; Docker and platforms collect stdout.
  • See Core Java · Module 7 for heap, GC and memory in detail.

Mistake: container killed with exit code 137, no Java stack trace

Exit code 137 = 128 + 9 (SIGKILL). When the whole container exceeds its memory limit the kernel's OOM killer kills it silently; Java never prints an OutOfMemoryError. Check docker inspect -f '{{.State.OOMKilled}}' name. Fix: lower MaxRAMPercentage to leave room for non-heap memory, raise the limit, or find the leak.

Alternative 1: Spring Boot Buildpacks (no Dockerfile)

Cloud Native Buildpacks inspect your project and build an optimised, layered image for you. Spring Boot wraps them in the Maven and Gradle plugins:

$ ./mvnw -DskipTests spring-boot:build-image \
    -Dspring-boot.build-image.imageName=shop-api:1.0
...
Successfully built image 'docker.io/library/shop-api:1.0'
$ docker run --rm -p 8080:8080 shop-api:1.0

It needs a running Docker daemon, downloads builder images on the first run (slow), and configures the JVM memory settings automatically at start-up. Good for quick builds and teams that want standard images; less control than a Dockerfile.

Alternative 2: Jib

Jib (a Google plugin for Maven and Gradle) builds a layered image directly from your compiled classes and dependencies without a Docker daemon and without a Dockerfile. It can push straight to a registry, which suits CI servers where Docker is not available.

<plugin>
    <groupId>com.google.cloud.tools</groupId>
    <artifactId>jib-maven-plugin</artifactId>
    <!-- use the latest version from the plugin's README -->
    <configuration>
        <to><image>ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:1.0</image></to>
    </configuration>
</plugin>
$ ./mvnw compile jib:dockerBuild    # build into the local Docker daemon
$ ./mvnw compile jib:build          # build and push straight to the registry
DockerfileBuildpacksJib
ControlFullLimitedMedium
Needs Docker daemonYesYesNo (for jib:build)
Learning costLearn DockerfileOne commandPlugin config
Best forAny app, interviews, most teamsSpring Boot with defaultsCI without Docker

Convention: know the Dockerfile first

Interviews and most existing projects use Dockerfiles. Learn that well; reach for Buildpacks or Jib when a project already uses them or when you need to avoid a Docker daemon.

Interview: "Your Java container keeps getting killed. Why?"

  • Exit 137 and OOMKilled=true: total memory (heap plus non-heap) exceeded the limit.
  • Set an explicit limit, use MaxRAMPercentage (60–75), leave room for metaspace and threads, and look for leaks.

Volumes, bind mounts and keeping data safe

A container's own filesystem disappears with the container; use a named volume for data that must survive (database files), a bind mount to share a host folder (source code, config), and remember that docker compose down -v deletes volumes.

The problem: containers are disposable

Remove a PostgreSQL container and all its tables are gone, because they lived in the container's writable layer. Containers should be disposable — you replace them on every release — so data that matters must live outside the container's own filesystem. Docker offers three ways to mount storage:

Named volumeBind mounttmpfs
Where the data livesDocker-managed area (/var/lib/docker/volumes)Any host path you chooseHost memory
Syntax-v pgdata:/path-v /host/dir:/path or -v ./dir:/path--tmpfs /path
Survives container removalYesYes (it is your folder)No
Best forDatabases, uploads, anything the app ownsDev: source code, config files, reading logsTemporary or sensitive scratch data
PortabilityHighDepends on host paths and permissionsHigh

Persisting PostgreSQL data

$ docker volume create pgdata
$ docker run -d --name pg -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=devpass \
    -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data postgres:16
$ docker exec -it pg psql -U postgres -c "CREATE TABLE t(id int); INSERT INTO t VALUES (1);"
$ docker rm -f pg                        # container gone ...
$ docker run -d --name pg2 -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=devpass \
    -v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data postgres:16
$ docker exec -it pg2 psql -U postgres -c "SELECT * FROM t;"
 id
----
  1
(1 row)

The data survived because the volume pgdata was mounted at the directory where PostgreSQL stores its files. If you mount a volume with a name that does not exist, Docker creates it. If you use a volume for a database, use the data directory documented on the image's Docker Hub page (/var/lib/postgresql/data for the official PostgreSQL image; MySQL uses /var/lib/mysql).

PostgreSQL 18 image path change

Check the image page for newer major versions: some recent PostgreSQL images changed the recommended mount point (for example to /var/lib/postgresql for 18+). Always read the "PGDATA" notes for the exact tag you use, otherwise your data may not land in the volume. For postgres:16 the path above is correct.

Bind mounts for development

$ docker run -d --name shop-api -p 8080:8080 \
    -v "$(pwd)/config/application-prod.yml:/app/config/application-prod.yml:ro" \
    -v "$(pwd)/uploads:/app/uploads" \
    shop-api:1.0

The suffix :ro makes the mount read-only inside the container. Spring Boot automatically reads ./config/application*.yml relative to its working directory, so a mounted /app/config folder overrides settings without rebuilding the image. In compose files use relative paths such as ./config:/app/config.

Managing volumes

$ docker volume ls
$ docker volume inspect pgdata        # shows the Mountpoint on the host
$ docker volume rm pgdata             # fails if a container still uses it
$ docker volume prune                 # remove volumes no container uses (data loss!)

Backing up a volume (brief)

For databases, prefer a logical backup that the database understands. For any volume, you can archive it with a throw-away container that mounts both the volume and a host folder:

$ docker exec pg pg_dump -U postgres shop > shop-backup.sql          # logical dump
$ docker run --rm -v pgdata:/data -v "$(pwd)":/backup alpine \
    tar czf /backup/pgdata.tar.gz -C /data .                         # raw copy (stop the DB first)
$ docker exec -i pg psql -U postgres shop < shop-backup.sql          # restore a dump

A raw copy of a running database's files may be inconsistent; stop the database first or use pg_dump. Real production databases normally run outside Docker, as managed services (Amazon RDS) with automated backups.

Mistake: docker compose down -v wipes your database

docker compose down removes containers and networks but keeps named volumes. docker compose down -v also deletes the volumes. People run it to "clean up" and lose the local database; on a server it would be a disaster. Likewise docker system prune --volumes and docker volume prune. Use -v deliberately, only when you want a fresh database.

Edge cases

  • An anonymous volume (-v /var/lib/data with no name) is created on each run with a random name, and silently piles up. Always name volumes.
  • A bind mount hides whatever the image had at that path. Mounting an empty host folder over /app hides your JAR.
  • Permission denied on a mounted folder: the container user's UID (a non-root spring user) must have rights to the host folder. On Linux fix with chown or --user; Docker Desktop on Windows/Mac usually hides this problem, which then appears on the Linux server.
  • The first start of the PostgreSQL image runs *.sql and *.sh files from /docker-entrypoint-initdb.d, but only when the data directory is empty.

Interview: "How do you persist database data in Docker?"

  • Mount a named volume at the database's data directory; the volume outlives the container.
  • Volume versus bind mount: Docker-managed and portable versus a host path for dev.
  • Know down -v deletes volumes; back up with pg_dump.

Docker networks and the localhost trap

Containers on the same user-defined network reach each other by name (Docker's built-in DNS); inside a container localhost means that container itself, so a Spring Boot container must connect to the database at postgres:5432, not localhost:5432.

Network drivers in practice

Docker gives every container a virtual network interface. The default and most common driver is bridge: a private virtual switch inside the host, with its own subnet (for example 172.17.0.0/16). Containers on it get private IPs and reach the internet through NAT (see Networking).

NetworkBehaviour
Default bridgeEvery container joins it if you say nothing. Containers can reach each other by IP only, no names.
User-defined bridgeYou create it (docker network create shop-net). Adds DNS by container name and better isolation. Compose creates one for you.
hostContainer shares the host's network stack; no port mapping. Linux only in practice.
noneNo networking.

Name resolution on a user-defined network

$ docker network create shop-net
$ docker run -d --name postgres --network shop-net \
    -e POSTGRES_PASSWORD=devpass -e POSTGRES_DB=shop postgres:16
$ docker run -d --name shop-api --network shop-net -p 8080:8080 \
    -e SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL=jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/shop \
    -e SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME=postgres \
    -e SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD=devpass \
    shop-api:1.0
$ docker exec shop-api getent hosts postgres      # (if the image has getent)
172.18.0.2      postgres

Docker runs an embedded DNS server (at 127.0.0.11 inside the container) that resolves a container's name, and in compose also its service name, to its IP on that network. The IP changes when the container is recreated, but the name does not, so always use names.

The localhost trap

Each container has its own network namespace, so localhost (127.0.0.1) inside the Spring Boot container is the Spring Boot container. If your datasource URL is jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/shop, the app looks for PostgreSQL inside its own container, finds nothing, and fails:

org.postgresql.util.PSQLException: Connection to localhost:5432 refused.
Check that the hostname and port are correct and that the postmaster is accepting TCP/IP connections.
Caused by: java.net.ConnectException: Connection refused

Wrong: localhost points at the app's own container

SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/shop

Right: use the service name on the shared network

SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/shop

It works on your laptop when you run the app from the IDE because there localhost is the host, where the database port is published. The same value fails inside a container. The fix is not "publish more ports" but "use the service name". This is the number one Docker bug in Spring Boot projects: whenever you see connection refused between containers, check the host name first.

Publishing ports versus internal communication

  • -p 8080:8080 (or compose ports) opens a port on the host for people and tools outside Docker: the browser, curl, an IDE database client.
  • Containers on the same network need no published ports to talk. The app reaches postgres:5432 directly. Do not publish the database port on a server unless you need it; leaving it unpublished means the internet cannot reach it at all.
  • The port on the right of host:container is the container's port, the one used inside the network. Mapping 5433:5432 changes only what the host sees; containers still use 5432.

Reaching the host from a container

Sometimes the app in a container must call something running on your machine (a database you installed locally). Docker Desktop provides the special name host.docker.internal that resolves to the host. On Linux Engine you add it yourself: docker run --add-host=host.docker.internal:host-gateway … (in compose: extra_hosts: ["host.docker.internal:host-gateway"]). Use it only for development; production services should be containers or managed services on a real network.

Network commands

$ docker network ls
$ docker network inspect shop-net      # subnet, attached containers and their IPs
$ docker network connect shop-net pg   # attach a running container
$ docker network rm shop-net

Rule: the app inside Docker must bind to 0.0.0.0, and call others by service name

Spring Boot listens on all interfaces by default, so published ports work. If you set server.address=127.0.0.1 (as on a bare-metal server behind Nginx), a published port in Docker will not reach the app; leave it unset in containers.

Edge cases

  • On the default bridge there is no name resolution; --link is legacy. Use a user-defined network (compose does this automatically).
  • Two compose projects get separate networks, so their services cannot see each other unless you attach both to an external network.
  • Publishing -p 5432:5432 on a cloud server exposes PostgreSQL to the internet. Docker writes its own firewall rules, so ufw rules may not block published ports.

Interview: "Spring Boot in Docker cannot connect to PostgreSQL. Why?"

  • Usually the URL uses localhost, which is the container itself; use the service name (postgres) on a shared network.
  • Also check: same network, database healthy before the app starts, correct credentials, and the container's port (5432), not the host-mapped one.

Docker Compose: Spring Boot, PostgreSQL and Redis with one command

A compose.yaml file describes all your containers, networks and volumes; docker compose up -d --build builds and starts the whole stack, and depends_on with condition: service_healthy makes the app wait until the database is really ready.

What Compose is for

A real app is several containers: the API, a database, a cache, maybe Nginx. Starting them with long docker run commands is error-prone. Docker Compose lets you declare them once in YAML and manage them together. The file is named compose.yaml (older name docker-compose.yml also works), and the command is docker compose (a space; the old standalone tool was docker-compose with a hyphen). A top-level version: key is obsolete in current Compose and can be left out.

File structure

KeyPurpose
servicesThe containers; each key is a service name (also its DNS name)
image / buildUse a ready image, or build from a Dockerfile (build: .)
portsPublish "host:container" ports
environment / env_fileVariables, inline or from a file
volumesMount named volumes or host paths
networksAttach to networks (default one is created automatically)
depends_onStart order; with condition also wait for health
healthcheckCommand that tells Docker if the service is ready
restartRestart policy: no, on-failure, always, unless-stopped
volumes / networks (top level)Declare the named volumes and networks used above

A complete stack: Spring Boot + PostgreSQL + Redis

Project layout:

shop-api/
  Dockerfile
  compose.yaml
  .env              (not committed)
  .env.example      (committed: names only, fake values)
  pom.xml
  src/
POSTGRES_DB=shop
POSTGRES_USER=shop
POSTGRES_PASSWORD=change-me-locally
APP_PORT=8080
services:
  postgres:
    image: postgres:16
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: ${POSTGRES_DB}
      POSTGRES_USER: ${POSTGRES_USER}
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD-SHELL", "pg_isready -U ${POSTGRES_USER} -d ${POSTGRES_DB}"]
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 10
    restart: unless-stopped

  redis:
    image: redis:7
    command: ["redis-server", "--appendonly", "yes"]
    volumes:
      - redisdata:/data
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "redis-cli", "ping"]
      interval: 5s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 10
    restart: unless-stopped

  api:
    build: .
    image: shop-api:dev
    ports:
      - "${APP_PORT}:8080"
    environment:
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/${POSTGRES_DB}
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME: ${POSTGRES_USER}
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD: ${POSTGRES_PASSWORD}
      SPRING_DATA_REDIS_HOST: redis
      SPRING_DATA_REDIS_PORT: "6379"
      SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE: prod
    depends_on:
      postgres:
        condition: service_healthy
      redis:
        condition: service_healthy
    restart: unless-stopped

volumes:
  pgdata:
  redisdata:

What each part does

Service names
postgres, redis, api are also the host names on the automatic default network. That is why the datasource URL uses postgres.
${POSTGRES_DB}
Compose reads a .env file in the same folder and substitutes ${VAR} in the YAML. You can set a default with ${VAR:-value} or require a value with ${VAR:?error message}.
image: postgres:16
A pinned version, not latest.
volumes: pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
The named volume holds the database. It is declared in the top-level volumes: block.
healthcheck (postgres)
pg_isready exits 0 once PostgreSQL accepts connections. Compose runs it every 5 s; after enough passes the service state becomes healthy.
healthcheck (redis)
redis-cli ping returns PONG when Redis is ready.
build: .
Builds the API image from the Dockerfile in this folder (the multi-stage one from before). image: shop-api:dev names the result.
ports: "${APP_PORT}:8080"
Only the API is published. PostgreSQL and Redis are reachable from the API through the network but not from outside.
SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL …
Spring Boot's relaxed binding maps the environment variable SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL to the property spring.datasource.url (upper case, dots become underscores). No code change needed.
SPRING_DATA_REDIS_HOST
Property spring.data.redis.host.
depends_on … service_healthy
Start the API only after PostgreSQL and Redis report healthy. Plain depends_on: [postgres] waits only until the container has started, which is long before the database accepts connections.
restart: unless-stopped
Containers come back after a crash or a server reboot.

Spring Boot version differences

Spring Boot 3.x and 4.x use spring.data.redis.*. Boot 2.x used spring.redis.* (so SPRING_REDIS_HOST). Schema changes: set SPRING_JPA_HIBERNATE_DDL_AUTO=validate and use Flyway or Liquibase in production.

Compose commands

$ docker compose up -d --build        # build images if needed, start everything in the background
$ docker compose ps                   # status, ports and health of each service
NAME            SERVICE    STATUS                  PORTS
shop-api-api-1  api        Up 25 seconds           0.0.0.0:8080->8080/tcp
shop-api-postgres-1  postgres   Up 40 seconds (healthy)   5432/tcp
shop-api-redis-1     redis      Up 40 seconds (healthy)   6379/tcp
$ docker compose logs -f api          # follow one service's logs
$ docker compose exec postgres psql -U shop -d shop
$ docker compose up -d --build api    # rebuild and recreate only the API
$ docker compose restart api
$ docker compose down                 # stop and remove containers and the network; keep volumes
$ docker compose down -v              # ALSO delete volumes (database data!)
$ docker compose config               # print the final file with variables substituted

docker compose config is the quickest way to check that variables from .env were substituted as you expected. Compose names containers <project>-<service>-<number>; the project name defaults to the folder name (override with -p or a top-level name:).

Overriding files and profiles (brief)

  • Override files. Compose automatically merges compose.yaml with compose.override.yaml if it exists — handy for developer-only settings (publishing the DB port, bind-mounting code). For production use explicit files: docker compose -f compose.yaml -f compose.prod.yaml up -d. Later files override earlier ones.
  • Profiles. Add profiles: ["tools"] to a service (for example pgAdmin); it starts only with docker compose --profile tools up.
services:
  postgres:
    ports:
      - "127.0.0.1:5432:5432"     # let my IDE reach the database
  pgadmin:
    image: dpage/pgadmin4:latest
    profiles: ["tools"]
    environment:
      PGADMIN_DEFAULT_EMAIL: dev@example.com
      PGADMIN_DEFAULT_PASSWORD: devonly
    ports:
      - "5050:80"

Mistake: the app starts before the database is ready

Without a healthcheck and condition: service_healthy, the API starts at the same time as PostgreSQL, tries to connect for the first time during DB initialisation and crashes with Connection refused. restart: unless-stopped may hide it by retrying, but the proper fix is the healthcheck. Spring's own retries (for example spring.datasource.hikari initialisation) are not a substitute.

Mistake: YAML indentation and quoting

  • Use spaces, never tabs; two spaces per level; list items start with - .
  • Quote port mappings ("8080:8080"): YAML can read unquoted xx:yy numbers below 60 as base-60 numbers.
  • In environment: map form write KEY: value; in list form - KEY=value. Do not mix.

Edge cases

  • depends_on only controls start order at up; if PostgreSQL restarts later, the API is not restarted. The app must handle reconnects (HikariCP does).
  • Changing a .env value does not recreate containers by itself; run docker compose up -d again.
  • Changing POSTGRES_PASSWORD after the volume exists has no effect: the database was initialised with the old one. Remove the volume (down -v) in dev, or change the password inside the database.

In real projects

The same compose.yaml is used on a developer laptop and on a small single-server production (an EC2 instance that runs docker compose pull && docker compose up -d; see CD over SSH). Larger systems move to Kubernetes or ECS, but the concepts — services, networks, volumes, health — carry over.

Interview: "How do you run Spring Boot with PostgreSQL in Docker?"

  • Compose file with three services, a named volume for the DB, and env vars with service names.
  • Healthcheck + condition: service_healthy so the app waits; publish only the API port.
  • up -d --build, logs -f, down (not down -v).

Environment variables and secrets: never put passwords inside images

Configuration and secrets enter a container at run time — through environment variables, an untracked .env file, or Docker/Compose secrets — never through the Dockerfile, because every layer of an image can be read by anyone who can pull it.

Why secrets must stay out of images

An image is a stack of layers, and each layer keeps its files forever. If a Dockerfile does COPY .env . and a later step runs rm .env, the file is still inside the earlier layer. docker history shows every ENV and ARG value. Once the image is pushed to a registry (or shared with a friend), the password is out. Rotating the secret is the only fix. The twelve-factor rule applies: the same image runs in every environment; only the configuration changes.

Wrong: password baked into the image

ENV SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD=SuperSecret123
ARG GITHUB_TOKEN
RUN git clone https://${GITHUB_TOKEN}@github.com/org/private.git

Right: supplied when the container starts

$ docker run -d --env-file .env shop-api:1.0
# or in compose: env_file / environment / secrets

Ways to pass configuration

MethodHowNotes
-e / environment-e SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prodFine for non-secret settings; secrets stay in shell history
--env-file / env_fileA file of KEY=value linesKeep out of Git; chmod 600 on servers
Compose .envSubstituted into ${VAR} in compose.yamlVariables for the YAML itself; not automatically passed to containers
Mounted config file-v ./config:/app/config:roSpring Boot reads config/application.yml
Compose / Docker secretsFiles mounted at /run/secrets/nameNot in docker inspect; app must read the file
Platform secret storesAWS Secrets Manager, Parameter Store, Vault, Kubernetes SecretsBest for production; injected as env or files

A .env file is plain text. Keep it in .gitignore and commit an .env.example with fake values so teammates know which variables exist.

# .gitignore
.env
.env.*
!.env.example

# .env.example (safe to commit)
POSTGRES_DB=shop
POSTGRES_USER=shop
POSTGRES_PASSWORD=replace-me
APP_PORT=8080

Docker and Compose secrets (brief)

A secret in Compose is a file that Docker mounts read-only into the container at /run/secrets/<name>. It does not appear in docker inspect or docker history. Many official images accept *_FILE variables so they read the value from such a file.

services:
  postgres:
    image: postgres:16
    environment:
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD_FILE: /run/secrets/db_password
    secrets:
      - db_password

secrets:
  db_password:
    file: ./secrets/db_password.txt   # keep this folder out of Git

For Spring Boot, either set spring.config.import=configtree:/run/secrets/ (every file in the folder becomes a property named after the file) or read the file in a small start-up script. Check the Spring Boot reference for "Config Tree" to confirm the property naming for your version.

Build args are not secret

ARG values appear in docker history and in image metadata. For a secret needed only during the build (a token for a private Maven repository), use a BuildKit secret mount, which is never written into a layer:

RUN --mount=type=secret,id=maven_settings,target=/root/.m2/settings.xml \
    mvn -B -q package -DskipTests
$ docker build --secret id=maven_settings,src=$HOME/.m2/settings.xml -t shop-api:1.0 .

Finding leaked secrets

  • Run a scanner such as gitleaks or trufflehog on the repository (and in CI). GitHub's secret scanning and push protection catch many token formats automatically.
  • Check an image: docker history --no-trunc shop-api:1.0 and docker run --rm -it --entrypoint sh shop-api:1.0, then look for .env, key files and passwords.
  • If a secret was ever committed or pushed in an image: rotate it. Deleting the file in a later commit does not remove it from history.

Rule: one image, many environments

Do not build a "production image" with production settings inside. Build once, tag, and deploy the same image to test and production with different environment variables and secrets.

Mistake: committing .env, or a fallback password in application.yml

A line like spring.datasource.password=${DB_PASSWORD:admin123} silently ships a default password. Prefer failing at start-up when the variable is missing: ${DB_PASSWORD} with no default in production profiles.

Edge cases

  • Environment variables are visible to anyone who can run docker inspect or read /proc/<pid>/environ, and Spring's Actuator /env endpoint (mask values and never expose it publicly).
  • Do not log the full environment or configuration on start-up.
  • A .env file used by Compose does not pass every variable into the container; only the ones you list under environment (or load with env_file) reach it.

Interview: "How do you handle secrets with Docker?"

  • Never in the Dockerfile, image or Git. Inject at runtime via env vars, env files, Docker secrets or a secret manager.
  • ARG/ENV are visible in docker history; use BuildKit secret mounts for build-time secrets; rotate anything that leaked.

Registries: Docker Hub, GHCR, tagging, pushing and image scanning

A registry stores images; log in with docker login, tag the image with the registry path and a version or git SHA, then docker push and docker pull — Docker Hub is the public default, GitHub Container Registry (ghcr.io) fits projects hosted on GitHub.

Docker Hub versus GitHub Container Registry

Docker HubGitHub Container Registry (GHCR)
Addressdocker.io (implicit)ghcr.io
Image nameusername/app:tagghcr.io/owner/app:tag
Login in CIDocker Hub username + access tokenThe workflow's built-in GITHUB_TOKEN (needs packages: write)
VisibilityPublic or private repos (free plans have limits)Public or private, tied to GitHub permissions
NotesWhere all official base images live; pulls from anonymous users are rate-limitedConvenient when code and images live together; check GitHub's current storage and bandwidth limits

Other registries you will meet: AWS ECR (see Cloud), Google Artifact Registry, Azure Container Registry, and self-hosted ones like Harbor or Nexus. They all speak the same protocol, so the commands are identical.

Tagging strategy

Give each build an immutable, traceable tag and optionally move a friendly tag:

  • shop-api:3f9c2ab — the short git commit SHA. Unique per commit; tells you exactly which code runs. Best for CI deploys.
  • shop-api:1.2.0 — a semantic version for releases. Never overwrite it.
  • shop-api:1.2, shop-api:1 — movable convenience tags for "latest patch".
  • shop-api:latest — optional alias for the newest build; do not deploy production from it.

Login, tag, push, pull

$ docker login                        # asks for username and an access token (not your password)
$ docker build -t shop-api:1.2.0 .
$ docker tag shop-api:1.2.0 asha/shop-api:1.2.0
$ docker tag shop-api:1.2.0 asha/shop-api:latest
$ docker push asha/shop-api:1.2.0
The push refers to repository [docker.io/asha/shop-api]
1.2.0: digest: sha256:4f2a... size: 1789
$ docker pull asha/shop-api:1.2.0    # on the server
$ echo "$GHCR_TOKEN" | docker login ghcr.io -u asha --password-stdin
$ docker tag shop-api:1.2.0 ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:1.2.0
$ docker push ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:1.2.0

docker tag does not copy anything; it adds another name to the same image id. --password-stdin keeps the token out of your shell history and process list. Always use an access token (revocable, limited scope) instead of your account password. In CI the workflow logs in with secrets (next module).

Image scanning (brief)

Images include an OS and libraries, which may contain known vulnerabilities (CVEs). Scanners compare the image contents with vulnerability databases. Docker Scout is built into Docker Desktop and the CLI; Trivy and Grype are popular open-source scanners.

$ docker scout quickview shop-api:1.2.0     # summary of CVEs by severity
$ docker scout cves shop-api:1.2.0          # detailed list
$ trivy image shop-api:1.2.0                # if Trivy is installed

Fix findings by updating the base image tag (rebuild regularly to get patched layers), upgrading dependencies and using smaller bases. Run a scan in CI and fail the build on critical issues that have a fix available.

Convention: tag by SHA in CI, by version on release

Pipelines push :<git-sha> on every merge to main; a release tag (v1.2.0) also pushes :1.2.0. Deployments reference the exact tag, which makes rollback a matter of deploying the previous tag.

Mistake: denied: requested access to the resource is denied

Pushing shop-api:1.0 without your namespace tries to write to docker.io/library/shop-api. Tag it as youruser/shop-api:1.0. Also check that you are logged in to the right registry and that the token has write access.

Edge cases

  • Anonymous pulls from Docker Hub are rate-limited; you may see toomanyrequests on shared CI runners. Log in before pulling, or mirror base images to your own registry.
  • Pushing a tag that already exists silently moves it; the old image becomes untagged ("dangling").
  • A GHCR package that you push from a workflow may be private by default; link it to the repository and set visibility in the package settings.
  • exec format error on a server usually means the image was built for another CPU architecture. Build multi-platform with docker buildx build --platform linux/amd64,linux/arm64.

Interview: "How do you version and publish Docker images?"

  • Build once in CI, tag with the git SHA (and semver on release), push to a registry (GHCR/ECR/Hub), deploy by tag; never deploy latest.
  • Log in with a token; scan for CVEs; use digests for strict reproducibility.

Troubleshooting Docker: the usual problems and how to fix them

Most Docker problems fall into a few patterns: read the logs first (docker logs), then the exit code and the state (docker inspect), then check ports, names and permissions.

A debugging routine

  1. docker ps -a — is the container running, or did it exit? Note the status and exit code.
  2. docker logs --tail 100 name — the app's stack trace is usually here.
  3. docker inspect name — State.ExitCode, State.OOMKilled, the environment, mounts and network.
  4. docker exec -it name sh — look around inside (if it is still running); try curl to the other service by name.
  5. If it will not start: docker run --rm -it --entrypoint sh image to get a shell in a fresh container and run the start command by hand.
  6. Compose: docker compose ps, docker compose logs -f, docker compose config.

Troubleshooting table

SymptomLikely causeFix
Container exits immediatelyThe main process ended: app crashed (see logs), wrong ENTRYPOINT, missing JAR, shell form, or a command that finishes (like bash without -it)docker logs; check exit code; run with --entrypoint sh to debug
Exit code 137Killed: out of memory (OOM) or docker killCheck OOMKilled; raise the limit or reduce MaxRAMPercentage
Exit code 143Stopped with SIGTERM (normal for docker stop)Usually fine
Port is already allocated / address already in useAnother process or container uses the host portStop it, or map to another host port (-p 8081:8080); find it with docker ps or netstat
App cannot reach the databaselocalhost used instead of service name; different networks; DB not ready; wrong credentialsUse postgres:5432; same network; healthcheck + service_healthy; check env vars
Page not reachable from the browserPort not published, app listens on 127.0.0.1 only, firewall or security group-p / ports; do not set server.address in the container; open the port
Permission denied on a volume or bind mountContainer user (UID) cannot write the host folder; SELinux labelschown the folder to the container UID; use a named volume; check USER
The image is huge (1 GB+)Single-stage build with JDK and Maven, big base, caches left in layers, no .dockerignoreMulti-stage build, JRE base, .dockerignore, clean in the same RUN; inspect with docker history
Changes are not picked upOld image still used (forgot --build), cache hit on a stale layer, container not recreated, bind mount missingdocker compose up -d --build; docker build --no-cache; check the image id with docker ps
Build is slow every timeSource copied before dependencies; huge build contextOrder layers; add .dockerignore
exec format errorImage built for another CPU architecture (ARM vs x86)Build for the target with --platform
no space left on deviceOld images, containers and volumes fill the diskdocker system df, then docker system prune
unhealthy containerHealthcheck command fails or tool missing (curl not in the image)docker inspect --format='{{json .State.Health}}' name
Cannot connect to the Docker daemonDocker Desktop not running, or your user is not in the docker group (Linux)Start Docker Desktop; sudo usermod -aG docker $USER then log in again

Tip: keep a throw-away debug container on the network

docker run --rm -it --network shop-net nicolaka/netshoot (or a plain alpine with apk add curl bind-tools) lets you test DNS and ports from inside the same network: nslookup postgres, nc -zv postgres 5432. Production images often have no such tools, which is why this helps.

Mistake: fixing the symptom with --privileged or root

Running containers with --privileged, as root, or mounting the Docker socket (/var/run/docker.sock) "to make it work" gives the container control of the host. Find the real cause (usually file ownership or a missing option).

Interview: "A container exits immediately after starting. How do you debug it?"

  • docker ps -a and docker logs for the error; docker inspect for exit code (137 = killed/OOM, 1 = app error).
  • Run the image with a shell entrypoint to start the app manually; check the JAR path, env vars and the Java version.

Interview questions — Docker

What is Docker and why do we use containers?

Docker packages an application with its runtime and dependencies into an image that runs the same everywhere as a container. It solves "works on my machine", gives isolation between apps, starts in seconds, and is the standard deployment unit for cloud platforms and Kubernetes.

Container versus virtual machine?

A VM runs a full guest OS with its own kernel on a hypervisor; a container is an isolated process sharing the host kernel. Containers are smaller (MBs), start in about a second and pack more density; VMs isolate more strongly. In the cloud, containers usually run inside VMs.

What is a container really, at the kernel level?

A normal Linux process with namespaces (own process IDs, network, hostname, mounts) and cgroups (limits for CPU, memory, I/O), running on a layered filesystem created from an image. Docker is a tool that sets these features up.

Image versus container? What are layers and why do they matter?

An image is a read-only template; a container is a running instance with a thin writable layer. Each filesystem-changing Dockerfile instruction creates a layer. Layers are cached and shared, so ordering instructions from least to most frequently changing makes rebuilds fast and downloads small.

Explain ENTRYPOINT versus CMD, and shell form versus exec form.

ENTRYPOINT is the fixed executable; CMD supplies default arguments that docker run image args replaces. Exec form (JSON array) runs the program directly as PID 1 so it gets SIGTERM and can shut down gracefully; shell form wraps it in sh -c, and signals may not reach the app.

How do you make a Docker image for a Spring Boot app small and fast to build?

Use a multi-stage build: Maven/JDK stage builds the JAR (copy pom.xml and resolve dependencies first so they are cached), runtime stage uses a JRE image and copies only the JAR. Add .dockerignore, run as a non-root user and consider layered JARs. Final images are a few hundred MB instead of 700 MB+.

What is the difference between COPY and ADD, ARG and ENV, EXPOSE and -p?

ADD also extracts local archives and fetches URLs; prefer COPY. ARG exists only at build time; ENV persists into the running container. EXPOSE only documents a port; -p host:container (or compose ports) actually publishes it.

Data in a container disappears when it is removed. How do you persist it?

Mount a named volume at the data directory (-v pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data). A bind mount maps a host folder and suits development. tmpfs is memory only. Volumes survive docker rm and docker compose down, but not down -v.

Your Spring Boot container throws "Connection refused" for localhost:5432. Why?

localhost inside a container is the container itself, not the host or the database container. Put both on the same user-defined network (Compose does this) and connect to the service name: jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/shop. Also make sure the database is healthy before the app starts.

What does depends_on do in Compose? How do you wait for the database?

By default only start order: the dependency container has started, not that it accepts connections. Add a healthcheck (pg_isready) to the database and depends_on: postgres: condition: service_healthy to the app so it starts when the database is truly ready.

How do you handle secrets in Docker?

Never bake them into images or commit .env. Inject at runtime with environment variables, an untracked env file, Docker/Compose secrets (files under /run/secrets) or a secret manager. ENV and ARG are visible in docker history. Use BuildKit secret mounts for build-time secrets and rotate anything that leaked.

How does the JVM behave in a container? What is MaxRAMPercentage?

Modern JVMs read cgroup limits for memory and CPUs. The default max heap is 25% of the container memory. -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75 sets it as a share of the limit, leaving room for metaspace, stacks and native memory. Always set a container memory limit; exit code 137 with OOMKilled means the total exceeded it.

docker stop versus docker kill, and docker run versus docker exec?

stop sends SIGTERM, waits 10 seconds by default, then SIGKILL; kill sends SIGKILL immediately. run creates a new container from an image; exec runs an additional process inside an already running container.

Why not use the latest tag in production? How do you tag images?

latest is a movable label; a rebuild can change the Java version or dependencies without notice, and you cannot tell what is deployed or roll back. Tag with the git SHA and a semantic version, deploy by explicit tag (or digest), and roll back by redeploying the previous tag.

Your image is 1.2 GB. How do you find out why and reduce it?

Run docker history image to see layer sizes. Typical causes: JDK and Maven in the final image, a big base, build output and caches, or a missing .dockerignore. Fix with a multi-stage build, a JRE-only base (or slim/Alpine after testing), cleaning in the same RUN and excluding files from the context.

Cheat sheet — Docker

  • Container vs VM shared kernel + namespaces and cgroups vs full guest OS on a hypervisor
  • Image / container / registry template / running instance + writable layer / image store
  • Layers one per RUN, COPY, ADD; cached by content; a changed layer rebuilds all after it
  • Tags name:tag; pin versions, tag with git SHA or semver, avoid latest in prod
  • Java base eclipse-temurin:21-jre to run, maven:3.9-eclipse-temurin-21 to build
  • docker run -d -p 8081:8080 --name n -e K=v --env-file f -v vol:/path --rm --network net --restart unless-stopped
  • Look docker ps -a, logs -f --tail 100, inspect, stats
  • Get inside docker exec -it name sh; new container: docker run --rm -it --entrypoint sh image
  • Stop / remove stop (SIGTERM, 10 s) vs kill (SIGKILL); rm, rmi
  • Clean up docker system df, system prune; --volumes deletes data
  • Build docker build -t name:tag .; --no-cache to ignore the cache
  • Dockerfile order FROM, WORKDIR, COPY pom.xml, RUN deps, COPY src, RUN package, then runtime stage
  • Exec form ENTRYPOINT ["java","-jar","app.jar"] so Java is PID 1 and gets SIGTERM
  • ENTRYPOINT vs CMD fixed executable vs overridable default arguments
  • Best practice multi-stage, .dockerignore, non-root USER, one process, no secrets, pinned versions
  • JVM -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75, always set a memory limit; exit 137 + OOMKilled = over limit
  • Volumes named volume for data, bind mount for dev files, tmpfs for RAM; down -v deletes volumes
  • Networks user-defined bridge gives DNS by name; compose creates one automatically
  • localhost trap inside a container localhost is itself; use postgres:5432, not localhost
  • Ports host:container; containers talk internally without published ports
  • Compose up -d --build, ps, logs -f, exec, down, config
  • Wait for DB healthcheck (pg_isready) + depends_on: condition: service_healthy
  • Spring env vars SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL = spring.datasource.url; SPRING_DATA_REDIS_HOST
  • Secrets never in image or Git; env file (untracked), Docker secrets, secret manager; ARG is not secret
  • Registry docker login, tag, push, pull; GHCR: ghcr.io/owner/app

Practice — Docker

  1. First containers. Install Docker Desktop (WSL2 backend), run hello-world, then docker run --rm -it eclipse-temurin:21-jdk jshell. Run nginx with -p 8081:80 and open it in the browser; view its logs, enter it with exec, stop and remove it. Done when you can list, stop and remove containers without looking anything up.
  2. Persist a database. Start PostgreSQL with a named volume, create a table, remove the container, start a new one with the same volume and check that the data is still there. Then run down -v style cleanup and watch it disappear. Done when you can explain which commands delete data.
  3. Dockerize Project 2 (single stage first). Write a simple Dockerfile (JDK image, copy the JAR, ENTRYPOINT) and run it. Note the image size with docker images. Done when the app answers on localhost:8080.
  4. Multi-stage build. Rewrite it as the multi-stage Dockerfile from this module (Maven stage, JRE stage, non-root user, healthcheck) and add a .dockerignore. Compare image size with step 3 and time a second build after changing one line of code to see the cache work. Done when the final image is clearly smaller and the second build skips the dependency download.
  5. Break the localhost trap. Run PostgreSQL and your app in two containers with the datasource URL set to localhost; read the error. Create a network, use the container name, and fix it. Done when you can explain the error to someone in two sentences.
  6. Compose the stack. Write a compose.yaml with the API, PostgreSQL and Redis, a .env file (git-ignored) and .env.example, named volumes and healthchecks with service_healthy. Run docker compose up -d --build and check docker compose ps shows all healthy. Done when a fresh clone plus one command starts the whole app.
  7. JVM limits. Run the container with -m 512m and -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75, watch docker stats, then try a very low limit (-m 128m) and find the exit code and the OOMKilled flag. Done when you can explain exit code 137.
  8. Secrets audit. Run docker history --no-trunc on your image and look for anything sensitive; scan the repository with gitleaks and the image with docker scout cves. Move any secret to an env file or Compose secret. Done when no password exists in the Dockerfile, the compose file or the image.
  9. Publish the image. Create a Docker Hub (or GHCR) access token, log in with --password-stdin, tag the image with a version and the git SHA, push it, then pull and run it from a different folder or machine. Done when the pulled image runs with only environment variables supplied.
  10. Final check (roadmap goal). Without notes, write a Dockerfile and a docker-compose.yml from scratch for Spring Boot + PostgreSQL, bring it up, and fix one deliberately introduced fault (wrong host name, missing healthcheck or wrong port). Done when you can do this in under 30 minutes.
4

Module 4·About 4 daysMust

CI/CD with GitHub Actions

10 topics · about 1 h of reading

CI/CD means a robot does the boring, risky work for you on every change: build the code, run the tests, package the app, and (if all is green) deploy it. GitHub Actions is the CI/CD service built into GitHub; you describe the steps in a YAML file inside your repository and GitHub runs them on its servers. Teams that do this ship smaller changes, more often, with fewer production surprises — and it is a standard expectation in a developer's first week at a company.

This module uses what you already know: Git branches and pull requests (Git · Module 3), Maven and tests (Core Java · Module 8, Spring Boot · Module 5), Docker images (Module 3) and SSH deployment to a Linux server (Module 1). Cloud-specific deployment targets follow in Module 5.

After this module you will be able to:

  • Explain CI, continuous delivery and continuous deployment, and why teams use them.
  • Read and write GitHub Actions workflows: triggers, jobs, steps, runners, contexts and secrets.
  • Build and test a Spring Boot project on every push and pull request, with caching and test reports.
  • Build a Docker image and push it to GitHub Container Registry (or Docker Hub) from the pipeline.
  • Deploy automatically on merge to main over SSH or through a deploy hook, with approvals.
  • Protect main with required checks, show a status badge, and describe Jenkins.

CI, continuous delivery and continuous deployment

Continuous Integration builds and tests every change automatically so the main branch always works; continuous delivery keeps every green build ready to release with one click; continuous deployment releases every green build to production automatically.

The problem CI solves

Without automation, a team of five works for two weeks on separate branches, merges everything on the last day, and finds that the pieces do not fit together. One person's change breaks another's, the tests were never run on the merged code, and a release needs a weekend. This is called integration hell. The cure is to integrate small changes often and let a machine check each one.

Three terms that sound alike

Continuous Integration (CI)
Every push and pull request triggers an automatic build and test run on a clean machine. Developers merge to the shared branch at least daily. The goal: main is always working.
Continuous Delivery (CD)
After CI passes, the pipeline also produces a release-ready artifact (a JAR or Docker image) and deploys it to a test/staging environment. Releasing to production is possible at any time, but a human clicks the button.
Continuous Deployment (CD)
Every change that passes all automated checks goes to production automatically, with no manual approval. It needs strong tests, monitoring and a fast rollback.
CIContinuous deliveryContinuous deployment
Builds and tests every changeYesYesYes
Creates a deployable artifactSometimesYesYes
Deploys to stagingNoAutomaticallyAutomatically
Deploys to productionNoOn manual approvalAutomatically
Typical teamEveryoneBanks, regulated productsWeb products with strong tests

"CD" is used for both delivery and deployment; ask which one people mean. In your Project 3 you will build continuous deployment on a small scale: a merge to main deploys itself.

A pipeline as a picture
