A pipeline is the whole chain from commit to running software. Each stage is a gate. If tests fail, nothing is packaged and nothing is deployed; the author gets an email or a red cross on the pull request.

Why CI matters for a team

  • Fast feedback. You learn within minutes that your change breaks a test — while you still remember the code.
  • A neutral build machine. "Works on my machine" is replaced by "works on the CI machine", which starts clean every time (right Java version, no leftover files).
  • A safe main branch. Combined with branch protection, broken code cannot be merged.
  • Repeatable releases. The same scripted steps run every time; no one forgets a step or deploys from a laptop.
  • Small, frequent releases. Small changes are easy to review and to roll back.
  • Quality gates. Style checks, test coverage, security scans and dependency checks run automatically on every pull request.

What CI/CD needs from you

Automation is only as good as what it runs. You need tests that are fast and reliable (a flaky test that fails randomly teaches everyone to ignore red builds), a build that works from a clean checkout with one command (./mvnw verify), configuration kept out of the code (environment variables, secrets), and a deployment you can repeat by script. Everything the pipeline does should also be doable by hand on your laptop.

Rule: a red main branch is the team's top priority

If the build on main fails, stop merging and fix or revert at once. Everyone else's pull requests will otherwise fail for reasons they did not cause.

Edge cases

  • CI proves only what your tests cover. A green build with weak tests gives false confidence.
  • Some changes (database migrations, breaking API changes) cannot simply be deployed and reverted; they need careful steps even with a perfect pipeline.
  • Continuous deployment is not "no testing"; it is "all the testing is automatic".

Interview: "What is the difference between CI, continuous delivery and continuous deployment?"

  • CI: automatic build and test on every change. Delivery: always releasable, production deploy is a manual decision. Deployment: every green change reaches production automatically.
  • Benefits: fast feedback, fewer integration problems, repeatable releases, smaller changes.

GitHub Actions concepts: workflows, jobs, steps, runners and expressions

A workflow is a YAML file in .github/workflows that runs when an event happens; it contains jobs (run on runners, in parallel unless you use needs), and each job is a list of steps that either run a shell command (run) or use a ready-made action (uses).

The building blocks

Workflow
One YAML file in .github/workflows/. A repository can have many (ci.yml, deploy.yml).
Event
What starts a workflow: a push, a pull request, a schedule, a manual click, a release.
Job
A group of steps that runs on one fresh runner machine. Jobs run in parallel by default.
Step
One task inside a job. Steps run in order and share the job's filesystem.
Action
A reusable, versioned unit of work (uses: actions/checkout@v4) written by GitHub or the community, found in the Marketplace.
Runner
The machine that runs a job. GitHub-hosted runners (ubuntu-latest, windows-latest, macos-latest) are fresh virtual machines that GitHub provides; self-hosted runners are machines you install the runner agent on (your own server, inside your network).

A first workflow

name: Hello

on: push

jobs:
  greet:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Say hello
        run: echo "Hello from commit ${{ github.sha }} on ${{ github.ref_name }}"
      - name: Show the runner
        run: |
          uname -a
          java -version

Push this file and open the repository's Actions tab: you see the run, each job and the log of every step. The file must be on the repository's default branch (or the branch you push) to run.

Run versus uses

run:uses:
What it isA shell command or scriptA packaged action: owner/repo@version
Examplerun: ./mvnw -B verifyuses: actions/setup-java@v4
InputsNone (use env vars)with: block
Use forYour own build commandsCheckout, Java setup, caching, Docker login, uploads

A multi-line command uses | (each line is run in order; the step fails if a line fails). The version after @ is a tag, such as v4 (a major version that receives compatible updates), a full tag, or a commit SHA (the safest, immutable choice).

Check each action's README for the latest major version

Official actions release new majors regularly (for example to move to a newer Node.js runtime). The versions in these notes (actions/checkout@v4, actions/setup-java@v4, actions/upload-artifact@v4, docker/build-push-action@v6 and so on) were current when written. Before you copy a workflow, open each action's page on GitHub and use the latest major shown in its README. Dependabot can also open pull requests that update action versions for you.

needs, if, env and outputs

env:
  JAVA_VERSION: "21"             # workflow-level variable

jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: echo "testing with Java $JAVA_VERSION"

  package:
    needs: test                  # waits for 'test'; skipped if 'test' fails
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: echo "packaging"

  deploy:
    needs: [test, package]
    if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'   # only on main
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Only when a previous step failed
        if: failure()
        run: echo "something earlier failed"
      - run: echo "deploying"
  • needs creates the order between jobs. Without it, jobs start at the same time.
  • if decides whether a job or step runs. Status functions: success() (default), failure(), always(), cancelled().
  • env can be set at workflow, job or step level; inner levels override outer ones.
  • Jobs run on different machines, so files from one job are not available in another. Pass files with artifacts and small values with outputs.

Contexts and expressions

Inside ${{ ... }} you write an expression that reads a context (an object with information) or calls a function. GitHub evaluates it before the step runs.

ExpressionGives
${{ github.sha }}The full commit SHA being built
${{ github.ref_name }}Branch or tag name (main, v1.2.0)
${{ github.actor }}The user who triggered the run
${{ github.repository }}owner/repo
${{ github.event_name }}push, pull_request, ...
${{ secrets.DEPLOY_KEY }}A secret value (masked in logs)
${{ vars.APP_URL }}A non-secret configuration variable
${{ runner.os }}Linux, Windows, macOS
${{ matrix.java }}Current value in a matrix build
${{ hashFiles('**/pom.xml') }}A hash of matching files (cache keys)

Matrix builds

A matrix runs the same job several times with different values — for example tests on Java 17 and 21, or on Linux and Windows. GitHub creates one job per combination.

jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    strategy:
      fail-fast: false            # let other combinations finish if one fails
      matrix:
        java: [17, 21]
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: ${{ matrix.java }}
      - run: ./mvnw -B verify

Permissions, GITHUB_TOKEN and concurrency

Every run gets a temporary GITHUB_TOKEN, valid only for that run and that repository. What it may do is set by the permissions key. Follow least privilege: give read access by default and add more only to jobs that need it.

permissions:
  contents: read                 # default for all jobs

concurrency:
  group: ci-${{ github.ref }}    # one run per branch at a time
  cancel-in-progress: true       # a new push cancels the older run

jobs:
  publish:
    permissions:
      contents: read
      packages: write            # this job may push to GHCR
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: echo "pushing"

A concurrency group makes sure only one run of that group is active. For CI, cancelling older runs on the same branch saves time and minutes. For deployments, use cancel-in-progress: false so a deploy is never cut in half.

Artifacts and caching in one line each

  • Artifact — a file produced by a run (test reports, a JAR) that you can download or pass to a later job: actions/upload-artifact and actions/download-artifact.
  • Cache — files reused between runs (Maven's ~/.m2) to make builds faster: actions/cache, or built into setup-java and setup-node.

YAML basics and indentation pitfalls

  • Indentation is meaning. Use spaces (two per level), never tabs. A step must be indented under steps:, and with: keys under their step.
  • A list item starts with - . In steps, each item is one step.
  • Quote strings that could be read as something else: java-version: "21" is fine either way, but "1.10" must be quoted or it becomes the number 1.1. Quote values starting with ${{ or containing : or # when in doubt.
  • on: and jobs: are top-level keys; a missing or misplaced space is the usual cause of "Invalid workflow file" errors.
  • A comment starts with #. The editor on github.com shows YAML errors before you commit; VS Code with the GitHub Actions extension validates too.

Mistake: the workflow file is in the wrong place or has the wrong extension

It must be at .github/workflows/name.yml (or .yaml) in the repository root. .github/workflow/ (missing s) or a file in a sub-folder never runs, and no error is shown.

Mistake: expecting files to exist in another job

Each job starts on a clean runner. A JAR built in build does not exist in deploy unless you upload and download it as an artifact, or rebuild, or push an image to a registry and pull it.

Interview: "Explain GitHub Actions: workflow, job, step, runner."

  • A workflow (YAML) is triggered by an event; it has jobs that run in parallel on separate runners (needs orders them); each job has sequential steps that are run commands or reusable uses actions.
  • Contexts such as github.sha and secrets.X; matrix for variations; permissions for the token; concurrency to avoid overlapping runs.

Workflow triggers: push, pull_request, manual, schedule and tags

The on: key decides when a workflow runs — typically push and pull_request with branch and path filters for CI, workflow_dispatch for a manual button, schedule for cron jobs and tag or release events for versioned releases.

push and pull_request

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]

With this, a push to main (including a merge) triggers the workflow, and so does any pull request that targets main — on creation and on each new commit pushed to it.

  • push runs on the pushed commit of that branch.
  • pull_request runs on a merge preview of the PR branch into the target branch, so you test what main would look like after the merge. This is the check that branch protection uses.
  • Feature branches without a pull request do not run, because of the filter. If you want CI on every branch push, use on: push without branches; but then a PR commit runs twice (once for push, once for the PR). Limiting push to main avoids this.

Filters: branches, paths and tags

on:
  push:
    branches: [main, "release/**"]      # patterns: * and ** wildcards
    paths:
      - "src/**"
      - "pom.xml"
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]
    paths-ignore:                       # use paths OR paths-ignore for one event, not both
      - "**.md"
    types: [opened, synchronize, reopened]   # default types; shown explicitly
FilterEffect
branches / branches-ignoreRun only for (or never for) matching branch names
paths / paths-ignoreRun only when changed files match (e.g. do not run a backend build for a README edit)
tagsRun on pushes of matching tags, e.g. v*
typesWhich activity of the event: PR opened, synchronize (new commits), reopened, ...

Careful with path filters and required checks: if a workflow is skipped because of its path filter, a required status check from it stays "pending" and the PR cannot be merged. Either do not use path filters on workflows that branch protection requires, or add a small always-running job that reports status.

workflow_dispatch: a manual button

on:
  workflow_dispatch:
    inputs:
      image_tag:
        description: "Image tag to deploy (e.g. a git SHA)"
        required: true
        type: string

jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - run: echo "Deploying tag ${{ inputs.image_tag }}"

The workflow appears with a Run workflow button in the Actions tab (the file must be on the default branch). It is perfect for "deploy this tag" and rollbacks, or for jobs you want to test without pushing commits. You can also trigger it with the GitHub CLI: gh workflow run deploy.yml -f image_tag=3f9c2ab.

schedule: cron

on:
  schedule:
    - cron: "0 2 * * *"      # minute hour day-of-month month day-of-week

The cron time is always UTC. India Standard Time is UTC+5:30, so 02:00 UTC is 07:30 IST. Scheduled runs happen on the default branch, may start a few minutes late when GitHub is busy, and GitHub may disable schedules on repositories with no activity for a long time. Use them for nightly full test suites, dependency checks or clean-up jobs.

Tags and releases

on:
  push:
    tags:
      - "v*.*.*"             # v1.2.0, v2.0.1 ...
  release:
    types: [published]

Create a tag with git tag v1.2.0 && git push origin v1.2.0. The workflow can then build the image with the tag name (${{ github.ref_name }}) and publish a GitHub Release. This is the usual way to produce versioned releases.

A few more events

  • workflow_call — lets one workflow call another (reusable workflows), so many repositories share one CI definition.
  • workflow_run — start a workflow after another one finishes (for example deploy after CI).
  • pull_request_target — runs with the base repository's permissions and secrets even for forks; powerful and easy to misuse. Avoid it until you understand the security notes in the GitHub docs.
  • issues, issue_comment, repository_dispatch — automation around issues and external triggers.

Documentation of every event and filter: docs.github.com/en/actions.

Mistake: workflow triggers itself, or runs twice per PR commit

  • A workflow that pushes a commit with the default GITHUB_TOKEN does not trigger other workflows (GitHub prevents loops), which surprises people who expect it to.
  • on: [push, pull_request] without filters runs twice for a PR branch commit. Filter push to main.

Edge cases

  • Pull requests from forks run with a read-only token and no secrets, so steps that need secrets (deploy, push image) must not run for them.
  • A workflow with on: pull_request uses the workflow file from the merge commit, so you can test a changed workflow inside its own PR.
  • Commits whose message contains [skip ci] skip push and pull_request workflows (useful for docs-only changes; check the GitHub docs for the exact supported phrases).

Interview: "When does your CI run, and how do you stop it running when only the README changed?"

  • On pull requests to main and on pushes to main; manual workflow_dispatch; optionally a nightly cron.
  • Use paths/paths-ignore filters, mindful of required status checks.

A complete CI workflow for a Spring Boot Maven project

The CI workflow checks out the code, installs Temurin Java with the Maven cache, runs ./mvnw -B verify, and uploads the test reports; add a PostgreSQL service container or Testcontainers for integration tests, and a separate job for the React frontend.

The goal

On every pull request to main and every push to main: compile the project, run all tests, and keep the test reports so a failure can be diagnosed without re-running. The build must be quick (dependencies cached) and reproducible (same Java version as production).

The workflow

name: CI

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]

permissions:
  contents: read                      # least privilege for GITHUB_TOKEN

concurrency:
  group: ci-${{ github.workflow }}-${{ github.ref }}
  cancel-in-progress: true            # a newer push cancels the older run

jobs:
  backend:
    name: Backend (Maven)
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 15               # never let a stuck build run for hours

    steps:
      - name: Check out the code
        uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - name: Set up Java 21 (Temurin) with Maven cache
        uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: "21"
          cache: maven                # caches ~/.m2, keyed on pom.xml files

      - name: Build and test
        run: ./mvnw -B -ntp verify

      - name: Upload test reports
        if: always()                  # also when tests failed
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with:
          name: surefire-reports
          path: |
            target/surefire-reports/
            target/failsafe-reports/
          if-no-files-found: ignore
          retention-days: 7

      - name: Upload the JAR
        uses: actions/upload-artifact@v4
        with:
          name: app-jar
          path: target/*.jar
          retention-days: 7

Line by line

on: push / pull_request
Run on merges to main and on every PR aimed at it.
permissions: contents: read
The token can only read the repository. Nothing in CI needs more.
concurrency
One run per branch; cancels outdated runs. The group includes the workflow name so different workflows do not cancel each other.
runs-on: ubuntu-latest
A fresh GitHub-hosted Ubuntu VM for this job. It already contains Docker, Git, Node, several JDKs and Maven; we still install our own Java to control the version.
timeout-minutes
Fails the job if it runs longer than this (the default is 360 minutes, which wastes your free minutes on a hang).
actions/checkout@v4
Clones your repository into the runner. Without this step the runner has no code. By default it fetches only the commit being built (shallow); add fetch-depth: 0 if a tool needs the full history.
actions/setup-java@v4
Installs the JDK. distribution: temurin is the Eclipse Temurin build; java-version is quoted so YAML keeps it a string. cache: maven restores and saves Maven's local repository, so dependency downloads happen only when a pom.xml changes.
./mvnw -B -ntp verify
The Maven wrapper pins the Maven version for the project. -B is batch mode, -ntp hides the noisy "downloading…" lines. verify runs the lifecycle through compile, unit tests (Surefire), packaging, and integration tests (Failsafe, if used), so it checks more than test. Use mvn test if you only want unit tests.
if: always()
The upload step runs even if the build step failed, which is exactly when you need the reports.
upload-artifact
Stores files with the run; download them from the run's summary page. Set a short retention-days to save storage.

Mistake: mvnw: Permission denied

If the wrapper was committed from Windows, its executable bit may be missing and Linux refuses to run it. Fix once on your machine and commit: git update-index --chmod=+x mvnw && git commit -m "Make mvnw executable". Or run sh ./mvnw …, or call mvn directly (Maven is preinstalled on the runner).

Integration tests that need PostgreSQL

Two good options exist.

Option 1: Testcontainers (recommended). Your tests start a real PostgreSQL container themselves (see Spring Boot · Module 5). GitHub-hosted Ubuntu runners have Docker installed, so it works with no extra workflow lines, and the test behaves the same locally and in CI. This is the preferred approach.

Option 2: a service container. The workflow starts a database next to the job, and your tests connect to it through environment variables.

  integration:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    services:
      postgres:
        image: postgres:16
        env:
          POSTGRES_DB: shop_test
          POSTGRES_USER: test
          POSTGRES_PASSWORD: test
        ports:
          - 5432:5432
        options: >-
          --health-cmd "pg_isready -U test -d shop_test"
          --health-interval 5s
          --health-timeout 3s
          --health-retries 10
    env:
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL: jdbc:postgresql://localhost:5432/shop_test
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME: test
      SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD: test
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: "21"
          cache: maven
      - run: ./mvnw -B -ntp verify

Here the job runs directly on the runner, so the database is reached at localhost:5432 (the published port). If the job itself ran in a container (container: key), you would use the service name postgres instead — the same localhost rule as in Docker networks. The options health check makes GitHub wait until the database is ready before the steps start.

Service containerTestcontainers
Defined inThe workflow YAMLYour test code
Same setup locally?No: you must start a database yourselfYes: ./mvnw verify works anywhere with Docker
IsolationOne database shared by all tests in the jobA fresh container per test class or suite
Best forSimple pipelines, other languagesSpring Boot projects

A frontend job (React with Vite)

The React app is built and tested in its own job, in parallel with the backend. If the frontend sits in a frontend/ folder of the same repository, set the working directory.

  frontend:
    name: Frontend (React)
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    timeout-minutes: 10
    defaults:
      run:
        working-directory: frontend
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: "22"
          cache: npm
          cache-dependency-path: frontend/package-lock.json
      - run: npm ci                  # clean, exact install from package-lock.json
      - run: npm run lint --if-present
      - run: npm test --if-present
      - run: npm run build           # fails the job on TypeScript/Vite errors

npm ci installs exactly what package-lock.json says and fails if the lock file and package.json disagree — which is what you want in CI (npm install may modify the lock file). Choose the Node version your project uses. See Frontend · Module 5 for the React side.

Showing results

On each pull request the checks appear at the bottom: a green tick or a red cross per job, with a link to the log. Open a failed step, read the last lines of output (Maven prints the failing test name and the assertion), and download the surefire-reports artifact for the full stack traces. Always try to reproduce locally with the same command: ./mvnw -B verify.

Convention: one command that CI and developers both run

Keep logic in Maven (or a script in the repository), not in long YAML. The workflow becomes "checkout, install tools, run the command". Then "it fails in CI" can be reproduced on a laptop.

Edge cases and errors

  • Tests pass locally but fail in CI — usually time zones (the runner is UTC), file name case (Linux is case-sensitive), line endings, a service that exists only on your laptop, or tests that depend on order.
  • Flaky tests — fix or quarantine them; do not just re-run until green.
  • Cache never hits — the key is based on pom.xml; editing it changes the key and rebuilds the cache once. Caches are scoped per branch (a PR can read the default branch's cache).
  • Out of disk or memory on a hosted runner — a standard runner has limited RAM and disk; keep Testcontainers and test data modest.

Interview: "Write a CI pipeline for a Spring Boot app."

  • Trigger on PR and push to main; checkout, setup-java (Temurin 21, cache: maven), ./mvnw -B verify, upload reports with if: always().
  • Integration tests with Testcontainers or a Postgres service container; parallel frontend job with npm ci; least-privilege permissions and concurrency.

Building and pushing a Docker image to GHCR or Docker Hub

After tests pass, log in to the registry with docker/login-action, let docker/metadata-action compute the tags (git SHA, branch, semver), and build and push with docker/build-push-action using the GitHub Actions cache — to GHCR with the built-in GITHUB_TOKEN.

The pieces

  • docker/setup-buildx-action — sets up Buildx, the modern builder (enables caching and multi-platform builds).
  • docker/login-action — logs the runner in to a registry.
  • docker/metadata-action — turns the git context into image tags and labels, so you do not assemble strings by hand.
  • docker/build-push-action — runs docker build (using your Dockerfile) and optionally pushes.

Your multi-stage Dockerfile from Module 3 already builds the JAR inside the image, so this job needs no Maven step.

Publishing to GitHub Container Registry

name: Docker image

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
    tags: ["v*.*.*"]

env:
  REGISTRY: ghcr.io
  IMAGE_NAME: ${{ github.repository }}      # owner/repo

jobs:
  image:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    permissions:
      contents: read
      packages: write                       # needed to push to GHCR
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4

      - uses: docker/setup-buildx-action@v3

      - name: Log in to GHCR
        uses: docker/login-action@v3
        with:
          registry: ${{ env.REGISTRY }}
          username: ${{ github.actor }}
          password: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}

      - name: Compute tags and labels
        id: meta
        uses: docker/metadata-action@v5
        with:
          images: ${{ env.REGISTRY }}/${{ env.IMAGE_NAME }}
          tags: |
            type=sha
            type=semver,pattern={{version}}
            type=raw,value=latest,enable={{is_default_branch}}

      - name: Build and push
        uses: docker/build-push-action@v6
        with:
          context: .
          push: true
          tags: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.tags }}
          labels: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.labels }}
          cache-from: type=gha
          cache-to: type=gha,mode=max

What each part does

permissions: packages: write
Allows the GITHUB_TOKEN to publish packages. Without it you get denied: permission_denied: write_package.
login-action with GITHUB_TOKEN
No password to store: the automatic token of the run logs in to ghcr.io as the triggering user.
images: ghcr.io/owner/repo
The image name. GHCR names must be lowercase; the metadata action lowercases the repository name for you. If you build the name by hand with a mixed-case owner, convert it first.
tags: type=sha
Adds a tag from the commit SHA, in the short form sha-3f9c2ab (the prefix sha- is the default). Every build gets a unique tag.
type=semver,pattern={{version}}
On a tag push such as v1.2.0, adds the tag 1.2.0 (without the v).
type=raw,value=latest,enable=…
Also tags latest on the default branch, for convenience. Do not deploy from it.
cache-from / cache-to: type=gha
Stores Docker layer cache in the GitHub Actions cache, so unchanged layers (the dependency layer!) are not rebuilt in the next run. mode=max also caches intermediate stages, which matters for multi-stage builds.
steps.meta.outputs.tags
A step with an id exposes outputs that later steps read as ${{ steps.<id>.outputs.<name> }}.

For pull requests you usually want to build the image (to make sure the Dockerfile works) but not push it. Use push: ${{ github.event_name != 'pull_request' }} and run the workflow on PRs too.

Publishing to Docker Hub instead

Create an access token in Docker Hub (Account settings → Security → New access token) and add two repository secrets: DOCKERHUB_USERNAME and DOCKERHUB_TOKEN.

      - name: Log in to Docker Hub
        uses: docker/login-action@v3
        with:
          username: ${{ secrets.DOCKERHUB_USERNAME }}
          password: ${{ secrets.DOCKERHUB_TOKEN }}

      - id: meta
        uses: docker/metadata-action@v5
        with:
          images: ${{ secrets.DOCKERHUB_USERNAME }}/shop-api
GHCRDocker Hub
Credentials in the workflowBuilt-in GITHUB_TOKENYour own secrets (token)
Permissions neededpackages: writeNone in the workflow
Image nameghcr.io/owner/repouser/repo
Pulling on the serverNeeds docker login ghcr.io if privateNeeds login if private

Where it fits

Common layouts: one workflow with jobs test → image → deploy chained by needs (shown in the CD article), or separate workflows. Having the image job depend on the test job means a broken build is never published.

Rule: build once, deploy that exact image

The image you tested is the image you deploy: reference it by the SHA tag. Do not rebuild on the server or build a second time for production.

Mistake: denied errors when pushing

  • denied: permission_denied: write_package — the job lacks packages: write, or the package already exists and is linked to another repository, or the organisation restricts token permissions (Settings → Actions → General → Workflow permissions).
  • invalid reference format — uppercase letters in the image name, or an empty tag from a mistyped metadata step.
  • Docker Hub login failing — you used your account password instead of an access token, or the secret name is misspelled.

Edge cases

  • Pull requests from forks cannot push (no secrets, read-only token); build without pushing.
  • The runner is x86-64. To run on ARM servers (AWS Graviton) add docker/setup-qemu-action and platforms: linux/amd64,linux/arm64; the ARM build under emulation is slow.
  • A newly created GHCR package may be private; make it public or give your server a token with read:packages.

Interview: "How does your pipeline build and publish a Docker image?"

  • After tests: buildx, login (GHCR with GITHUB_TOKEN and packages: write), metadata for tags (SHA, semver), build-push with the GHA layer cache.
  • Immutable SHA tags; deploy by tag; never push from untrusted PRs.

Continuous deployment: SSH to a server, deploy hooks, environments and rollback

After the image is pushed, a deploy job logs in to your server over SSH and runs docker compose pull && docker compose up -d with the new tag — or calls your platform's deploy hook; an environment adds approvals, and rollback is just deploying the previous tag.

Option A: deploy over SSH to your own server

The server (for example an EC2 instance from Module 1) has Docker, a folder /opt/shop with the production compose.yaml and a .env file with real secrets. The compose file references the image through a variable:

services:
  api:
    image: ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:${IMAGE_TAG:-latest}
    env_file: .env
    ports:
      - "127.0.0.1:8080:8080"          # Nginx on the host proxies to this
    restart: unless-stopped
  # postgres, redis ... as in the Compose article

One-time setup on the server: create a deploy user, add the CI's public SSH key to ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, and log Docker in to GHCR with a token that can read packages (docker login ghcr.io) if the image is private. Generate a dedicated key pair on your laptop (ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -f deploy_key -C "github-actions") and put the private key into a GitHub secret. Never reuse your personal key.

Add these repository (or environment) secrets: SSH_HOST, SSH_USER, SSH_PRIVATE_KEY, and SSH_KNOWN_HOSTS (the output of ssh-keyscan -t ed25519 your.server.ip, which pins the server's identity so a man-in-the-middle cannot impersonate it).

The full CI → image → deploy workflow

This is the workflow for your Project 3: tests on every push and PR; on main only, push the image and deploy.

name: CI/CD

on:
  push:
    branches: [main]
  pull_request:
    branches: [main]

permissions:
  contents: read

env:
  REGISTRY: ghcr.io

jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-java@v4
        with:
          distribution: temurin
          java-version: "21"
          cache: maven
      - run: ./mvnw -B -ntp verify

  image:
    needs: test
    if: github.event_name == 'push' && github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    permissions:
      contents: read
      packages: write
    outputs:
      tag: ${{ steps.vars.outputs.tag }}
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - id: vars
        run: echo "tag=sha-${GITHUB_SHA::7}" >> "$GITHUB_OUTPUT"
      - uses: docker/setup-buildx-action@v3
      - uses: docker/login-action@v3
        with:
          registry: ${{ env.REGISTRY }}
          username: ${{ github.actor }}
          password: ${{ secrets.GITHUB_TOKEN }}
      - id: meta
        uses: docker/metadata-action@v5
        with:
          images: ${{ env.REGISTRY }}/${{ github.repository }}
          tags: |
            type=sha
      - uses: docker/build-push-action@v6
        with:
          context: .
          push: true
          tags: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.tags }}
          labels: ${{ steps.meta.outputs.labels }}
          cache-from: type=gha
          cache-to: type=gha,mode=max

  deploy:
    needs: image
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    environment:
      name: production                  # approvals and environment secrets apply here
      url: https://shop.example.com
    concurrency:
      group: deploy-production
      cancel-in-progress: false         # never cancel a deployment half way
    steps:
      - name: Prepare SSH
        env:
          SSH_PRIVATE_KEY: ${{ secrets.SSH_PRIVATE_KEY }}
          SSH_KNOWN_HOSTS: ${{ secrets.SSH_KNOWN_HOSTS }}
        run: |
          mkdir -p ~/.ssh
          printf '%s\n' "$SSH_PRIVATE_KEY" > ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
          chmod 600 ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
          printf '%s\n' "$SSH_KNOWN_HOSTS" > ~/.ssh/known_hosts

      - name: Deploy the new image
        env:
          TAG: ${{ needs.image.outputs.tag }}
          TARGET: ${{ secrets.SSH_USER }}@${{ secrets.SSH_HOST }}
        run: |
          ssh "$TARGET" "cd /opt/shop && \
            IMAGE_TAG=$TAG docker compose pull api && \
            IMAGE_TAG=$TAG docker compose up -d api && \
            docker image prune -f"

      - name: Smoke test
        run: |
          for i in 1 2 3 4 5 6; do
            if curl -fsS https://shop.example.com/actuator/health; then exit 0; fi
            sleep 10
          done
          echo "Health check failed" && exit 1

How it works

  • Job chain. test runs on PRs and pushes. image and deploy run only for pushes to main (the if on the image job skips it for PRs, and deploy is skipped because its need was skipped).
  • The tag handoff. The image job exposes tag as a job output (sha- plus the first seven characters of the commit). The metadata action's type=sha produces the same short form, so the deploy job pulls exactly the image just built.
  • SSH. The private key is written to a file with permission 600 (SSH refuses looser permissions). Secrets are passed in through env and used as shell variables, not pasted into the script text with ${{ }}, which keeps them out of the script source and avoids shell injection. known_hosts holds the server's pinned key, so we do not need StrictHostKeyChecking=no.
  • The remote command. docker compose pull api downloads the new image; up -d api recreates only the API container with it; databases keep running. docker image prune -f removes dangling old layers.
  • Smoke test. After deployment, call the health URL a few times. A failing smoke test marks the run red so someone notices, instead of "deployed" meaning "started".
  • You can use a ready-made action such as appleboy/ssh-action instead of raw ssh, but any third-party action that receives your key is part of your trusted code; read its README and pin a version.

Option B: deploy hooks and platform integrations

Platforms such as Render give each service a deploy hook: a secret URL that, when called with POST, starts a deploy. Store the URL as a secret and call it after the tests pass:

  deploy:
    needs: test
    if: github.ref == 'refs/heads/main'
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - name: Trigger deploy
        env:
          HOOK: ${{ secrets.RENDER_DEPLOY_HOOK_URL }}
        run: curl -fsS -X POST "$HOOK"

Railway, Fly.io, Heroku and others have a CLI or API token you call from a step, or can deploy straight from your GitHub repository on every push to main. If the platform builds from GitHub itself, make sure it waits for your CI checks (most have a "wait for CI" setting) so a failing build is not deployed. Read the platform's current documentation for exact commands; they change often. More on these platforms in Module 5.

Environments, approvals and rollback

A GitHub environment (repository Settings → Environments) such as staging or production can have protection rules: required reviewers (the job pauses until someone approves), a wait timer, and branch restrictions (only main may deploy). It also has its own secrets, so production credentials are available only to jobs that target the production environment. Required reviewers turn continuous deployment into continuous delivery for production: staging deploys automatically, production waits for a click.

Rollback. Because every image has an immutable tag, rolling back means deploying an older one. Add a manual workflow that takes the tag as input (workflow_dispatch with image_tag, see Triggers) and runs the same SSH step with IMAGE_TAG=${{ inputs.image_tag }}. Or revert the bad commit on main and let the pipeline deploy the revert. Database migrations make rollback harder: write backward-compatible migrations (add a column first, remove the old one in a later release).

Mistakes in deployment pipelines

  • Deploying from latest, so you cannot tell or roll back what is running.
  • Using StrictHostKeyChecking=no and a personal SSH key with full access.
  • Printing secrets with echo or set -x in the deploy script.
  • Deploying without a health check, or two deployments overlapping (use the concurrency group).
  • Putting production secrets at repository level where any workflow or branch can use them.

Edge cases

  • Replacing a container causes a few seconds of downtime. For zero downtime you need two instances behind a load balancer, or an orchestrator (Kubernetes, ECS) doing rolling updates.
  • If the server's firewall only allows SSH from your IP, GitHub-hosted runners (many changing IPs) cannot connect. Options: a self-hosted runner, a deploy hook/pull-based deploy, or SSM instead of SSH on AWS.
  • If the image on GHCR is private, the server needs credentials; a failed pull shows as unauthorized in the deploy log.

In real projects

Small teams use exactly this: test, build image, push, SSH or platform deploy, smoke test. Larger teams separate "build" from "deploy": CI publishes a versioned image, and a deployment tool (Argo CD, Spinnaker, AWS CodeDeploy) rolls it out. The idea — immutable artifact, promote the same artifact through environments — stays the same.

Interview: "How does your code get from a merge to production?"

  • Merge to main triggers: tests, Docker build and push (SHA tag), deploy job in the production environment (approval optional), SSH compose pull/up or deploy hook, then a smoke test.
  • Rollback: redeploy the previous tag via workflow_dispatch or revert the commit.

Secrets and variables in GitHub Actions, and OIDC instead of long-lived keys

Store passwords, tokens and keys as GitHub secrets (repository, environment or organization level) and read them as ${{ secrets.NAME }}; GitHub masks them in logs, but you must never echo them, forks do not receive them, and for AWS you should use OIDC to get short-lived credentials instead of stored access keys.

Secrets versus variables

A secret is encrypted configuration you must not reveal: tokens, SSH keys, passwords. A variable is plain configuration that is fine to see: a URL, a region, a Java version. Add them at Settings → Secrets and variables → Actions, or with the GitHub CLI (gh secret set SSH_HOST, gh variable set APP_URL).

LevelAvailable toUse for
RepositoryAll workflows in this repoRegistry tokens, shared test settings
EnvironmentOnly jobs that name that environment (and pass its rules)Production database URL, deploy key
OrganizationChosen repos in the organizationA token every project uses

The value is read in the workflow as ${{ secrets.NAME }} or ${{ vars.NAME }}. Environment-level values override repository-level ones with the same name, which lets staging and production use the same workflow with different credentials.

      - name: Call the API
        env:
          API_TOKEN: ${{ secrets.API_TOKEN }}     # pass through env ...
        run: curl -fsS -H "Authorization: Bearer $API_TOKEN" "${{ vars.APP_URL }}/ping"

Rules for handling secrets

  1. Never print them. GitHub masks exact secret values in logs, but masking is not perfect: if you transform a value (base64, split, URL-encode) the result is not masked. Do not echo secrets, do not run with set -x around them, and do not print environment dumps (env, printenv).
  2. Pass secrets through env and refer to $NAME in the shell, instead of pasting ${{ secrets.X }} into the script text. This avoids quoting bugs and script injection.
  3. Least privilege. Create tokens with the minimum scope (a deploy key that can only SSH as the deploy user; a registry token that can only push one repository). Prefer environment secrets for production so a feature branch cannot use them.
  4. Rotate. If a secret appears in a log, a commit or a screenshot, treat it as stolen: revoke it and create a new one.
  5. Do not commit secrets. Enable GitHub secret scanning and push protection; keep .env in .gitignore.
  6. Be careful with third-party actions. An action runs with access to the secrets you give it. Pin trusted actions to a version or commit SHA, and prefer official or well-known ones.

Pull requests from forks

For a pull request from a fork (someone else's copy of your repository), GitHub does not give the workflow your secrets and gives GITHUB_TOKEN read-only access. Otherwise any stranger could open a PR whose workflow prints your secrets. Consequences: tests that need a secret fail on fork PRs, and deploy or push steps must be restricted to pushes on your own branches (for example if: github.event_name == 'push'). Keep CI independent of secrets whenever you can; use Testcontainers and fake values for tests.

OIDC to AWS (brief)

Storing an AWS access key in GitHub secrets is a long-lived credential: if it leaks, it works until someone notices. OpenID Connect (OIDC) removes it. GitHub issues each workflow run a short-lived signed identity token; AWS is configured to trust tokens from GitHub for a specific repository (and branch or environment), and exchanges the token for temporary AWS credentials that expire in about an hour. Nothing secret is stored in GitHub.

permissions:
  id-token: write                 # lets the job request the OIDC token
  contents: read

jobs:
  deploy:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: aws-actions/configure-aws-credentials@v4
        with:
          role-to-assume: arn:aws:iam::123456789012:role/github-deploy
          aws-region: ap-south-1
      - run: aws sts get-caller-identity       # shows the assumed role

On the AWS side you create an IAM identity provider for token.actions.githubusercontent.com and an IAM role whose trust policy allows only your repository (and, better, only the main branch or the production environment). Follow the current instructions in the GitHub docs ("Configuring OpenID Connect in Amazon Web Services") and the README of the AWS action for the exact trust policy. The same idea works for Google Cloud and Azure.

Mistakes with secrets

  • A multi-line secret (SSH key) pasted with lost line breaks, or missing the final newline: the key fails with invalid format. Paste the whole file including the BEGIN and END lines.
  • Using a secret in a step's if: condition: secrets are not available there; copy it to an env variable first.
  • Giving the whole organisation's admin token to a workflow "to make it work".
  • Checking in a workflow that echoes ${{ secrets.X }} "just to debug".

Edge cases

  • Secrets are limited in size (a few tens of KB) and cannot be read back in the UI after saving; you can only overwrite them.
  • Dependabot-triggered runs use a separate set of secrets (Dependabot secrets), not the Actions ones.
  • Workflows triggered by pull_request_target run with secrets even for forks; combined with checking out the PR's code this is a known attack path. Avoid it unless you follow GitHub's hardening guide.

Interview: "How do you manage secrets in CI/CD?"

  • GitHub secrets at the right level (environment for production), passed via env, never echoed; masked in logs; not available to fork PRs.
  • Prefer OIDC and short-lived credentials over stored cloud keys; rotate on leak; scan for secrets.

Branch protection, required checks and the status badge

A branch protection rule (or ruleset) on main requires a pull request, passing status checks and reviews before merging and blocks force pushes — so broken code cannot reach main; add a workflow status badge to the README to show the build state.

Why protect main

CI is only useful if its result matters. Without protection, anyone can push straight to main, skip the tests, or force-push and rewrite history. A branch protection rule makes the rules enforceable by GitHub itself, for the whole team (including admins if you choose).

Setting it up

  1. Open the repository → Settings → Branches → Add branch protection rule (or Rules → Rulesets → new branch ruleset). Target the branch main.
  2. Tick Require a pull request before merging; set Required approvals to 1 or more. Optionally dismiss stale approvals when new commits arrive and require review from code owners (a CODEOWNERS file).
  3. Tick Require status checks to pass before merging and choose the checks by their job name (for example Backend (Maven) and Frontend (React)). A check appears in the list only after it has run at least once in the repository.
  4. Tick Require branches to be up to date before merging, so the checks ran against the latest main, not an old base.
  5. Tick Require conversation resolution, Require linear history if the team uses squash or rebase merges, and keep Do not allow force pushes and Do not allow deletions on.
  6. Consider Do not allow bypassing the above settings so admins follow the rules too.
SettingWhat it prevents
Require a pull requestDirect pushes to main
Required approvals / code ownersUnreviewed changes
Required status checksMerging when tests or build fail
Up-to-date branchesTwo PRs that pass alone but break together
No force pushes, no deletionRewriting or removing shared history
Linear historyMerge commits (squash or rebase only)

Branch protection rules are the classic feature; rulesets are the newer, more flexible system (several rulesets can apply, they can target many branches or tags by pattern, and they can be tested in "evaluate" mode first). The checkboxes are similar in both. Some features, such as protection on private repositories, depend on your GitHub plan.

What the developer sees

On a pull request, the merge button stays grey with messages like Required statuses must pass before merging until the CI jobs are green and the review is approved. If CI fails, the author pushes a fix to the same branch and the checks run again. Typical flow: branch → PR → CI runs → review → merge. See Git · Module 3 for the pull-request workflow.

The CI status badge

A badge is a small image in the README that shows whether the latest run on a branch passed. GitHub generates it for each workflow: Actions tab → the workflow → ⋯ → Create status badge, or build the URL yourself:

# Shop API

[![CI][badge]][workflow]

Spring Boot REST API for an online shop.

[badge]: https://github.com/asha/shop-api/actions/workflows/ci.yml/badge.svg?branch=main
[workflow]: https://github.com/asha/shop-api/actions/workflows/ci.yml

The pattern is https://github.com/OWNER/REPO/actions/workflows/FILE.yml/badge.svg?branch=main, wrapped in a link to the workflow page. Replace OWNER, REPO and FILE.yml with your own. A green badge in a project README tells recruiters at a glance that your code builds and tests run; a red one tells them to fix it.

Convention: protect main from day one, even on solo projects

Working through pull requests on your own repositories builds the habit, produces a visible history of checks, and lets you show the workflow to interviewers. Set the rule once CI has run.

Mistakes

  • Requiring a check by a name that is never reported (renamed job, or path-filtered workflow that did not run): the PR waits forever. Select the check from the list and keep job names stable.
  • Leaving admins able to bypass the rule, then bypassing it "just this once" before a demo.
  • Requiring approvals on a one-person repository: you can never merge. Use 0 approvals and keep the status checks.

Edge cases

  • A required check that is skipped (because of an if condition) counts as passed for a job, but a workflow that never starts leaves the check pending.
  • Merge queues (a GitHub feature for busy repositories) re-run checks on the combined result before merging; the workflow then also needs the merge_group trigger.
  • Rules apply to merges done in the UI and to pushes from Git; the deployment workflow's own commits (like version bumps) must go through a PR or be allowed explicitly.

Interview: "How do you make sure broken code never reaches main?"

  • Branch protection or rulesets: PR required, required status checks (the CI jobs), up-to-date branch, at least one review, no force pushes.
  • Developers work on feature branches; CI runs on the PR; merging is blocked until green.

Jenkins, the Jenkinsfile, and other CI/CD tools

Jenkins is a self-hosted automation server (controller plus agents, extended with plugins) whose pipelines are written in a Jenkinsfile; many Indian companies still run it, so you should be able to read a declarative Jenkinsfile even if you use GitHub Actions yourself.

What Jenkins is

Jenkins is an open-source automation server written in Java, first released in 2011 (it grew out of the Hudson project). You install and run it yourself — on a VM, in Docker or on Kubernetes — and connect it to your Git repository. It was the standard CI server of the 2010s, so large enterprises, banks and service companies have years of Jenkins jobs and pipelines in place. That is why interviews in India still ask about it.

Controller
The main Jenkins server: web UI, configuration, job scheduling, plugin management. (Older docs call it the "master".)
Agent
A machine (or container) that actually runs build steps; the controller sends work to agents (also called nodes). It lets one Jenkins serve many projects and platforms.
Plugin
Extension that adds features: Git, Pipeline, Docker, credentials, SonarQube, Slack. Jenkins has a very large plugin ecosystem — a strength, and a maintenance burden, because plugins need updates.
Job / pipeline
A unit of automation. A Pipeline job is defined as code.
Jenkinsfile
A text file in the repository root that defines the pipeline (Pipeline as Code).
Credentials
Jenkins' encrypted store for passwords, tokens and SSH keys, referenced by id.

A declarative Jenkinsfile

Jenkins pipelines are written in Groovy, in two styles: declarative (structured, recommended) and scripted (free-form Groovy). Here is a declarative pipeline with the stages you know: checkout, build, test, Docker build and push, deploy.

pipeline {
    agent any                               // run on any available agent

    tools {
        maven 'Maven-3.9'                   // names configured in Jenkins -> Tools
        jdk   'JDK-21'
    }

    environment {
        IMAGE = "registry.example.com/shop-api"
        TAG   = "${env.GIT_COMMIT.take(7)}"
    }

    options {
        timeout(time: 20, unit: 'MINUTES')
        buildDiscarder(logRotator(numToKeepStr: '20'))
    }

    stages {
        stage('Checkout') {
            steps {
                checkout scm                // the repository that holds this Jenkinsfile
            }
        }
        stage('Build') {
            steps {
                sh 'mvn -B -ntp clean package -DskipTests'
            }
        }
        stage('Test') {
            steps {
                sh 'mvn -B -ntp test'
            }
            post {
                always {
                    junit 'target/surefire-reports/*.xml'   // test results in the UI
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Docker build and push') {
            when { branch 'main' }
            steps {
                withCredentials([usernamePassword(credentialsId: 'registry-creds',
                        usernameVariable: 'REG_USER', passwordVariable: 'REG_PASS')]) {
                    sh '''
                        docker build -t $IMAGE:$TAG .
                        echo "$REG_PASS" | docker login registry.example.com \
                          -u "$REG_USER" --password-stdin
                        docker push $IMAGE:$TAG
                    '''
                }
            }
        }
        stage('Deploy') {
            when { branch 'main' }
            steps {
                sshagent(credentials: ['deploy-server-key']) {
                    sh '''
                        ssh -o StrictHostKeyChecking=yes deploy@server \
                          "cd /opt/shop && IMAGE_TAG=$TAG docker compose up -d api"
                    '''
                }
            }
        }
    }

    post {
        success { echo 'Build and deploy finished' }
        failure { echo 'Pipeline failed - notify the team' }   // mail or Slack step in real life
    }
}
pipeline, agent, stages, stage, steps
The fixed skeleton of the declarative syntax.
tools / environment
Select configured JDK and Maven; define variables for all stages.
when { branch 'main' }
Run a stage only for some branches (needs a multibranch pipeline).
post { always / success / failure }
Actions after the pipeline or a stage, like cleanup, reports and notifications.
withCredentials / sshagent
Inject stored credentials for a block of steps; the values are masked in the console log.
sh
Run a shell command on the agent; the same mvn and docker commands you already know.

Jenkins creates the pipeline job from the repository: a Multibranch Pipeline scans the repository, and builds every branch and pull request that has a Jenkinsfile. A GitHub webhook calls Jenkins on each push so the build starts immediately instead of polling.

Jenkins versus GitHub Actions versus GitLab CI

JenkinsGitHub ActionsGitLab CI
HostingYou run and maintain itManaged by GitHub (or self-hosted runners)GitLab.com or self-managed GitLab
Config fileJenkinsfile (Groovy)YAML in .github/workflows.gitlab-ci.yml (YAML)
Setup effortHigh: server, plugins, agents, upgrades, securityVery low: commit a fileLow if you use GitLab
EcosystemHuge plugin listMarketplace of actionsBuilt-in features, templates
FlexibilityVery high; any network, any hardwareHigh; limited by runners and minutesHigh
CostFree software; you pay for servers and admin timeFree tier of minutes, then per minuteFree tier, then plans
Common inLarge enterprises, on-premises, legacy pipelinesOpen source, startups, GitHub shopsTeams on GitLab

The ideas are identical: trigger, stages, steps, credentials, artifacts, approvals. If you understand one, you can read the others; only the syntax differs.

Other tools in one line each

  • GitLab CI/CD — pipelines built into GitLab, defined in .gitlab-ci.yml.
  • Azure DevOps Pipelines — Microsoft's CI/CD, common in companies on Azure and .NET.
  • CircleCI — hosted CI service with YAML config, popular with startups.
  • AWS CodePipeline / CodeBuild — AWS-native build and release services.
  • Argo CD, Spinnaker — deployment tools for Kubernetes and multi-cloud releases.

In real projects

At a service company you may inherit a Jenkins server with dozens of pipelines: read the Jenkinsfile, open the console output of a failed build, check the credentials and agents. Many of these teams are slowly migrating to GitHub Actions or GitLab CI because Jenkins needs ongoing care (plugin updates, security patches, agent capacity).

Edge cases and problems

  • An outdated plugin or an exposed Jenkins UI is a frequent security problem; keep Jenkins updated, put it behind HTTPS and authentication, and restrict who can configure jobs.
  • A build that works on one agent and fails on another usually depends on tools installed only on that machine; use Docker agents or the tools block to make it reproducible.
  • "Scripted" pipelines use node { ... } blocks and plain Groovy; they look different from the declarative form but do the same job.

Interview: "What is Jenkins? Jenkins versus GitHub Actions?"

  • A self-hosted automation server: controller schedules, agents execute, plugins extend; pipelines as code in a declarative Jenkinsfile with stages and steps.
  • Jenkins: maximum control, more maintenance. Actions: hosted, YAML, tight GitHub integration, minimal setup. The concepts are the same.

CI/CD best practices

Keep pipelines fast, fail early, cache dependencies, keep main green, make small pull requests, build reproducibly, and use the same commands locally and in CI.

The practices

  1. Fast feedback. Aim for CI under about 10 minutes. Slow pipelines make people stop waiting, batch their changes and ignore failures. Measure the run time and attack the slowest step.
  2. Fail fast. Put the cheapest, most likely-to-fail checks first (compile, lint, unit tests) and the slow ones (integration tests, image build, scans) after them or in parallel jobs. Use needs so expensive jobs do not run when cheap ones failed.
  3. Cache dependencies and layers. cache: maven or cache: npm, and Docker layer cache with type=gha. Order the Dockerfile so dependency layers are reused.
  4. Keep main green. Protect the branch; require passing checks; fix or revert a broken main immediately; do not merge on red.
  5. Small pull requests. A 100-line PR is reviewed in minutes and rarely breaks the build; a 3,000-line PR hides bugs and conflicts. Merge often.
  6. Reproducible builds. Pin tool versions (Maven wrapper, Java 21, locked package-lock.json with npm ci, tagged base images), avoid "latest", and build the artifact once and promote the same one through environments.
  7. Same commands locally and in CI. The workflow should call ./mvnw verify, npm ci && npm run build or a script in the repository. Developers can then reproduce any CI failure on their laptop.
  8. Test reliably. Remove flaky tests or fix their root cause (time, ordering, shared state, sleeps). Prefer Testcontainers to a shared test database.
  9. Least privilege and safe secrets. Minimal permissions, environment secrets for production, OIDC instead of stored keys, scanning for leaked secrets.
  10. Make failures easy to read. Upload test reports, name jobs and steps clearly, set timeouts, and notify the team (GitHub emails the PR author; add Slack if the team uses it).
  11. Deploy small and often, with a way back. Immutable image tags, health checks and smoke tests after deploy, a manual rollback workflow, and backward-compatible database migrations.
  12. Keep workflows maintainable. Reuse with reusable workflows or composite actions, let Dependabot update action versions, and delete old branches and artifacts.

A quick review checklist for any pipeline

QuestionGood answer
How long does a PR check take?Under ~10 minutes, cached
Can I run the same build on my laptop?Yes, one command
What stops a failing PR from merging?Required status checks
Which exact artifact is in production?An immutable tag (git SHA)
Where are the secrets, and who can read them?Environment secrets, least privilege
How do I roll back?Redeploy the previous tag, tested

Mistake: ignoring a flaky red build

"Just re-run it" teaches the team that red does not mean broken. Then a real failure is merged. Track flaky tests, fix them, or move them out of the required path.

Tip: pin actions and let Dependabot update them

Add .github/dependabot.yml with the github-actions ecosystem (and maven, npm, docker) so version bumps arrive as pull requests that your CI checks. Look at each action's README for the latest major when you write a workflow by hand.

Interview: "How would you improve a slow, unreliable pipeline?"

  • Measure; cache Maven/npm and Docker layers; parallelise jobs; run cheap checks first; trim the build context; use path filters carefully.
  • Fix flaky tests, pin versions, add timeouts and good reports, keep main protected and green.

Interview questions — CI/CD with GitHub Actions

What is CI/CD? Explain CI, continuous delivery and continuous deployment.

CI builds and tests every change automatically on a clean machine so the main branch always works. Continuous delivery also produces a release-ready artifact and deploys it to staging; production release is a manual approval. Continuous deployment sends every green change to production with no manual step. Together they give fast feedback, small releases and repeatable deployments.

Describe the structure of a GitHub Actions workflow.

A YAML file in .github/workflows. on lists the triggering events; jobs contains named jobs, each with runs-on (the runner) and steps. A step is either run (a shell command) or uses (a reusable action with with inputs). Jobs run in parallel unless ordered with needs; each runs on its own fresh runner.

GitHub-hosted versus self-hosted runners?

Hosted runners are fresh VMs managed by GitHub (ubuntu-latest): no maintenance, clean every time, but limited resources and no access to your private network. Self-hosted runners are your own machines running the runner agent: custom hardware, private network access and no per-minute fee, but you must secure, update and clean them, and never use them for untrusted fork PRs on public repositories.

How do jobs share data, and how do you control their order?

needs orders jobs and skips a job if a dependency failed. Because each job has its own machine, files are shared with artifacts (upload-artifact, download-artifact), small values with job outputs, and built images through a registry.

What triggers can you use? How do you run CI only for pull requests and main?

on: push: branches: [main] and pull_request: branches: [main]. Other events: workflow_dispatch (manual), schedule (cron in UTC), tag pushes and releases, workflow_call for reusable workflows. Filters paths, branches and tags narrow when it runs.

How do you speed up a Maven build in GitHub Actions?

Use cache: maven in actions/setup-java (keyed on the POMs), run with -B -ntp, run unit and integration tests in parallel jobs, cancel outdated runs with a concurrency group, and avoid building twice. Docker layer caching with type=gha speeds up the image build.

How do you handle secrets in GitHub Actions? What about pull requests from forks?

Store them as repository, environment or organization secrets and read ${{ secrets.NAME }}, passing them through env. They are masked in logs but never echoed. Forks get no secrets and a read-only token, so deploy and push steps must not run for them. Use environment secrets with required reviewers for production, and OIDC for cloud access.

What is OIDC in GitHub Actions and why is it better than stored AWS keys?

GitHub issues each run a short-lived signed token (needs permissions: id-token: write). AWS trusts GitHub as an identity provider and, for a role limited to your repository or branch, exchanges the token for temporary credentials. No long-lived access key sits in GitHub, so there is nothing to leak or rotate.

How do you build and push a Docker image from a workflow?

After tests: docker/setup-buildx-action, docker/login-action (GHCR using GITHUB_TOKEN with packages: write, or Docker Hub with a token secret), docker/metadata-action for tags (git SHA, semver), and docker/build-push-action with push: true and the GHA cache. Deploy by the SHA tag, not latest.

How do you deploy automatically to your own server?

A deploy job (after tests and image push, on main only) connects over SSH with a dedicated key stored as a secret and a pinned known_hosts, then runs IMAGE_TAG=… docker compose pull && docker compose up -d on the server and finishes with a health check. Alternatively call a platform deploy hook URL (Render) or its CLI.

What are GitHub environments and how do they help?

Named deployment targets (staging, production) with their own secrets and protection rules: required reviewers, wait timers, and allowed branches. A job with environment: production pauses for approval and is the only one that can read the production secrets. They also record deployment history.

How do you roll back a bad deployment?

Because every image has an immutable tag, redeploy the previous tag using a manual workflow_dispatch workflow that takes the tag as input, or revert the commit and let the pipeline redeploy. Keep database migrations backward-compatible so the old version still works with the new schema.

How do you stop broken code from being merged into main?

Branch protection rules or rulesets on main: require a pull request, required status checks (the CI jobs), up-to-date branches and at least one review; disallow force pushes and deletion. The merge button stays disabled until checks pass. Add a CI badge to the README for visibility.

What is Jenkins, and how does it differ from GitHub Actions?

Jenkins is a self-hosted automation server: a controller schedules builds on agents and plugins add features; pipelines are Groovy Jenkinsfiles with stages and steps. You maintain the server, plugins and security. GitHub Actions is hosted, YAML-based and integrated with GitHub, with almost no setup. Concepts (trigger, stages, credentials, artifacts) are the same.

Tests pass on your laptop but fail in CI. What do you check?

Java version and OS differences (Linux is case-sensitive), time zone (runner is UTC), missing services or environment variables, tests depending on order or on files that exist only locally, line endings, and flaky timing. Download the test report artifact, read the first failure, and reproduce with the exact CI command and Java version.

Cheat sheet — CI/CD with GitHub Actions

  • CI / delivery / deployment auto build+test / releasable + manual prod / every green change to prod
  • Workflow location .github/workflows/name.yml in the repository root
  • Skeleton name, on, permissions, jobs.<id>.runs-on, steps
  • run vs uses shell command vs reusable action (owner/repo@v4 with with:)
  • Runner ubuntu-latest (hosted, fresh VM) or self-hosted (your machine)
  • Job order parallel by default; needs: [a, b] to wait; if: to condition
  • Triggers push, pull_request (+ branches, paths), workflow_dispatch, schedule (UTC cron), tags, workflow_call
  • Contexts ${{ github.sha }}, github.ref_name, github.actor, secrets.X, vars.X, matrix.x
  • Java CI core checkout@v4 → setup-java@v4 (temurin, 21, cache: maven) → ./mvnw -B -ntp verify
  • Reports upload-artifact with if: always() to keep surefire reports
  • DB in CI Testcontainers (Docker is on the runner) or services: postgres with a health check
  • Frontend CI setup-node with cache: npm; npm ci, npm test, npm run build
  • Matrix strategy.matrix.java: [17, 21] runs a job per value
  • Concurrency group + cancel-in-progress: true for CI; false for deploys
  • Token permissions permissions: contents: read by default; packages: write to push to GHCR
  • Docker publish setup-buildx, login (GHCR with GITHUB_TOKEN), metadata (type=sha), build-push (cache-from: type=gha)
  • Image names GHCR ghcr.io/owner/repo (lowercase), Docker Hub user/repo
  • SSH deploy dedicated key in secrets, pinned known_hosts, docker compose pull && up -d, health check
  • Deploy hook curl -fsS -X POST "$HOOK" with the URL stored as a secret
  • Environments staging/production with required reviewers, wait timer, own secrets
  • Secrets repo / environment / organization; pass via env; never echo; forks get none
  • OIDC id-token: write + configure-aws-credentials with role-to-assume; no stored keys
  • Branch protection PR required, required status checks, up-to-date branch, reviews, no force push
  • Badge https://github.com/OWNER/REPO/actions/workflows/ci.yml/badge.svg?branch=main
  • Jenkins self-hosted; controller + agents + plugins; declarative Jenkinsfile (pipeline, agent, stages, steps, post)
  • Rollback redeploy the previous immutable tag (workflow_dispatch with image_tag)
  • Best practices fast, fail early, cache, keep main green, small PRs, reproducible, same commands locally

Practice — CI/CD with GitHub Actions

  1. Hello workflow. In a test repository add .github/workflows/hello.yml that prints the commit SHA, branch name and the Java version. Push and read the run in the Actions tab. Done when you can point to the event, job, step and runner in the log.
  2. CI on every push and PR. In Project 2 add ci.yml that runs on push to main and on pull requests: checkout, Temurin 21 with Maven cache, ./mvnw -B verify (this runs mvn test and more), and upload the surefire reports as an artifact. Done when a pull request shows a green check, and a deliberately broken test makes it red with a downloadable report.
  3. Make it faster. Compare the run time of the first and second runs to see the Maven cache work. Add a concurrency group and timeout-minutes. Done when a push cancels the older run on the same branch.
  4. Test on two Java versions and add the frontend. Add a matrix for Java 17 and 21 (if your code supports both), and a separate frontend job with npm ci and npm run build. Done when both jobs run in parallel.
  5. Protect main and add the badge. Create a branch protection rule (or ruleset) that requires a pull request and your CI checks and blocks force pushes. Add the CI status badge to the top of the README. Done when you cannot push to main directly, a failing PR cannot be merged, and the badge shows the state.
  6. Dockerize and publish. Add the multi-stage Dockerfile (Module 3) and a job that builds and pushes the image to GHCR on pushes to main, tagged with the short SHA. Done when the package appears under your repository's Packages and docker pull ghcr.io/you/app:sha-… works.
  7. CD on merge to main. Prepare a server (an EC2 instance from Module 1/5 or any VPS) with Docker and the production compose.yaml. Create a dedicated SSH key, store the secrets, add a deploy job in the production environment that pulls the new tag, runs docker compose up -d, and ends with a smoke test. Done when merging a pull request deploys the new version without you touching the server.
  8. Approval and rollback. Add a required reviewer to the production environment, and write a workflow_dispatch workflow that deploys a given image tag. Deploy a "bad" change, then roll back to the previous tag. Done when you can restore the previous version in under five minutes.
  9. Document it. In the README add the CI badge and a "Deployment" section: how the pipeline works (a small diagram or list), the secrets and variables it needs (names only), how to roll back, and how to run the same steps locally. Done when a stranger could set up the pipeline from your README.
  10. Read a Jenkinsfile (roadmap goal). Translate your GitHub Actions pipeline into a declarative Jenkinsfile on paper (stages: checkout, build, test, docker build, deploy). Optionally run Jenkins in Docker and execute it. Done when you can explain every GitHub Actions concept's Jenkins equivalent, and a push to main in your own project runs your tests and deploys automatically.
5

Module 5·About 1 weekMust

Cloud and deployment

16 topics · about 1.5 h of reading

This module takes the application you built, tested and containerised in the earlier modules and puts it on the public internet. You will learn what "the cloud" really is (rented computers, billed by use), the core AWS services a backend developer needs (EC2, RDS, S3, IAM), and the simpler platforms (Render, Railway, Vercel, Netlify) that hide most of the work. You will also learn how to point your own domain at the app and serve it over HTTPS.

The first rule of the cloud is not technical: set up account safety and a billing alert before you create anything. A forgotten server or a leaked access key is the most common way students get a surprise bill. This module starts with that, then builds up to a full deployment runbook with exact commands. It connects to Linux (SSH, systemd), Networking (DNS, TLS, Nginx), Docker and CI/CD.

  • Explain IaaS, PaaS and SaaS, regions and availability zones, and the shared-responsibility model.
  • Protect an AWS account (MFA, non-root user, least privilege) and set a budget alert on day one.
  • Launch an EC2 server, SSH in, and deploy a Spring Boot container behind Nginx with HTTPS.
  • Use RDS for PostgreSQL and S3 (including presigned URLs) safely, with IAM roles instead of keys.
  • Deploy a backend to Render or Railway and a React app to Vercel or Netlify.
  • Connect a domain with DNS records, choose a deployment strategy, and follow a production checklist.

Cloud basics: IaaS, PaaS, SaaS and pay-as-you-go

The cloud means renting computing (servers, storage, databases, networks) from a provider over the internet and paying only for what you use; IaaS rents raw machines, PaaS rents a ready platform for your code, SaaS rents finished software.

What the cloud is

Before the cloud, a company that wanted to run a website bought servers, put them in a room, hired people to look after them, and paid for the peak load all year. In the cloud, a provider such as Amazon, Microsoft or Google owns huge data centres. You rent a slice of them through a web console or an API, in minutes, and stop paying when you delete the slice.

Every provider sells the same building blocks:

Compute
Machines that run your code: virtual servers (EC2), containers (ECS, Kubernetes) or functions (Lambda).
Storage
Object storage for files (S3), block disks attached to a server (EBS), file shares.
Managed databases
A database the provider installs, backs up and patches for you (RDS for PostgreSQL/MySQL, DynamoDB).
Networking
Private networks (VPC), load balancers, DNS, firewalls (security groups).
Identity
Users, roles and permissions: who may do what (IAM).

IaaS vs PaaS vs SaaS

The three service models differ in how much the provider manages for you. Think of getting a pizza: IaaS is buying the flour and using their oven, PaaS is ordering the base and adding your own toppings, SaaS is eating the finished pizza.

ModelYou manageProvider managesExamples
IaaS (Infrastructure as a Service)OS, runtime, patches, app, data, firewall rulesHardware, network, virtualisationAWS EC2, Azure Virtual Machines, Google Compute Engine
PaaS (Platform as a Service)Your code (or container) and its settingsOS, runtime, scaling, TLS, deploysRender, Railway, Heroku, Elastic Beanstalk, Vercel, Netlify
SaaS (Software as a Service)Your data and usersEverything elseGmail, Slack, Jira, Zoho, Salesforce, GitHub

There is also FaaS (Function as a Service, "serverless"): you upload a function and pay per call, for example AWS Lambda. As a backend developer you mostly choose between IaaS and PaaS for your own apps, and use SaaS tools (GitHub, Jira, Slack) every day.

The trade-off is always control versus effort. On IaaS you can install anything and tune everything, but you must patch Linux, configure Nginx and renew certificates. On PaaS you push code and get a URL, but you accept the platform's rules, and costs can be higher at scale. This module teaches both, because interviews ask about EC2 and real jobs use both.

Pay-as-you-go

Cloud billing is pay-as-you-go: there is no purchase, you are metered. Servers are billed for the time they run, storage for the gigabytes kept, data leaving the provider for the gigabytes sent out, and many services per request. Good effects: no big upfront cost, you can try an idea for a few hours, and you can scale up for a sale and back down after. Bad effect: a mistake costs money immediately. A server you forget about keeps billing, and a loop that calls a paid API can bill fast. That is why the next two articles are about account safety and billing alerts.

Providers also offer cheaper long-term options, for example committing to steady usage for one or three years (Savings Plans, Reserved Instances) or using spare capacity that can be taken back (Spot). You do not need them while learning; just know the names. Never trust a price you read in an old blog post: always check the provider's current pricing page.

The shared-responsibility model

Security in the cloud is split. The provider is responsible for the security of the cloud: buildings, hardware, network, and the virtualisation layer. You are responsible for security in the cloud: your data, your accounts and permissions, your firewall rules, and, depending on the service, your operating system and application.

ServiceProvider handlesYou handle
EC2 (IaaS)Hardware, hypervisorOS patches, Nginx/Docker, app, security groups, SSH keys, backups
RDS (managed)OS and database patching, backups, failoverDatabase users and passwords, network access, schema, queries
S3Durability, hardwareBucket policies, public-access settings, what you upload
Render / Vercel (PaaS)OS, runtime, TLS, scalingYour code, secrets, dependencies, access to your account

The practical meaning: when a company's S3 bucket leaks customer data, it is almost always because the customer made it public, not because AWS was hacked.

The big three, in one line each

  • AWS (Amazon Web Services): the oldest and largest, the widest range of services and the most job listings; this module uses it.
  • Microsoft Azure: strong in enterprises that already use Microsoft products (Windows, Active Directory, .NET), also popular for Java.
  • Google Cloud (GCP): strong in data, machine learning and Kubernetes, which Google created.

The ideas (virtual machine, object storage, managed database, IAM, load balancer) are the same everywhere, with different names. Learn them on AWS and the others become a vocabulary exercise.

Mistake: "the cloud is secure by default, so I need not worry"

Providers secure the data centre, not your configuration. A public bucket, an SSH port open to the whole internet, or an access key in a public GitHub repository are your mistakes, and bots find them within minutes.

Interview: "Explain IaaS, PaaS and SaaS with examples."

  • IaaS: raw machines (EC2); you manage OS upwards. PaaS: you bring code (Render, Elastic Beanstalk). SaaS: finished software (Gmail, Jira).
  • Add the trade-off (control versus effort) and the shared-responsibility model.

Regions and availability zones (Mumbai and Hyderabad)

A region is a geographic area with several isolated availability zones; you pick the region nearest your users (Mumbai ap-south-1 or Hyderabad ap-south-2 for India) and spread across zones to survive a data-centre failure.

Regions

An AWS region is a physical area of the world where AWS runs a cluster of data centres, such as Mumbai or Frankfurt. Each region has a name and a code. For India you will use:

RegionCodeNotes
Asia Pacific (Mumbai)ap-south-1The older, most complete Indian region
Asia Pacific (Hyderabad)ap-south-2Newer; may need to be enabled in your account, and some services may be missing, so check
US East (N. Virginia)us-east-1The oldest and largest; several global features are managed from here

Regions are independent. A server, a database or a bucket you create lives in one region. Switching the region in the top-right menu of the console shows a different, empty set of resources. This causes the classic panic "my server has disappeared": you simply looked at the wrong region. Some services are global (IAM users and roles, Route 53 DNS, CloudFront), so they do not belong to a region.

How to choose a region
