  • Latency. Data travels at finite speed; a user in Pune is faster served from Mumbai than from Virginia. Choose the region closest to most users.
  • Rules about data. Some companies and sectors (banking, health, government) require data to stay in India. Check the rules that apply to you.
  • Service availability. New services and instance types arrive first in a few big regions. Check that the services you need exist in your region.
  • Price. The same service can cost differently in different regions. Check the current pricing page.
  • Keep related things together. Your EC2 server, RDS database and S3 bucket should be in the same region. Cross-region traffic is slower and is billed as data transfer.

Availability zones

A region is split into several availability zones (AZs), normally three or more in the bigger regions. An AZ is one or more data centres with its own power, cooling and network, close enough to others in the region for very low latency, but separate enough that a fire or power cut in one does not take down the others. Zone names look like ap-south-1a, ap-south-1b, ap-south-1c. (AWS maps these letters to physical zones differently per account, so your "1a" may be someone else's "1b"; the zone ID such as aps1-az1 is the stable name.)

Why zones matter for your design

One server in one zone is a single point of failure: if that zone has a problem, your app is down. The standard fix is to run at least two copies in different zones behind a load balancer (ELB/ALB), and to turn on Multi-AZ for the database (RDS keeps a standby copy in another zone and fails over automatically). Subnets (slices of your private network) are tied to one AZ, so "use two AZs" means "create subnets in two zones".

For learning and for small apps, one EC2 instance in one zone is fine, and it is cheaper. For a business system that must stay up, use at least two zones. Even then, a whole-region outage is rare but possible; multi-region designs exist and cost much more, so they are used only when the business needs them.

Rule: create all related resources in one region

Pick the region first, keep the console on it, and set the same region in the AWS CLI (aws configure) and in your code (for example Region.AP_SOUTH_1). A mismatch gives errors such as "bucket not found" or an empty list.

Edge cases

  • An EBS disk can only be attached to a server in the same AZ.
  • An S3 bucket name is global across all of AWS, but the bucket's data lives in one region.
  • Some regions are "opt-in": you must enable them in your account settings before use. If Hyderabad is not in your region list, this is why.

Interview: "What is the difference between a region and an availability zone?"

  • Region = a geographic area; AZ = an isolated group of data centres inside it. Zones in a region are connected by fast links.
  • Use multiple AZs for high availability (load balancer + Multi-AZ database); use the region closest to users and legal needs.

AWS account safety first: root MFA, daily-use users, least privilege and keys

Protect the root user with MFA and never use it daily; do your work as an IAM Identity Center or IAM user with only the permissions you need, and never put access keys in code, Git or an image.

Why this comes before any server

Bots scan GitHub for AWS keys all day. A leaked key can be used within minutes to start expensive servers for crypto-mining, and you receive the bill. Spending an hour on the steps below prevents that.

Step 1: secure the root user

When you create an AWS account, you sign up with an email and password. That identity is the root user. It can do everything, including closing the account and changing billing, and its permissions cannot be limited. So:

  1. Use a strong, unique password from a password manager, and a mailbox you control.
  2. Turn on MFA (multi-factor authentication) for the root user: in the console, open your account menu → Security credentials → Assign MFA device. Use an authenticator app or a passkey/security key. Save recovery details safely.
  3. Do not create access keys for the root user. If any exist, delete them.
  4. Use the root user only for the few tasks that need it (changing the account email, closing the account, some billing settings). Sign out afterwards.

Step 2: create an identity for daily work

You have two good options. Check the current AWS "Getting started" guidance, because the console changes.

  • IAM Identity Center (AWS's recommended way for people): you create a user, assign a permission set, and sign in through a portal URL. It gives temporary credentials, so there are no long-lived keys on your laptop. Best choice when you can use it.
  • An IAM user (simpler, fine for learning): create a user such as asha-admin, tick console access, enable MFA for it too, and attach permissions through a group. For a personal account you may attach the managed policy AdministratorAccess to a group, but prefer narrower policies as soon as you know what you need. See IAM.

From now on, sign in with this identity, not root. The sign-in URL for IAM users contains your account ID or alias; bookmark it.

Step 3: least privilege

The principle of least privilege means each user, application and server gets only the permissions it needs for its job. An app that uploads photos needs s3:PutObject on one bucket, not AdministratorAccess. If the app is ever compromised, the damage is limited to that bucket.

Step 4: handle access keys carefully

An access key is a pair (access key ID plus secret access key) that lets a program or the AWS CLI act as a user. It is a password in another form.

Rules for access keys

  • Never commit keys to Git, even in a private repository, and never put them in a Dockerfile, an image, a React app (everything in frontend code is public), a screenshot or a chat message.
  • Prefer no keys at all: use IAM Identity Center on your laptop and an instance role on EC2.
  • If you must use keys, store them with aws configure (it writes ~/.aws/credentials outside any repository), give the user minimal permissions, and rotate or delete unused keys.
  • Add .env and credential files to .gitignore before the first commit.

Check which identity the CLI is using at any time:

$ aws sts get-caller-identity
{
    "UserId": "AIDAEXAMPLEUSERID",
    "Account": "123456789012",
    "Arn": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/asha-admin"
}

If a key leaks

  1. Deactivate and then delete the key immediately (IAM → Users → Security credentials).
  2. Look for anything you did not create: instances in every region, new IAM users, new keys. CloudTrail (the audit log) shows what the key did.
  3. Delete unknown resources, then check the billing page. AWS often emails you about exposed keys; follow its instructions and contact AWS Support if you see charges you did not make.
  4. Remove the secret from Git history (rotating the key matters more than rewriting history, because anyone may already have copied it).

The free tier

AWS has offered a free tier for new accounts: some services free up to a limit for a period, some free always, and some as trials. The terms, limits, durations and even the structure of the programme have changed over the years, and they differ by account creation date. So do not rely on a number from a blog or this page. Open the current "AWS Free Tier" page, read the exact terms for your account, and note which services and instance types qualify. "Free tier" never means "unlimited": going over the limit, using a non-eligible instance type, or running in a way the tier does not cover creates a normal bill.

Convention: one account for learning, tags on everything

Use a separate account for experiments. Give resources a tag such as project=shop so you can find and delete them later. Real companies use separate accounts for development and production.

Interview: "How do you secure a new AWS account?"

  • MFA on root, no root keys, root unused; IAM Identity Center or IAM users with groups and least privilege; roles instead of keys for servers; billing alerts; CloudTrail on.

Billing alerts, AWS Budgets and the surprise-cost list

Create a monthly budget with email alerts on day one, learn to read the bill by service and region, and know the usual surprise costs: running instances, forgotten disks, public IPs, NAT gateways, data transfer and idle databases.

Create a budget, step by step

AWS Budgets watches your spending (actual and forecast) against a number you choose and emails you at thresholds. The console layout changes now and then, so treat the labels below as a guide.

  1. Sign in as the root user (or an admin). By default, IAM users cannot see billing pages. As root, open Account → "IAM user and role access to Billing information" and activate it if you want your admin user to see billing.
  2. Open Billing and Cost Management → Billing preferences and turn on the email alerts offered there, for example free-tier usage alerts, and set the email addresses.
  3. Open Budgets → Create budget. For a first budget choose a template such as "Zero spend budget" (alerts as soon as you spend anything) or "Monthly cost budget" (you enter an amount).
  4. Enter a name, the monthly amount you are comfortable with, and your email address. Create the budget.
  5. Optionally, make a second, customised cost budget with several alerts: for example at 50% and 80% of the amount on actual spend, and at 100% on forecasted spend (the forecast warns you before you cross the line).
  6. Confirm the email subscription if AWS sends a confirmation message, then check that alert emails do not go to spam.
  7. Open Cost Explorer and enable it (it may take a day to fill). It shows cost by service, by region and over time.

Limits of budget alerts

  • A budget alerts, it does not stop spending. (Advanced "budget actions" can apply a restrictive policy automatically, but they need setup and care.)
  • Billing data lags by hours, so an alert can arrive after the cost happened. Alerts are a smoke detector, not a fire brigade; the real protection is cleaning up when you finish.
  • Look into Cost Anomaly Detection as well; it flags unusual spikes by service.

Reading the bill

Open Billing → Bills to see the current month, grouped by service, with a view by region. The region view quickly shows the mistake "I created a server in a region I forgot". Make it a weekly habit for the first months: two minutes, once a week.

The usual surprise costs

SurpriseWhy it happensWhat to do
Running EC2 instancesThey bill while running, even when idleStop or terminate when you finish; check every region
Unattached EBS volumes and old snapshotsDisks bill for their size even when no server uses them; terminating a server may not delete extra volumesDelete unused volumes and snapshots; check "delete on termination"
Elastic IPs and public IPv4 addressesAWS charges for public IPv4 addresses, and an Elastic IP not attached to a running instance is a classic wasteRelease IPs you do not use; check the current IPv4 pricing
NAT gatewaysBilled per hour plus per GB processed, which is a common shock in "private subnet" tutorialsAvoid for learning; delete after labs
Data transferData going out to the internet, or between regions and zones, is billedKeep resources in one region; use CloudFront for heavy downloads
RDS left runningA database bills all day, plus storage and backups; stopped RDS instances restart automatically after a number of daysDelete test databases (take a final snapshot only if needed)
Load balancersBill per hour while they exist, even with no trafficDelete after experiments
Big S3 buckets, logsStorage and requests add up; CloudWatch logs grow without limit by defaultLifecycle rules; set log retention

Do not memorise prices. Memorise the habit: everything that exists in the console may be billed, so when a lab ends, delete what you created.

A cleanup routine after every lab

  1. Terminate EC2 instances you do not need (stopping keeps the disk, which is still billed).
  2. Delete the leftover EBS volumes and snapshots; release Elastic IPs.
  3. Delete RDS instances, load balancers, NAT gateways.
  4. Empty and delete S3 buckets used for tests.
  5. Look at Bills → by region next day to confirm nothing is still running.

Tip: use tags and a "delete me" day

Tag lab resources with purpose=learning. When you search resources by tag, you can delete them all together. For repeatable labs, write them as Terraform (Module 6) and run terraform destroy.

Mistake: setting up alerts to an email you never read

An alert nobody sees is useless. Use an address you check daily, add a second recipient, and test by creating a tiny "zero spend" budget.

Interview: "How do you keep cloud costs under control?"

  • Budgets and alerts, Cost Explorer, tags, right-sizing, shutting down non-production at night, deleting unused resources, committing to Savings Plans for steady load, and reviewing the bill regularly.

EC2: virtual servers, instance types, keys, security groups and disks

EC2 rents you a Linux virtual machine: you choose an AMI (the OS image), an instance type (CPU and RAM), a key pair for SSH, a security group (the firewall) and an EBS disk, then pay while it runs.

What EC2 is

EC2 (Elastic Compute Cloud) is AWS's virtual-server service, the main IaaS product. An instance is one virtual machine. You get a normal Linux server with SSH access, exactly like the machine you practised on in Module 1: install Docker, run containers, configure Nginx. When you launch an instance you choose these things:

AMI
Amazon Machine Image: the template of the operating system and preinstalled software. For this course use Ubuntu Server LTS (the login user is ubuntu). Amazon Linux is AWS's own distribution (login user ec2-user) and also works.
Instance type
The CPU and memory size, for example t3.micro.
Key pair
The SSH key used to log in. AWS stores the public half; you download the private half (.pem file) once.
Security group
A virtual firewall around the instance.
EBS volume
The virtual disk (Elastic Block Store).

Instance types and names

A name like t3.micro has three parts: the family (t), the generation (3) and the size (micro). Sizes go nano, micro, small, medium, large, xlarge, 2xlarge and so on, doubling each step.

FamilyMeaningUse for
t (t3, t4g)Burstable: a low baseline CPU with credits for burstsLearning, small apps, dev servers
mGeneral purpose, balanced CPU and memoryTypical application servers
cCompute-optimisedCPU-heavy work
rMemory-optimisedCaches, big in-memory data

Burstable (t-family) instances run at a baseline and earn credits while idle, which they spend when busy. If you run at full CPU for long, credits run out and the instance slows (or, in "unlimited" mode, bills extra), so they suit apps that are mostly idle. A letter g in the name (t4g, m7g) means Graviton, AWS's ARM processor. Graviton is often cheaper for the same power, but it needs ARM builds of your software. Java runs fine on ARM, and Docker images must be built for linux/arm64 (see the multi-platform note in the GHCR article). Which types qualify for any free offer changes, so check the current free-tier page before choosing.

Size for memory, not only CPU. A Spring Boot app plus Nginx plus Docker needs roughly a gigabyte to run comfortably; on a 1 GiB instance with a database too, memory is tight. Options: a bigger type, a swap file (shown in the runbook), or a managed database so the instance only runs the app.

Key pairs and SSH

When you create a key pair, AWS lets you download the private key only once. Keep it safe, and restrict its permissions, or SSH refuses it:

$ chmod 400 shop-key.pem
$ ssh -i shop-key.pem ubuntu@203.0.113.25
Welcome to Ubuntu 24.04 LTS (GNU/Linux 6.8.0-1012-aws x86_64)
ubuntu@ip-172-31-5-20:~$
PS> icacls .\shop-key.pem /inheritance:r
PS> icacls .\shop-key.pem /grant:r "$($env:USERNAME):(R)"
PS> ssh -i .\shop-key.pem ubuntu@203.0.113.25

If you lose the private key, you cannot recover it; you would create a new key pair and launch a new instance (or use AWS Systems Manager Session Manager or EC2 Instance Connect to get in and add a new key).

Security groups

A security group is a stateful firewall attached to the instance. Inbound traffic is denied unless a rule allows it; outbound is allowed by default. "Stateful" means that a reply to an allowed request is allowed automatically. For one web server:

TypePortSourceWhy
SSH22My IP onlyAdmin login
HTTP800.0.0.0/0 (anywhere)Nginx, and the Let's Encrypt check
HTTPS4430.0.0.0/0Public traffic

Do not open port 8080 (the Spring Boot container) to the world; only Nginx on the same machine talks to it. Never leave SSH open to 0.0.0.0/0: bots try passwords on port 22 all day. A rule's source can also be another security group, which is how the database lets in only your server (see RDS). Your home IP may change; if SSH suddenly times out, update the rule.

Disks, IP addresses and user data

  • EBS volumes are network disks. The root volume holds the OS. A volume survives a stop; whether it is deleted at termination is the "delete on termination" setting. Common type: gp3 (general-purpose SSD). Snapshots are backups of a volume. A volume lives in one AZ.
  • Public IP: the automatic public address of an instance changes when you stop and start it. An Elastic IP is a static public IPv4 address that you own until you release it; associate it with the instance so your DNS record stays valid. AWS now charges for public IPv4 addresses, and an unused Elastic IP is a classic waste, so check current pricing and release what you do not need.
  • User data: a script that runs once as root at first boot (through cloud-init). Good for installing packages automatically. Its output goes to /var/log/cloud-init-output.log.
#!/bin/bash
apt-get update -y
apt-get install -y nginx
systemctl enable --now nginx

Stop vs terminate

ActionWhat happensBilling
RebootRestart of the OS; same host, same IPsContinues
StopShut down; the instance and disks remain; the public IP changesNo compute charge, but EBS storage (and any Elastic IP) still bills
TerminateInstance deleted forever; root volume deleted if "delete on termination" is onStops, except volumes that were kept

Mistake: SSH errors and what they mean

  • Connection timed out — the security group does not allow your IP on port 22, you used the wrong IP, or the instance has no public address.
  • Permission denied (publickey) — wrong user (ubuntu vs ec2-user), wrong key file, or the instance was launched with another key pair.
  • UNPROTECTED PRIVATE KEY FILE — the .pem permissions are too open; chmod 400 it.

Tip: enable termination protection and IMDSv2

Turn on termination protection for the one server you care about. Prefer IMDSv2 (token-based instance metadata, the default on recent AMIs); it blocks a class of attacks where a bug in your app is tricked into reading the server's credentials.

Interview: "What happens to data when you stop vs terminate an instance?"

  • Stop: EBS data stays, the public IP changes (unless Elastic IP). Terminate: the instance is gone, and the root volume is deleted by default. Instance-store data is lost on stop.
  • Mention security group statefulness and the t-family credit model.

Runbook: deploy Spring Boot on EC2 with Docker, Nginx and HTTPS

Launch an Ubuntu instance, attach an Elastic IP, install Docker, run your container from GHCR with Compose, put Nginx in front, point an A record at the IP, get a certificate with certbot, and keep two small scripts for updating and rolling back.

What we are building

Your Spring Boot image (from Module 3), published to GHCR by your pipeline (Module 4), runs on one EC2 server. Nginx listens on ports 80 and 443 and forwards to the container on 127.0.0.1:8080. The database is RDS (or a container for a cheap experiment). We use the example name api.example.com and image ghcr.io/asha/shop-api; replace them with yours.

Step 1: launch the instance

  1. In the console, select your region (for example Mumbai), open EC2 → Launch instance.
  2. Name: shop-api. AMI: Ubuntu Server LTS. Instance type: pick a small one that fits your plan (check the current free-tier page; for a Spring Boot app 2 GiB of RAM is more comfortable than 1 GiB).
  3. Key pair: create shop-key (ED25519 or RSA, .pem), and save the download.
  4. Network settings: create a security group allowing SSH from My IP, and HTTP and HTTPS from anywhere. Do not open 8080.
  5. Storage: the default root volume (gp3) is enough; leave "delete on termination" on.
  6. Launch. Wait for "Running" and 2/2 status checks.
  7. EC2 → Elastic IPs → Allocate, then Associate it with your instance. This is the address for DNS.

Step 2: connect and prepare the server

$ ssh -i shop-key.pem ubuntu@203.0.113.25
$ sudo apt update && sudo apt -y upgrade
$ sudo timedatectl set-timezone Asia/Kolkata     # optional, logs in IST
$ free -h                                         # check the RAM you actually have

On a small instance, add a swap file so a short memory spike does not kill the app (swap is slow, so it is a safety net, not a substitute for RAM):

$ sudo fallocate -l 1G /swapfile
$ sudo chmod 600 /swapfile
$ sudo mkswap /swapfile
$ sudo swapon /swapfile
$ echo '/swapfile none swap sw 0 0' | sudo tee -a /etc/fstab
$ swapon --show

Step 3: install Docker

Docker's documentation (docs.docker.com) has the official apt-repository steps for Ubuntu. For a learning server, Docker's convenience script is a short alternative, but read a script before you run it as root and avoid it for production machines.

$ curl -fsSL https://get.docker.com -o get-docker.sh
$ less get-docker.sh                 # read it first
$ sudo sh get-docker.sh
$ sudo usermod -aG docker $USER      # allow 'docker' without sudo
$ exit                               # log out and back in for the group to apply
$ ssh -i shop-key.pem ubuntu@203.0.113.25
$ docker run --rm hello-world
$ docker compose version
$ sudo systemctl enable docker       # start Docker at boot (usually already on)

Members of the docker group effectively have root on the machine, so add only yourself.

Step 4: configuration, secrets and the Compose file

Create a folder that holds only configuration, never source code. The .env file holds secrets, so its permissions are strict.

$ sudo mkdir -p /opt/shop && sudo chown $USER:$USER /opt/shop
$ cd /opt/shop
$ nano .env
$ chmod 600 .env
IMAGE_TAG=sha-3f9c2ab
SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod
SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL=jdbc:postgresql://shop-db.abc123.ap-south-1.rds.amazonaws.com:5432/shop
SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME=shop_app
SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD=change-me-long-random
JWT_SECRET=change-me-another-long-random-value
JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS=-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75
services:
  api:
    image: ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:${IMAGE_TAG}
    env_file: .env
    ports:
      - "127.0.0.1:8080:8080"        # reachable only from this machine (Nginx)
    restart: unless-stopped          # comes back after a crash or a reboot
    logging:
      driver: json-file
      options:
        max-size: "10m"              # rotate logs so the disk never fills
        max-file: "3"

The binding 127.0.0.1:8080:8080 is the important line: Docker publishes the port on the loopback interface only. A plain 8080:8080 would open it on every interface, and Docker bypasses some host firewall rules, so the security group is your real protection too. In production a HEALTHCHECK is also useful (Module 6). The Spring property server.forward-headers-strategy=framework makes the app trust Nginx's X-Forwarded-* headers, so generated links and redirects use https.

Step 5: pull and run

If your image is private on GHCR, log in with a personal access token that has the read:packages scope. Type the token without leaving it in your shell history:

$ read -rs GHCR_TOKEN                 # paste the token, press Enter (nothing is echoed)
$ echo "$GHCR_TOKEN" | docker login ghcr.io -u asha --password-stdin
$ unset GHCR_TOKEN
$ cd /opt/shop
$ docker compose pull
$ docker compose up -d
$ docker compose ps
$ docker compose logs -f api          # Ctrl+C to leave; look for "Started ShopApplication"
$ curl -s http://127.0.0.1:8080/actuator/health
{"status":"UP"}

Step 6: Nginx as a reverse proxy

Nginx accepts public connections and forwards them to the app. The full configuration with TLS settings is in Module 2; here is the minimum.

$ sudo apt install -y nginx
$ sudo nano /etc/nginx/sites-available/shop
server {
    listen 80;
    listen [::]:80;
    server_name api.example.com;

    client_max_body_size 10m;

    location / {
        proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8080;
        proxy_set_header Host $host;
        proxy_set_header X-Real-IP $remote_addr;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-For $proxy_add_x_forwarded_for;
        proxy_set_header X-Forwarded-Proto $scheme;
        proxy_read_timeout 60s;
    }
}
$ sudo ln -s /etc/nginx/sites-available/shop /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/shop
$ sudo rm -f /etc/nginx/sites-enabled/default
$ sudo nginx -t                      # always test the syntax first
$ sudo systemctl reload nginx
$ curl -i -H "Host: api.example.com" http://127.0.0.1/actuator/health

Step 7: point the domain (A record)

At your DNS provider, add an A record: name api, value your Elastic IP. Details are in Domains and DNS. Check it from your laptop:

$ dig +short api.example.com
203.0.113.25
$ curl -i http://api.example.com/actuator/health

Wait until dig returns your IP. Do not run certbot before DNS works; the certificate check needs to reach your server by that name.

Step 8: HTTPS with Let's Encrypt

Let's Encrypt issues free certificates, and certbot is the tool that obtains them and edits Nginx for you. (The certbot website also documents a snap-based install, which gives newer versions; the apt packages below are simplest on Ubuntu.)

$ sudo apt install -y certbot python3-certbot-nginx
$ sudo certbot --nginx -d api.example.com
# It asks for an email address and for you to accept the terms of service,
# then offers to redirect HTTP to HTTPS: choose the redirect.
$ curl -I https://api.example.com/actuator/health
HTTP/2 200
$ sudo certbot renew --dry-run         # prove that automatic renewal works
$ systemctl list-timers | grep certbot # the renewal timer

Certificates last a short time (90 days), so renewal must be automatic. The package installs a timer that runs twice a day; the dry run proves it works. Port 80 must stay open for renewals.

Step 9: survive crashes and reboots

Three layers keep the app alive: restart: unless-stopped restarts the container if it exits; Docker itself starts at boot (systemctl enable docker); Nginx is a systemd service and starts at boot (systemctl enable nginx, already done by the package). Test it once:

$ sudo reboot
# wait a minute, ssh in again
$ docker compose -f /opt/shop/compose.yaml ps
$ curl -s https://api.example.com/actuator/health

If you run a plain JAR without Docker, use a systemd unit instead (see Module 1 for how units work):

[Unit]
Description=Shop API
After=network.target

[Service]
User=shop
EnvironmentFile=/etc/shop/shop.env
ExecStart=/usr/bin/java -jar /opt/shop/app.jar
Restart=on-failure
RestartSec=5

[Install]
WantedBy=multi-user.target

Step 10: logs

$ docker compose logs --tail 100 -f api
$ sudo tail -f /var/log/nginx/access.log /var/log/nginx/error.log

More methods are in Module 6.

Step 11: update to a new version, and roll back

Each build in your pipeline has an immutable tag (sha-xxxxxxx). A small script on the server changes the tag, pulls, restarts, checks health, and goes back automatically if the new version does not become healthy.

#!/usr/bin/env bash
# Usage: ./deploy.sh sha-3f9c2ab
set -euo pipefail
cd /opt/shop

NEW_TAG="${1:?usage: deploy.sh <image-tag>}"
OLD_TAG="$(grep '^IMAGE_TAG=' .env | cut -d= -f2)"

sed -i "s/^IMAGE_TAG=.*/IMAGE_TAG=${NEW_TAG}/" .env
echo "${OLD_TAG}" > .previous_tag

docker compose pull api
docker compose up -d api

for i in $(seq 1 40); do
  if curl -fsS http://127.0.0.1:8080/actuator/health > /dev/null; then
    echo "Healthy on ${NEW_TAG}"
    docker image prune -f > /dev/null
    exit 0
  fi
  sleep 3
done

echo "New version is not healthy, going back to ${OLD_TAG}"
sed -i "s/^IMAGE_TAG=.*/IMAGE_TAG=${OLD_TAG}/" .env
docker compose up -d api
exit 1
$ chmod +x /opt/shop/deploy.sh
$ /opt/shop/deploy.sh sha-9b1d4e7          # deploy a new version
$ /opt/shop/deploy.sh "$(cat /opt/shop/.previous_tag)"   # manual rollback

Module 4's CD workflow runs these same commands over SSH after the image is pushed. A rollback works only because old images still exist (so prune carefully) and because database changes are backward compatible.

Troubleshooting table

SymptomLikely causeCheck
Browser times out on the domainSecurity group, wrong IP in DNS, Nginx downdig, security group rules, systemctl status nginx
502 Bad GatewayNginx cannot reach the app (down, wrong port, still starting)docker compose ps, curl 127.0.0.1:8080
Container restarts in a loopApp crashes at startup (DB, missing env)docker compose logs api
certbot failsDNS not pointing yet, port 80 blockeddig, security group port 80
unauthorized on pullNot logged in to GHCR, or token lacks read:packagesdocker login ghcr.io

Rule: only 80, 443 (and SSH from your IP) are public

The app port and the database port must never be reachable from the internet. From your laptop, curl http://203.0.113.25:8080 should time out.

Mistakes

  • Putting secrets in the Compose file or the image instead of an env file with mode 600.
  • Deploying latest, so you cannot name or roll back what runs.
  • Running certbot before the DNS record exists.
  • Editing files only on the server and not keeping the Compose file and Nginx config in Git.
  • Forgetting that stopping the instance changes the public IP when no Elastic IP is attached.

Edge cases

  • Let's Encrypt applies rate limits; use --dry-run or its staging server while testing.
  • If the app runs on Cloudflare's proxy (orange cloud), certificate and redirect settings behave differently; see Domains and DNS.
  • docker compose up -d recreates the container, causing a few seconds of downtime. See deployment strategies.

Interview: "Walk me through deploying a Spring Boot app to a server."

  • Provision (EC2, Elastic IP, security group), install Docker, pull the tagged image, run with env vars, Nginx reverse proxy, DNS A record, certbot HTTPS, restart policy, logs, scripted update with health check and rollback.
  • Add: secrets never in the image, only 80/443 public, database private.

RDS: managed PostgreSQL, private access and the cheap Docker alternative

RDS runs PostgreSQL for you (installation, patching, backups, failover); you keep it private by allowing port 5432 only from your EC2 security group, pass the endpoint to Spring Boot through environment variables, and delete it when a lab ends.

Why a managed database

Running PostgreSQL yourself means installing it, tuning it, patching it, taking backups, testing restores and planning for a failed disk. Amazon RDS (Relational Database Service) does the heavy parts:

  • Backups: automatic daily backups plus transaction logs, so you can restore to a point in time within the retention period you set.
  • Patching: minor engine updates and OS patches in a maintenance window you choose.
  • Multi-AZ: a standby copy in another availability zone with automatic failover.
  • Monitoring and storage growth: metrics in CloudWatch and storage that can scale.

You still own the schema, the users, the queries, the indexes and the network rules. RDS is the right default for a real application; the cost is higher than running the database yourself, and the exact price changes, so check the current RDS pricing page and any free-tier offer before you create one.

Create an instance (console steps)

  1. RDS → Create database → Standard create → engine PostgreSQL.
  2. Pick a template for your purpose (the console offers dev/test or free-tier style templates; read the current descriptions) and a small instance class. Use the same region as your EC2 instance.
  3. DB instance identifier shop-db; master username and a long random password (or let RDS keep the password in Secrets Manager).
  4. Storage: small is enough; understand whether storage autoscaling is on.
  5. Connectivity: the same VPC as your EC2 instance; Public access: No; create a new security group shop-db-sg.
  6. Additional configuration: initial database name shop; backup retention; Multi-AZ only if you need it (it roughly doubles the cost).
  7. Create. After a few minutes, copy the endpoint, a DNS name such as shop-db.abc123.ap-south-1.rds.amazonaws.com.

Let only your server in

A security group rule can name another security group as its source. Edit shop-db-sg and add an inbound rule: type PostgreSQL, port 5432, source = the security group of your EC2 instance (for example shop-ec2-sg). Now any instance in that group can connect, whatever its IP, and nobody else can, including you from your laptop.

Connect and create an application user

$ sudo apt install -y postgresql-client
$ psql -h shop-db.abc123.ap-south-1.rds.amazonaws.com -U postgres -d shop
Password for user postgres:
shop=>
CREATE USER shop_app WITH PASSWORD 'change-me-long-random';
ALTER DATABASE shop OWNER TO shop_app;   -- simple for a small app; Flyway needs DDL rights

Do not run the application as the master user. A dedicated user limits the damage of a bug or a leaked password. If you need to reach the database from your laptop (for a one-time import), use an SSH tunnel through the EC2 server instead of opening 5432 to the world:

$ ssh -i shop-key.pem -N -L 5433:shop-db.abc123.ap-south-1.rds.amazonaws.com:5432 ubuntu@203.0.113.25
# in another terminal:
$ psql -h localhost -p 5433 -U shop_app -d shop

Connect from Spring Boot

Spring Boot reads the standard properties, and any property can be set by an environment variable (dots become underscores, uppercase). Put the values in the server's .env file:

SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL=jdbc:postgresql://shop-db.abc123.ap-south-1.rds.amazonaws.com:5432/shop?sslmode=require
SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME=shop_app
SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD=change-me-long-random
SPRING_JPA_HIBERNATE_DDL_AUTO=validate
SPRING_DATASOURCE_HIKARI_MAXIMUM_POOL_SIZE=10

sslmode=require asks the PostgreSQL JDBC driver to encrypt the connection (it does not verify the server certificate; stricter modes need the RDS CA bundle). ddl-auto=validate means Hibernate only checks the schema; create tables with Flyway or Liquibase migrations, never with update in production (see Spring Boot · Module 3). Limit the Hikari pool: a small RDS instance allows only a limited number of connections, and several app instances each hold a pool.

Backups, snapshots and restore

  • Automated backups are on by default with a retention period you set; they enable point-in-time restore.
  • Manual snapshots are kept until you delete them. Take one before a risky migration. Snapshots use (billed) storage.
  • A restore creates a new instance with a new endpoint; it does not rewind the old one. You then update SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL. Practise a restore once while nothing depends on it; a backup you have never restored is only a hope.
  • When you delete an instance, the console offers a final snapshot. Automated backups are removed with the instance unless you choose otherwise.

Multi-AZ and read replicas

Multi-AZ keeps a synchronous standby in another AZ. If the primary fails, RDS flips the endpoint's DNS to the standby, usually within a minute or two. It improves availability, not read speed. A read replica is a separate copy used to spread read queries and has its own endpoint; it is for scaling, not failover. Do not confuse the two (a common interview question).

The cheap option: PostgreSQL in Docker on the same EC2

For a learning project you can run PostgreSQL as another Compose service and skip the RDS cost:

  postgres:
    image: postgres:16
    environment:
      POSTGRES_DB: shop
      POSTGRES_USER: shop_app
      POSTGRES_PASSWORD: ${DB_PASSWORD}
    volumes:
      - pgdata:/var/lib/postgresql/data
    restart: unless-stopped
    # no 'ports:' section: only the api container can reach it

volumes:
  pgdata:

The app then uses jdbc:postgresql://postgres:5432/shop (the service name, see Docker networks). The risks are real:

Pros

  • No extra service cost; simple to start.
  • Fast: same machine, no network hop.

Cons

  • One disk and one machine: if the instance dies, the data may die with it.
  • No automatic backups, failover or patching; you must do them.
  • The database and the app fight over a small amount of RAM.
  • docker compose down -v deletes the data volume.
$ docker compose exec -T postgres pg_dump -U shop_app shop | gzip > /opt/backups/shop-$(date +%F).sql.gz
$ aws s3 cp /opt/backups/shop-$(date +%F).sql.gz s3://shop-backups-asha/

Also take regular EBS snapshots. For anything with real users, use RDS.

Mistakes

  • Turning on "Public access" and opening 5432 to 0.0.0.0/0 to "make it work". Bots find it in minutes.
  • Leaving a test RDS instance running for weeks.
  • Using the master user and ddl-auto=create in production.

Errors and what they mean

  • Connection timed out or PSQLException: The connection attempt failed — security group rule missing or wrong source, wrong endpoint, or EC2 in a different VPC.
  • password authentication failed for user — wrong password or user; special characters in a password inside a URL need escaping (put credentials in separate variables as above).
  • too many connections — pool sizes times app instances exceed the database limit.
  • After the instance was stopped: RDS can be stopped for a limited time and then restarts by itself, so a "stopped" database may begin billing again.

Interview: "Why use RDS instead of installing PostgreSQL on EC2?"

  • Managed backups with point-in-time restore, patching, Multi-AZ failover, monitoring; less operational risk. Cost: higher price and less OS-level control.
  • Security: private subnet or no public access, access only from the app's security group.

S3: buckets, objects, safe access and presigned URLs

S3 stores files as objects in buckets; keep buckets private with Block Public Access, let your app reach them through an IAM role, and let browsers upload or download directly using short-lived presigned URLs.

Buckets and objects

S3 (Simple Storage Service) is object storage: you put whole files in and get whole files out over HTTP. A bucket is a top-level container, and its name is unique across all AWS accounts (lowercase letters, digits, hyphens). An object is a file plus metadata, found by its key, for example products/42/photo.jpg. S3 has no real folders; the slashes are just part of the key, and the console shows them as folders using the prefix. S3 is very durable and scales without limit, but it is not a file system: you cannot append to an object or edit part of it, only replace it. Typical uses: user uploads, invoices and PDFs, images, backups, static websites and build artefacts.

Storage classes (brief)

ClassMeant for
StandardFrequently accessed data (the default)
Intelligent-TieringUnknown or changing access patterns; S3 moves objects automatically
Standard-IA / One Zone-IARarely accessed but needs quick access; cheaper storage, retrieval fee
Glacier classesArchives and compliance data; retrieval takes minutes to hours

A lifecycle rule moves or deletes objects automatically, for example "delete temp uploads after 7 days" or "move logs to an archive class after 30 days". Costs depend on class, requests and data transfer; check current pricing.

Keep it private

  • Block Public Access is on by default for new buckets. Leave it on. Public buckets are behind many data leaks.
  • Access is decided by IAM policies (what a user or role may do) and optionally a bucket policy. Your app gets access through an instance role, not through keys in the code.
  • Enable default encryption (new buckets already encrypt objects at rest) and consider versioning to recover from accidental overwrites.

Uploading from Spring Boot (AWS SDK for Java v2)

Add the SDK's BOM (bill of materials, which keeps module versions aligned) and the s3 module. Use the latest 2.x version shown on the SDK's documentation page for the property below.

<dependencyManagement>
  <dependencies>
    <dependency>
      <groupId>software.amazon.awssdk</groupId>
      <artifactId>bom</artifactId>
      <version>${aws.sdk.version}</version>
      <type>pom</type>
      <scope>import</scope>
    </dependency>
  </dependencies>
</dependencyManagement>

<dependencies>
  <dependency>
    <groupId>software.amazon.awssdk</groupId>
    <artifactId>s3</artifactId>
  </dependency>
</dependencies>
@Configuration
public class AwsConfig {

    @Bean
    S3Client s3Client(@Value("${app.aws.region}") String region) {
        // No keys here: the SDK finds credentials by itself (env, profile, or the EC2 role)
        return S3Client.builder().region(Region.of(region)).build();
    }

    @Bean
    S3Presigner s3Presigner(@Value("${app.aws.region}") String region) {
        return S3Presigner.builder().region(Region.of(region)).build();
    }
}
@Service
public class FileStorageService {

    public record UploadTicket(String key, String url) { }

    private final S3Client s3;
    private final S3Presigner presigner;
    private final String bucket;

    public FileStorageService(S3Client s3, S3Presigner presigner,
                              @Value("${app.aws.bucket}") String bucket) {
        this.s3 = s3;
        this.presigner = presigner;
        this.bucket = bucket;
    }

    /** Server-side upload: the file passes through your app. Fine for small files. */
    public String upload(MultipartFile file) throws IOException {
        String key = "uploads/" + UUID.randomUUID();      // never trust the user's file name
        PutObjectRequest request = PutObjectRequest.builder()
                .bucket(bucket).key(key).contentType(file.getContentType()).build();
        s3.putObject(request, RequestBody.fromInputStream(file.getInputStream(), file.getSize()));
        return key;
    }

    /** A link the browser can use to download one private object for 10 minutes. */
    public String downloadUrl(String key) {
        GetObjectRequest get = GetObjectRequest.builder().bucket(bucket).key(key).build();
        GetObjectPresignRequest presign = GetObjectPresignRequest.builder()
                .signatureDuration(Duration.ofMinutes(10)).getObjectRequest(get).build();
        return presigner.presignGetObject(presign).url().toString();
    }

    /** A link the browser can use to upload one object directly to S3. */
    public UploadTicket uploadTicket(String contentType) {
        String key = "uploads/" + UUID.randomUUID();
        PutObjectRequest put = PutObjectRequest.builder()
                .bucket(bucket).key(key).contentType(contentType).build();
        PutObjectPresignRequest presign = PutObjectPresignRequest.builder()
                .signatureDuration(Duration.ofMinutes(10)).putObjectRequest(put).build();
        return new UploadTicket(key, presigner.presignPutObject(presign).url().toString());
    }
}

Presigned URLs

A presigned URL is a normal S3 URL with a signature in its query string, created by your server using its credentials. Whoever holds the URL can perform that one operation on that one object until it expires, with no AWS account. This lets the browser talk to S3 directly, so a 200 MB video does not pass through your Spring Boot server.

async function uploadFile(file) {
  const res = await fetch("/api/files/upload-url", {
    method: "POST",
    headers: { "Content-Type": "application/json" },
    body: JSON.stringify({ contentType: file.type }),
  });
  const { key, url } = await res.json();

  // The Content-Type here must match the one that was signed
  const put = await fetch(url, {
    method: "PUT",
    headers: { "Content-Type": file.type },
    body: file,
  });
  if (!put.ok) throw new Error("Upload failed: " + put.status);
  return key;
}

The browser calls S3 from your site's origin, so the bucket needs a CORS configuration (Bucket → Permissions → Cross-origin resource sharing). Restrict it to your real origins:

[
  {
    "AllowedOrigins": ["https://shop.example.com", "http://localhost:5173"],
    "AllowedMethods": ["GET", "PUT"],
    "AllowedHeaders": ["*"],
    "ExposeHeaders": ["ETag"],
    "MaxAgeSeconds": 3000
  }
]

Serving a React build from S3 and CloudFront (brief)

S3 can hold the static files of a React app, and CloudFront (AWS's CDN) serves them over HTTPS from locations near the user. Keep the bucket private and give CloudFront access with an Origin Access Control.

  1. Build: npm run build creates dist/.
  2. Upload: aws s3 sync dist/ s3://shop-web-asha --delete.
  3. Create a CloudFront distribution with the bucket as origin, default root object index.html.
  4. For client-side routing, add custom error responses that return /index.html with status 200 for 403 and 404.
  5. For a custom domain, request a certificate in AWS Certificate Manager (CloudFront needs it in us-east-1) and add the domain to the distribution.
  6. After each deploy: aws cloudfront create-invalidation --distribution-id EXXXX --paths "/*" so users do not see old files.

This is fine, but Vercel or Netlify do the same with far less setup (see Vercel and Netlify).

Mistakes

  • Making the bucket public so "the images load". Use presigned GET URLs, or CloudFront.
  • Putting AWS keys in the React code to upload directly. Everything in the browser is public; use a presigned URL.
  • Using the original file name as the key (path tricks, clashes, personal data). Generate the key.
  • Very long expiry times on presigned URLs. Use minutes.

Exceptions and errors

  • A presigned URL made with temporary role credentials stops working when those credentials expire, even if you asked for a longer time.
  • SignatureDoesNotMatch on a PUT — the browser sent a different Content-Type (or signed header) than the one signed.
  • S3Exception with status 403 AccessDenied — the role lacks the permission, or the bucket policy denies it; 404 NoSuchKeyException — wrong key; NoSuchBucket — wrong name or region.
  • SdkClientException: Unable to load credentials — no role, profile or environment credentials on this machine.
  • CORS errors appear in the browser console, not in your Java logs.

Interview: "How would you let users upload a large file securely?"

  • The API authenticates the user and returns a short-lived presigned PUT URL for a generated key; the browser uploads directly to a private bucket (with CORS); the API then stores the key in the database. Limit type and size, and scan if needed.

IAM: users, roles, policies, least privilege and instance roles

IAM decides who may do what in AWS: policies (JSON documents) grant actions on resources to users, groups or roles; give every person and application the minimum, and give EC2 a role so no access keys are stored on the server.

The building blocks

User
An identity for one person or program, with a password and/or access keys. Long-lived credentials, so use them sparingly.
Group
A set of users that share permissions ("developers"). Permissions attach to the group, not to each user.
Role
An identity with no permanent password or key that a trusted entity can assume to receive temporary credentials: an EC2 instance, a Lambda function, another AWS account, or a person signing in through Identity Center.
Policy
A JSON document listing allowed or denied actions on resources. Attached to users, groups and roles (identity policies), or to a resource such as a bucket (resource policy).

IAM is global, not tied to a region. By default everything is denied. A request succeeds only when some policy allows it and no policy explicitly denies it (an explicit Deny always wins).

Anatomy of a policy

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Sid": "ReadWriteObjects",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": ["s3:GetObject", "s3:PutObject", "s3:DeleteObject"],
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::shop-uploads-asha/*"
    },
    {
      "Sid": "ListTheBucket",
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Action": "s3:ListBucket",
      "Resource": "arn:aws:s3:::shop-uploads-asha"
    }
  ]
}
Version
The policy language version. Always 2012-10-17.
Statement
One or more rules.
Effect
Allow or Deny.
Action
The API operation, written service:Operation. Wildcards such as s3:* are allowed but broad.
Resource
What the action applies to, as an ARN (Amazon Resource Name), in the form arn:aws:service:region:account-id:resource. S3 ARNs have no region or account.
Condition
Optional extra checks, for example the region or source IP.

Notice the two ARNs. Object actions apply to bucket/* (the objects), while s3:ListBucket applies to the bucket itself, without /*. Mixing them up is the most common S3 policy bug. A condition example, which allows an action only in the Mumbai region:

{
  "Effect": "Allow",
  "Action": "ec2:*",
  "Resource": "*",
  "Condition": {
    "StringEquals": { "aws:RequestedRegion": "ap-south-1" }
  }
}

Policies are either AWS managed (ready-made, for example AmazonS3ReadOnlyAccess, easy but often broader than needed) or customer managed (yours, like the one above), plus inline policies embedded in one identity. Prefer customer managed policies for anything real.

Least privilege in practice

  1. Start with no permissions. Add what the task needs.
  2. Name exact resources (one bucket) instead of "Resource": "*".
  3. Name exact actions instead of "Action": "*".
  4. When a call fails with AccessDenied, read the message. It names the action and resource, such as "not authorized to perform: s3:PutObject on resource: arn:aws:s3:::shop-uploads-asha/uploads/x", and tells you exactly what to add.
  5. Later, use IAM's "last accessed" information and Access Analyzer to remove unused permissions.

Instance roles: no keys on the server

Your Spring Boot app on EC2 needs to reach S3. The wrong way is to create an access key and paste it into .env. The right way is an IAM role for EC2. AWS gives the instance temporary credentials that rotate automatically, and the SDK finds them by itself through the instance metadata service.

  1. IAM → Roles → Create role → trusted entity: AWS service → EC2.
  2. Attach your customer-managed policy (the JSON above). Name the role shop-api-role.
  3. EC2 → select the instance → Actions → Security → Modify IAM role → choose shop-api-role. (Behind the scenes the role sits in an "instance profile".)
  4. Run the app with no AWS_ACCESS_KEY_ID variables. The SDK's default credentials provider chain tries environment variables, then profile files, and finally the instance role.

The role also has a trust policy, saying who may assume it. The console writes it for you when you pick EC2:

{
  "Version": "2012-10-17",
  "Statement": [
    {
      "Effect": "Allow",
      "Principal": { "Service": "ec2.amazonaws.com" },
      "Action": "sts:AssumeRole"
    }
  ]
}

Two policies, two questions: the trust policy answers "who can become this role?" and the permissions policy answers "what can the role do?".

Edge case: the role does not work inside a Docker container

Recent instances require IMDSv2 with a hop limit of 1, and a request from a container on the default bridge network takes one more hop, so the SDK inside the container may fail with "Unable to load credentials". Raise the hop limit to 2 for the instance:

$ aws ec2 modify-instance-metadata-options \
    --instance-id i-0abc123def456789 \
    --http-tokens required \
    --http-put-response-hop-limit 2

Check which identity the server uses (install the AWS CLI first, or read the app's startup log):

{
    "UserId": "AROAEXAMPLEROLEID:i-0abc123def456789",
    "Account": "123456789012",
    "Arn": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/shop-api-role/i-0abc123def456789"
}

Other tools worth knowing

  • MFA for every human user. Password policy for IAM users.
  • Permission boundaries and Service Control Policies (organisation-wide limits) set the maximum permissions; companies with many accounts use them.
  • IAM Identity Center for staff sign-in with temporary credentials.
  • CloudTrail records who called which API, which is essential for audits and for investigating incidents.

Mistakes

  • Attaching AdministratorAccess to the application, "just to make it work".
  • Keys in .env on the server, in the image, or in Git, when a role would do.
  • Sharing one IAM user among several people.
  • Writing s3:ListBucket on bucket/*; it needs the bucket ARN.

In real projects

Each service has its own role with a small policy; developers sign in through SSO with roles that expire; production access is limited and audited; policies are written as Terraform (Module 6) and reviewed like code.

Interview: "How does an application on EC2 access S3 securely?"

  • An IAM role with a least-privilege policy, attached to the instance (instance profile); the SDK uses temporary credentials from the metadata service; nothing is stored on disk.
  • Explain user vs role, trust policy vs permissions policy, default deny and explicit deny.

AWS services glossary: what the other names mean

AWS has hundreds of services, but a Java backend developer meets about twenty; this glossary gives one line for each and shows how they fit into a typical production setup.

Networking

VPC
Virtual Private Cloud: your own isolated network inside a region, with an IP range you choose. Every EC2 and RDS instance lives in one. New accounts get a default VPC.
Subnet
A slice of the VPC's IP range that lives in one availability zone. A public subnet has a route to the internet gateway; a private subnet does not (databases go here).
Internet gateway / NAT gateway
The internet gateway connects a VPC to the internet. A NAT gateway lets private machines make outbound calls only; it is billed per hour and per GB, a famous surprise cost.
Security group
The stateful firewall around an instance, as in the EC2 article.
ELB / ALB
Elastic Load Balancing. The Application Load Balancer spreads HTTP(S) traffic across several servers in different zones, checks their health, and can terminate TLS.
Route 53
AWS's DNS service (and domain registrar). It can send users to a load balancer, CloudFront or an IP.
CloudFront
The CDN: caches content at edge locations close to users and serves it over HTTPS.

Compute and containers

ECR
Elastic Container Registry: AWS's private Docker image registry (like GHCR).
ECS
Elastic Container Service: AWS's own container orchestrator. You describe tasks and services; it runs and replaces containers.
Fargate
A mode where ECS (or EKS) runs your containers without you managing any EC2 servers.
EKS
Elastic Kubernetes Service: managed Kubernetes (see Module 6).
Elastic Beanstalk
A PaaS on AWS: upload a JAR or container, and it creates the servers, load balancer and scaling for you. Quick, but less flexible and less modern than ECS.
Lambda
Serverless functions: upload code, AWS runs it on demand, billed by calls and duration. Good for events and small tasks; Spring Boot cold starts need care.

Data and messaging

EBS / EFS
EBS is a block disk for one instance; EFS is a shared network file system.
DynamoDB
A managed NoSQL key-value and document database with very fast, predictable access.
SQS
Simple Queue Service: a managed message queue for decoupling services.
SNS
Simple Notification Service: publish a message and fan it out to emails, SMS, queues or HTTP endpoints. Budget alerts use it.
SES
Simple Email Service: send application emails (OTP, invoices). New accounts start in a sandbox with limits; you request production access.

Security, secrets and operations

IAM
Users, roles and policies (IAM article).
Secrets Manager
Stores secrets (database passwords, API keys), encrypts them, controls access with IAM, and can rotate some automatically. It is a paid service.
Systems Manager Parameter Store
Stores configuration values and secrets (as SecureString), simpler than Secrets Manager. Spring Cloud AWS can load them as properties.
ACM
AWS Certificate Manager: free public TLS certificates for load balancers and CloudFront.
CloudWatch
Logs, metrics, dashboards and alarms for AWS resources and your app. Set a retention period on log groups, or they grow forever.
CloudTrail
The audit log of API calls: who did what, when.

How they fit together

In a production layout the app servers (EC2 or Fargate containers) sit in private subnets behind the load balancer in public subnets; CloudWatch collects logs and alarms; ECR holds the images; Secrets Manager holds the database password; IAM roles tie it together. You do not need all of this for Project 3, but you should be able to draw it and say what each box does.

What to learn first

PriorityServices
MustEC2, security groups, EBS, IAM, S3, RDS, Route 53 basics, CloudWatch basics
Good to knowVPC and subnets, ALB, CloudFront, ECR, Secrets Manager / Parameter Store, SES
LaterECS/Fargate, EKS, Lambda, SQS/SNS, DynamoDB, Elastic Beanstalk

Tip: learn the shape, not the console

The console changes layout often and every service has dozens of settings. Learn what each service is for and what a typical architecture looks like. Look up the exact buttons in the current documentation when you need them.

Interview: "Draw the AWS architecture for a Spring Boot web app."

  • Route 53 → (CloudFront for static) → ALB in public subnets → app servers/containers in private subnets across two AZs → RDS Multi-AZ in private subnets; S3 for files; IAM roles; CloudWatch; ECR for images; Secrets Manager.
  • Explain which parts are public and which are private, and why.

Render and Railway: deploying Spring Boot without managing servers

Render and Railway build your repository (from a Dockerfile) on every push, run it behind HTTPS with a public URL, inject environment variables, and offer managed PostgreSQL, so you deploy a backend in minutes with no SSH or Nginx.

What these platforms do for you

A PaaS such as Render or Railway replaces most of the runbook you just read. You connect a GitHub repository; the platform builds the image, runs the container, gives it an https://… URL with a certificate, restarts it if it crashes, keeps logs, and lets you attach a database. The price of the convenience is less control, platform-specific settings, and cost that grows faster than a self-managed server at scale. They are excellent for portfolio projects, demos and small startups.

Plans, free tiers, credits, sleep behaviour and prices change often. Read each platform's current pricing and docs before choosing, and do not assume a free plan fits a Spring Boot app, which needs more memory than a small Node service.

Deploy on Render

  1. Push your project (with the multi-stage Dockerfile from Module 3) to GitHub.
  2. In Render choose New → Web Service → connect the repository. For a Java project the safest choice is the Docker runtime so Render uses your Dockerfile (check the docs for any native Java build option).
  3. Pick the region nearest your users and an instance type. If your repository holds several projects, set the root directory.
  4. Add environment variables (see below).
  5. Set the health check path to /actuator/health, so Render only sends traffic to a started app.
  6. Create the service; watch the build and deploy logs. Later pushes to the branch redeploy automatically.

Create a PostgreSQL database in the same region from the dashboard. It shows an internal connection address (for services in the same region and account) and an external one (for your laptop). Use the internal address from your service.

Deploy on Railway

  1. New project → Deploy from GitHub repo. Railway looks for a Dockerfile; without one, it tries to detect a Maven or Gradle project and build it automatically. A Dockerfile gives the most predictable result.
  2. Add a PostgreSQL service to the project from the dashboard.
  3. In your service's Variables tab, set variables. Railway lets one service reference another's variables (for the database host, port and password), so you do not copy secrets by hand; read its docs for the exact reference syntax.
  4. Generate a public domain for the service in its Networking settings.

Spring Boot settings that platforms need

Two things trip up most first deployments.

1. The port. The platform tells the app which port to listen on through the PORT environment variable. Make Spring Boot use it, with 8080 as the default for local runs:

server.port=${PORT:8080}
server.forward-headers-strategy=framework

spring.datasource.url=jdbc:postgresql://${DB_HOST}:${DB_PORT:5432}/${DB_NAME}
spring.datasource.username=${DB_USER}
spring.datasource.password=${DB_PASSWORD}
spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=validate

management.endpoints.web.exposure.include=health,info
management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled=true

2. The database URL. Platforms often give a URL like postgres://user:password@host:5432/dbname. The JDBC driver does not understand that form: it needs jdbc:postgresql://host:5432/dbname with user and password given separately. So copy the host, port, name, user and password into separate variables as above (many platforms show them separately, or you can split the URL yourself). If the platform offers a ready-made JDBC URL, use it.

VariableValueNote
SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVEprodActivates application-prod.properties
DB_HOST, DB_NAME, DB_USER, DB_PASSWORDFrom the platform's database pageUse the internal host
JWT_SECRETA long random valueSecret, never in Git
JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75Keeps the heap inside a small container
CORS_ALLOWED_ORIGINSYour frontend URLCustom variable your CORS config reads

Free plans, sleeping and cold starts

Some platforms put free services to sleep after a period without traffic, and wake them on the next request. A Spring Boot app takes many seconds to start, so the first visitor waits a long time (and a browser or proxy may time out). Free databases may also expire or have strict limits. These terms change, so read the current plan details. Acceptable for a demo, not for a real customer. Workarounds such as pinging the app every few minutes may break the platform's rules or use up your quota; paid plans remove sleeping. For job interviews, tell the interviewer the first load may be slow and why.

Auto-deploy, CI and rollback

By default the platform deploys on every push to the chosen branch, even if your tests fail. Fix that by turning on "wait for CI to pass" if the platform offers it, or by turning auto-deploy off and calling the platform's deploy hook from your pipeline after tests succeed (see the Render example in the CD article). Both platforms keep earlier deployments and let you roll back to one from the dashboard.

Mistakes

  • Hard-coding server.port=8080: the platform's health check never reaches the app, and the deploy is marked failed.
  • Using localhost or the external database address instead of the internal one.
  • Forgetting CORS for the frontend's new HTTPS origin (Frontend · Module 6).
  • Committing application-prod.properties with real passwords.

Edge cases

  • An app killed with exit code 137 on a small instance usually ran out of memory; reduce the heap with MaxRAMPercentage or choose a bigger instance.
  • Local disk on these platforms is usually ephemeral: files saved to the container's disk disappear on redeploy. Use S3 or a platform disk.
  • Build timeouts: a Maven build that downloads the world each time is slow; keep the Dockerfile's dependency layer cached.

Interview: "When would you choose Render or Railway over EC2?"

  • Small team, MVP, demo or low traffic: faster to ship, no server upkeep, built-in HTTPS and deploys.
  • Choose EC2/ECS when you need control, private networking, compliance, or cost optimisation at scale.

Vercel and Netlify: deploying a React (Vite) app

Connect the GitHub repository, set the build command (npm run build) and output directory (dist), add VITE_ environment variables, add a rewrite so React Router works, and every pull request gets its own preview URL.

What they do

Vercel and Netlify host static sites and frontend apps. A React app built with Vite is just HTML, JavaScript and CSS files after npm run build; these platforms build it for you on every push, upload the files to a global CDN, give you HTTPS and a URL, and keep every older version. They are the easiest place for a React frontend, and both have free plans for personal projects (check current terms; commercial use may need a paid plan).

Deploy steps

  1. Push the React project to GitHub. Sign in to Vercel or Netlify with GitHub.
  2. Add New Project / Add new site → import the repository.
  3. Settings (usually detected for Vite, but check): framework Vite; install command npm install (or npm ci); build command npm run build; output directory dist. If the React app is in a subfolder (for example frontend/), set the root directory to it.
  4. Add environment variables, for example VITE_API_BASE_URL=https://api.example.com.
  5. Deploy. The first build gives a URL like your-project.vercel.app or your-site.netlify.app.

Environment variables in Vite

Vite exposes only variables that start with VITE_ to the browser code, read as import.meta.env.VITE_API_BASE_URL. The value is baked into the JavaScript at build time, so after changing a variable you must redeploy, and every visitor can read the value in the bundle. Therefore never put secrets (API secrets, database passwords) in VITE_ variables; only public settings such as the API URL.

const API = import.meta.env.VITE_API_BASE_URL ?? "http://localhost:8080";

export async function getProducts() {
  const res = await fetch(`${API}/api/products`);
  if (!res.ok) throw new Error("Request failed: " + res.status);
  return res.json();
}

Single-page app rewrites

A React Router app has one real file, index.html. If a user opens /orders/42 directly or refreshes there, the server looks for a file at that path, finds none, and returns 404. The fix is a rewrite: serve index.html for unknown paths while keeping the URL, and let React Router draw the page.

{
  "rewrites": [
    { "source": "/(.*)", "destination": "/index.html" }
  ]
}
[build]
  command = "npm run build"
  publish = "dist"

[[redirects]]
  from = "/*"
  to = "/index.html"
  status = 200

Netlify also accepts a file named _redirects in the public/ folder containing /* /index.html 200. Static files (JS, CSS, images) that exist are served first, so the catch-all does not break them. Netlify's rule is status 200 (a rewrite), not 301.

Talking to your backend

Two ways to connect the deployed frontend and the API:

  • Direct calls with CORS. The browser calls https://api.example.com directly; the Spring Boot app must allow the frontend's origin in its CORS configuration (see Spring Boot · Module 2).
  • Proxy rewrite. The platform forwards /api/* to the backend, so the browser sees one origin and CORS is not needed. On Vercel:
{
  "rewrites": [
    { "source": "/api/:path*", "destination": "https://api.example.com/api/:path*" },
    { "source": "/(.*)", "destination": "/index.html" }
  ]
}

Netlify has the same idea with a redirect to a full URL and status 200. Whichever you pick, the backend must be served over HTTPS: a page loaded over HTTPS cannot call an http:// API (the browser blocks this "mixed content").

Preview deployments and rollback

Every pull request or branch gets its own build and URL (a preview deployment), so you and your reviewer can click the real result before merging. The production URL updates only when you merge into the production branch (usually main). Note that previews have different origins, so your backend CORS list must allow them if previews call a real API (or you point previews to a staging backend). Both platforms keep every deployment: rolling back means promoting an earlier one, which takes seconds and needs no rebuild.

Custom domain

In the project's Domains settings add shop.example.com (or your apex domain). The platform shows the DNS record to create, normally a CNAME for a subdomain and A or ALIAS records for the apex, and issues the HTTPS certificate itself. Details in Domains and DNS.

Mistakes

  • Refresh gives 404 on a nested route: the rewrite is missing.
  • API calls go to localhost:8080 in production: the VITE_ variable was not set for the production environment, or it was set after the build and not rebuilt.
  • Wrong output directory (build is Create React App's name; Vite uses dist): the site shows 404 for everything.
  • Variable without the VITE_ prefix: it is undefined in the browser.

Edge cases

  • Case-sensitive file names: import Header from "./header" works on Windows and fails on the Linux build server when the file is Header.jsx.
  • Node version mismatch can break the build; set the Node version in the project settings or an engines field if needed.
  • Warnings treated as errors: some platforms set CI=true, and lint warnings may fail the build in some toolchains.

Interview: "How do you deploy a React app and handle client-side routing?"

  • Build to static files, host on a CDN platform (Vercel, Netlify, S3 + CloudFront); add a rewrite of all unknown paths to index.html; build-time VITE_ variables; HTTPS; CORS or a proxy to the API.

Where to deploy: choosing between the options

Use Vercel or Netlify for the React frontend, Render or Railway for a quick backend, EC2 with Docker to learn real operations, and ECS, Kubernetes or Lambda when the team and scale need them.

The comparison table

Effort means the work you do after the code is ready; control means how much of the stack you can change. Prices change, so compare current pricing yourself.

OptionBest forEffortControlWatch out for
Vercel / NetlifyReact and other frontends, static sitesVery lowLowNot for long-running Java servers
Render / RailwayDemos, MVPs, small backendsLowMediumFree plans sleep; plans and limits change
EC2 + Docker + NginxLearning operations, cheap full controlHighFullYou patch, secure, back up and monitor
Elastic BeanstalkAWS-native PaaS for a JAR or containerMediumMediumOlder style; less used in new projects
ECS on FargateContainers on AWS without servers; teams on AWSMedium to highHighMany AWS pieces (ALB, roles, VPC)
Kubernetes (EKS, GKE, AKS)Many services, big teams, portabilityVery highVery highComplexity; needs a platform team
LambdaEvent-driven tasks, spiky small workloadsMediumMediumJava cold starts, timeouts, different design

How to decide

  1. Who runs it? No operations person: prefer a PaaS. A team with DevOps skills: ECS, Kubernetes, or EC2 with automation.
  2. How important is uptime? A demo may sleep. A paying customer's system needs two instances in two zones behind a load balancer, a Multi-AZ database and monitoring.
  3. What does it cost over time? A PaaS saves your time at a higher price per unit; a server saves money and costs time. At small scale, time is usually the more expensive part.
  4. What must you learn? For a job, EC2 plus Docker plus Nginx teaches the most; a PaaS teaches the least about operations but gets you live fastest.

A good plan for Project 3

  • Frontend: Vercel or Netlify, with your own domain.
  • Backend: EC2 with Docker, Nginx and HTTPS (this is the impressive, learnable path), or Render if you only need it live quickly. You can do both: Render first, EC2 second.
  • Database: RDS, or the platform's PostgreSQL; a container database only for practice.
  • Pipeline: the GitHub Actions workflow from Module 4 that builds, tests, pushes the image and deploys.

Convention: keep the choice reversible

A Docker image, environment-variable configuration and a managed Postgres (a standard database) let you move from Render to EC2 to ECS with small changes. Avoid features that lock you to one platform unless they give a clear benefit.

Mistake: choosing the most complex option to look impressive

Kubernetes for one small app is a poor choice, and interviewers know it. It is better to deploy something simple well (HTTPS, health checks, backups, rollback) and explain the trade-offs.

Interview: "How would you deploy this application?"

  • Ask about traffic, uptime needs, team skills and budget first. Then propose: static frontend on a CDN platform; backend as a container on ECS or EC2 behind a load balancer; managed Postgres; CI/CD with immutable image tags; monitoring and rollback.

Domains and DNS: pointing your own name at the app

Buy a domain from a registrar, then create DNS records: an A record from api to your Elastic IP and a CNAME from shop (or www) to Vercel or Netlify; changes appear after caches expire, and you check them with dig.

Buying a domain

A domain name (such as example.com) is rented yearly from a registrar (GoDaddy, Namecheap, Hostinger, BigRock, Route 53, Cloudflare Registrar and many others; .com, .in and .dev are examples of top-level domains). Prices differ between registrars and between the first year and renewals, so compare the renewal price and check that WHOIS privacy (hiding your personal details from the public record) is included. Use a strong password and MFA on the registrar account: whoever controls the domain controls your email and website. Turn on auto-renew, or you may lose the name.

How DNS works (resolvers, name servers, root and TLD servers) is explained in Module 2. Here we only manage records.

The records you need

TypeMapsExample
AA name to an IPv4 addressapi → 203.0.113.25
AAAAA name to an IPv6 addressapi → an IPv6 address
CNAMEA name to another name (an alias)www → your-site.netlify.app
TXTText: ownership proofs, SPF/DKIM for emailVerification strings
MXWhere email for the domain goesSet by your mail provider
NSWhich name servers are in charge of the zoneSet at the registrar

Each record has a TTL (time to live, in seconds): how long resolvers may cache the answer. The apex (or root) is the bare domain example.com; a subdomain is api.example.com. A CNAME is not allowed at the apex, because the apex must carry other records (NS, MX). Providers solve this with special alias-style records (ALIAS, ANAME or "CNAME flattening", and Route 53 "alias records"), or you can send the apex to an address given by the platform.

A plan for Project 3

NameTypeValuePurpose
apiAThe EC2 Elastic IPSpring Boot behind Nginx
shop or wwwCNAMEThe target Vercel/Netlify showsReact frontend
apexALIAS/A, as the platform instructsPlatform's valueRedirect or serve the frontend

Add the domain in the Vercel or Netlify project first; it shows the exact records and checks them. Then open your DNS provider's panel and create the records. Do not copy values from a blog: use the ones the platform shows today.

Using Cloudflare DNS (brief)

You can host your DNS at Cloudflare: add the site, then change the name servers at the registrar to the two Cloudflare gives you. Cloudflare has a free plan (check current terms) and a fast DNS panel. Each record has a proxy switch:

  • DNS only (grey cloud): the record answers with your real IP. The simplest option while learning, and what certbot expects.
  • Proxied (orange cloud): traffic passes through Cloudflare, which hides your IP and adds caching and protection. Then use SSL mode "Full (strict)" so Cloudflare-to-origin traffic is encrypted and validated. "Flexible" mode leaves the last hop unencrypted and often causes redirect loops.

Records for Vercel/Netlify are usually best left DNS-only, so those platforms can manage certificates themselves.

Propagation and checking

There is no global switch that "propagates". Each resolver caches an old answer until its TTL ends, so a change can appear in seconds for you and take hours for someone else. If you plan a move, lower the TTL a day before. Check what the world sees:

$ dig +short api.example.com
203.0.113.25
$ dig +short shop.example.com CNAME
cname.vercel-dns.com.
$ dig api.example.com @1.1.1.1 +short      # ask a specific public resolver
$ dig +short NS example.com
$ nslookup api.example.com                  # works on Windows too

Your own machine may also cache a failed lookup; if it still shows the old answer, wait a little or flush the local DNS cache (ipconfig /flushdns on Windows).

Mistakes

  • Typing the full name in the host field (api.example.com.example.com). Many panels want only api.
  • Pointing the A record at the instance's temporary public IP instead of the Elastic IP; it changes at the next stop and start.
  • Leaving an old A record next to a new CNAME for the same name (a name cannot be both).
  • Running certbot before the record exists.

Edge cases

  • NXDOMAIN means the name does not exist in DNS: a typo, a missing record, or the name servers are not changed yet.
  • A CAA record, if present, lists the authorities allowed to issue certificates; it must allow letsencrypt.org or issuance fails.
  • www and the apex are different names; set up both and redirect one to the other.

Interview: "Difference between A and CNAME records? Why not CNAME at the root?"

  • A maps a name to an IP; CNAME maps a name to another name. A CNAME cannot coexist with other records for the same name, and the apex needs NS/SOA/MX, so use ALIAS/ANAME/flattening there.
  • TTL and caching explain "propagation".

Deployment strategies: recreate, rolling, blue-green, canary and rollback

A deployment strategy decides how the new version replaces the old one: recreate (stop, then start), rolling (replace one by one), blue-green (switch between two full copies) or canary (send a small share of traffic first); whatever you pick, keep the previous image tag so you can go back fast.

The four strategies

StrategyHow it worksDowntimeExtra capacity
RecreateStop the old version, start the new oneYes, seconds to minutesNone
RollingReplace instances a few at a time; old and new run together brieflyNone if done rightA little
Blue-greenRun the new version (green) beside the old (blue), test it, then switch all trafficNoneA full second copy
CanarySend a small percentage of users to the new version, watch metrics, then increaseNoneA little
  • Recreate is what docker compose up -d does on a single server. It is simple and fine for a small app where a few seconds of downtime at a quiet hour is acceptable. It is also the only option when two versions cannot run at the same time (for example an incompatible schema change).
  • Rolling is the default in Kubernetes Deployments, ECS services and Elastic Beanstalk. With a load balancer in front, each instance is taken out of rotation, updated, health-checked and put back. During the update both versions serve users, so they must be compatible with each other and with the database.
  • Blue-green gives instant rollback: if green misbehaves, switch the load balancer or the Nginx upstream back to blue. It costs double capacity during the switch and needs care with shared databases.
  • Canary limits the damage of a bad release to a few users and gives real traffic evidence. It needs a router that can split traffic and good metrics (Module 6). A related tool is a feature flag: code ships dark and is switched on for chosen users without a new deployment.

Zero downtime on one server (a blue-green sketch)

With one EC2 server you can still avoid downtime by starting the new container on a second port, checking it, and switching Nginx. Assume Nginx proxies to an upstream defined in a small file:

upstream shop_backend {
    server 127.0.0.1:8080;      # blue is live; green would be 8081
}
$ docker run -d --name shop-green --env-file /opt/shop/.env \
    -p 127.0.0.1:8081:8080 ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:sha-9b1d4e7
$ curl -fsS http://127.0.0.1:8081/actuator/health          # wait until UP
