$ sudo sed -i 's/127.0.0.1:8080/127.0.0.1:8081/' /etc/nginx/conf.d/shop-upstream.conf
$ sudo nginx -t && sudo systemctl reload nginx          # reload does not drop connections
$ docker stop shop-blue                                     # keep it for a while for rollback

In the site config use proxy_pass http://shop_backend;. This is a manual teaching example; in a real team a load balancer with target groups, or Kubernetes, automates the same idea.

Zero-downtime basics in Spring Boot

  • Readiness: traffic should reach an instance only after it started (health checks and probes, Module 6).
  • Graceful shutdown: when the old instance is told to stop, finish in-flight requests first. Spring Boot supports this:
server.shutdown=graceful
spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase=30s
  • Connection draining: the load balancer stops sending new requests to an instance being removed and lets existing ones finish (a "deregistration delay").
  • Backward-compatible database changes (the expand and contract pattern): release 1 adds a nullable column; release 2 starts using it; a later release removes the old column. Then old and new code both work against the same schema, which makes rolling updates and rollbacks safe. Renaming or dropping a column in a single step breaks the version that is still running.

Rollback

Rollback should be boring and fast. Rules that make it so:

  1. Tag images immutably (sha-9b1d4e7, 1.4.2); never deploy latest.
  2. Record the previously running tag on every deployment (the runbook's .previous_tag file).
  3. Keep several old images in the registry and on the server (careful with docker image prune -a).
  4. Make the rollback a command or a button (a script, a workflow_dispatch run, a platform "redeploy previous").
  5. Keep database migrations backward compatible. If a release changed data irreversibly, an image rollback alone will not help; that is why the migration discipline matters.
  6. Practise it before you need it.

Mistakes

  • Dropping or renaming a column in the same release that stops using it.
  • Calling a deployment successful because the container started, without a health check.
  • No graceful shutdown: users see errors for requests that were in progress.
  • Deploying a big release on Friday evening with no rollback plan.

In real projects

Most teams use rolling updates by default, blue-green for risky releases, and canary or feature flags for large user bases. A deployment typically has a health-check gate, a smoke test, and dashboards watched for the first minutes after release.

Interview: "Blue-green vs canary vs rolling?"

  • Rolling: gradual replacement, cheap, mixed versions for a while. Blue-green: two full environments, instant switch and rollback, double cost. Canary: small traffic slice with metrics before full rollout.
  • Mention database compatibility and health checks.

Production deployment checklist

Before you call a deployment production-ready, check security, configuration, data, runtime, networking, observability, release process and cost, using this list as a final pass.

How to use it

Go through the list once before the first launch and again before a major change. Every line is a small failure that has hurt someone. A "no" is not always wrong; it should be a decision you can explain.

Security

  • Root account has MFA, and no root access keys exist. Daily work uses a non-root identity.
  • SSH is limited to your IP (or replaced by Session Manager); password login is off; keys are not shared.
  • Only ports 80 and 443 are public. The app port and the database port are private.
  • The database is not publicly accessible. The app uses its own limited database user.
  • No secrets in Git, images or frontend code. The .env file has mode 600.
  • AWS access uses roles; policies name exact resources; S3 buckets block public access.
  • The OS gets security updates (unattended-upgrades or a patch routine). Images are rebuilt on newer base images regularly.
  • Swagger UI, H2 console and sensitive Actuator endpoints are off or protected in production.

Configuration

  • All environment-specific values come from environment variables or a config service, per environment.
  • The production profile is active (SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=prod); ddl-auto is validate or none; migrations run through Flyway or Liquibase.
  • CORS allows exactly the real frontend origins, not * with credentials.
  • Time zone, character set and locale are set on purpose (store timestamps in UTC).

Data

  • Automated backups are on, and you have tested a restore.
  • The database has enough connections for the pool sizes times the number of instances.
  • Uploaded files live in S3 or a durable volume, not in the container's temporary disk.

Runtime

  • Memory is sized: MaxRAMPercentage is set, container limits match, and swap exists on tiny machines.
  • Restart policy is set; the app and Docker start on boot; you tested a reboot.
  • Graceful shutdown is on. Health endpoints (liveness and readiness) exist.
  • Logs go to stdout, are rotated (max-size, max-file), and the disk cannot fill silently.

Networking and domain

  • DNS records are correct; HTTPS works; HTTP redirects to HTTPS; the certificate renewal was tested with certbot renew --dry-run.
  • Nginx forwards the right headers; upload size and timeouts match your needs.
  • The Elastic IP is attached (the address survives restarts).

Observability

  • An external uptime monitor checks the public health URL and alerts you by email, Slack or Telegram.
  • You can read logs quickly; important errors are noticed without a user complaint.
  • Basic metrics (CPU, memory, disk, request errors, latency) are visible (Module 6).

Release process

  • CI runs tests on every pull request; deployment comes only from the main branch after tests pass.
  • Images carry immutable tags. The previous tag is known and rollback is rehearsed.
  • A smoke test runs after each deployment.
  • The runbook (how to deploy, roll back, and who to call) is written down and stored in the repository.

Cost

  • A budget alert exists and goes to an email you read.
  • You know what each resource costs per month (from the current pricing pages), and unused resources are deleted: stopped servers with disks, old snapshots, unattached Elastic IPs, forgotten databases.

Tip: keep the checklist in the repository

Put a DEPLOYMENT.md file next to the code with the checklist and the runbook commands. Reviewers can then check it, and a new team member can deploy on day one.

Interview: "What do you check before releasing to production?"

  • Tests and migrations are green and backward compatible; config and secrets are correct for the environment; health checks and monitoring are in place; there is a rollback plan; the deployment is done at a quiet time with a smoke test afterwards.

Interview questions — Cloud and deployment

What are IaaS, PaaS and SaaS? Give one example of each.

IaaS rents virtual infrastructure (servers, disks, networks): you manage the OS and everything above it; example EC2. PaaS rents a platform to run your code: you manage the app and its settings; examples Render, Railway, Elastic Beanstalk. SaaS is finished software you just use: Gmail, Jira, Slack. The further toward SaaS, the less you manage and the less you can change.

What is the shared-responsibility model?

The provider secures the cloud itself (buildings, hardware, virtualisation). You secure what you put in it: data, accounts and permissions, network rules, and, on EC2, the OS and application. The split changes with the service: for RDS, AWS patches the engine; for EC2, you patch the OS. Typical customer errors are public buckets, open security groups and leaked keys.

What is the difference between a region and an availability zone? How do you get high availability?

A region is a geographic area (Mumbai, ap-south-1); an availability zone is an isolated group of data centres inside it. Spread instances over at least two AZs behind a load balancer and enable Multi-AZ on the database, so a failure of one zone does not stop the app. Regions are independent, so cross-region designs cost more and are used only when needed.

What do you do first in a new AWS account?

Enable MFA on the root user and stop using it, create an IAM Identity Center or IAM user (with MFA) for daily work, grant least privilege, avoid access keys, set a budget with email alerts, and enable CloudTrail. Check the current free-tier terms instead of assuming. These steps prevent the most common incidents: leaked keys and surprise bills.

Name common surprise costs on AWS and how to avoid them.

Instances left running, EBS volumes and snapshots that remain after termination, Elastic IPs and public IPv4 addresses, NAT gateways, load balancers, data transfer, and RDS instances left on. Avoid them with budgets and alerts, tags, a cleanup routine after labs, a weekly look at the bill by service and region, and infrastructure as code so terraform destroy removes everything.

What is the difference between stopping and terminating an EC2 instance?

Stopping shuts the instance down but keeps it and its EBS volumes; you stop paying for compute but still pay for storage, and the automatic public IP changes on the next start. Terminating deletes the instance and, by default, its root volume; it cannot be undone. Data on instance-store disks is lost on stop.

How do you let only your application reach the RDS database?

Create the database with Public access off, in the same VPC as the app. In its security group add an inbound rule for port 5432 whose source is the application's security group, not an IP range. Then only instances in that group can connect. Use a dedicated database user and pass the credentials through environment variables or a secrets service.

RDS Multi-AZ vs read replica?

Multi-AZ keeps a synchronous standby in another AZ for automatic failover; it is for availability and you cannot read from the standby. A read replica is an asynchronously updated copy with its own endpoint that serves read queries to scale reads; it can lag behind. They solve different problems and can be used together.

How does an app on EC2 call S3 without storing access keys?

Create an IAM role with a least-privilege policy and attach it to the instance (through an instance profile). The AWS SDK's default credentials chain gets temporary, automatically rotated credentials from the instance metadata service. Nothing is written to disk or Git. Inside Docker you may need the metadata hop limit set to 2.

What is a presigned URL and why use it?

It is an S3 URL signed with your server's credentials that allows one operation (GET or PUT) on one object until it expires. The browser uploads or downloads directly to and from a private bucket, so large files do not pass through your server, and you do not expose credentials or make the bucket public. Keep the expiry short and generate the object key on the server.

Walk me through putting a Spring Boot app on a fresh EC2 server with HTTPS.

Launch Ubuntu with a security group (SSH from my IP, 80 and 443 open), attach an Elastic IP, SSH in, install Docker, log in to the registry and pull the tagged image, run it with Compose using an env file and a port bound to 127.0.0.1:8080, install Nginx as a reverse proxy, create a DNS A record to the Elastic IP, run certbot --nginx -d api.example.com, verify renewal with a dry run, and enable restart policies. Finish with logs, a health check, and a deploy and rollback script.

Why put Nginx in front of Spring Boot?

It terminates TLS, serves on ports 80/443 without running Java as a privileged user, redirects HTTP to HTTPS, can add compression, request size limits, caching and rate limiting, and hides the app port. It also lets you switch between app versions without changing the public address. The app then trusts the X-Forwarded-* headers (server.forward-headers-strategy).

Why does a React app on Vercel or Netlify return 404 on refresh, and how do you fix it?

Client-side routes such as /orders/42 do not exist as files; the server finds nothing and returns 404. Add a rewrite sending all unknown paths to index.html (vercel.json rewrites, or a Netlify redirect with status 200), and React Router renders the right page. Also remember that VITE_ variables are fixed at build time.

Compare recreate, rolling, blue-green and canary deployments. How do you roll back?

Recreate: stop old, start new, with downtime. Rolling: replace instances gradually behind a load balancer. Blue-green: run a full new copy and switch traffic, with instant rollback and double capacity. Canary: send a small share of traffic to the new version first. Rollback is easy when images have immutable tags, the previous tag is known, the switch is scripted, and database changes are backward compatible.

Your site works on its Elastic IP but not on the domain. What do you check?

Run dig +short name to see what DNS returns. Check the record type and host field (only api, not the full name), that it points to the Elastic IP, the TTL and cached answers, and that Nginx has a matching server_name. For HTTPS, check certbot succeeded, port 443 is open, and the certificate covers that name. If the domain is proxied by Cloudflare, check the SSL mode.

Cheat sheet — Cloud and deployment

  • IaaS / PaaS / SaaS servers (EC2) / platform for code (Render) / finished software (Gmail)
  • Region vs AZ geographic area (ap-south-1 Mumbai, ap-south-2 Hyderabad) / isolated data centres inside it
  • Shared responsibility provider secures the cloud; you secure what you put in it
  • Day one root MFA, no root keys, non-root user, budget alert, read the current free-tier terms
  • Surprise costs running EC2, orphan EBS, Elastic IPs, NAT gateway, load balancer, RDS, data transfer
  • Instance name t3.micro = family, generation, size; g = Graviton (ARM)
  • Security group stateful firewall; SSH from my IP only; open 80 and 443; never 8080 or 5432
  • Key pair chmod 400 key.pem; ssh -i key.pem ubuntu@IP (Amazon Linux: ec2-user)
  • Stop vs terminate stop keeps disks and bills storage; terminate deletes the instance
  • Elastic IP static public IPv4; attach it before DNS; release when unused
  • Docker on Ubuntu install, usermod -aG docker $USER, re-login, docker compose up -d
  • Compose port "127.0.0.1:8080:8080" and restart: unless-stopped
  • Nginx proxy_pass http://127.0.0.1:8080 plus X-Forwarded headers; nginx -t then reload
  • HTTPS certbot --nginx -d api.example.com; test certbot renew --dry-run
  • Update change the image tag, compose pull api && compose up -d api, health check, roll back on failure
  • RDS managed Postgres; public access off; port 5432 only from the app's security group
  • Spring datasource env vars SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL, _USERNAME, _PASSWORD
  • S3 private bucket, Block Public Access on, keys generated by the server
  • Presigned URL short-lived signed link for one GET or PUT; needs bucket CORS for browsers
  • IAM user / group / role / policy; default deny; least privilege; role for EC2 instead of keys
  • Policy ARNs object actions on bucket/*; s3:ListBucket on bucket
  • Vercel / Netlify build npm run build, output dist, SPA rewrite, VITE_ vars at build time
  • Render / Railway server.port=${PORT:8080}, JDBC URL not postgres://, free plans may sleep
  • DNS A = name to IP; CNAME = name to name (not at the apex); check with dig +short
  • Strategies recreate, rolling, blue-green, canary; immutable tags make rollback easy
  • Zero downtime readiness checks, graceful shutdown, backward-compatible migrations

Practice — Cloud and deployment

  1. Secure the account (easy). Turn on MFA for root, create a daily-use user or Identity Center user with MFA, and create a monthly budget with email alerts. Done when: you can sign in as the new user, aws sts get-caller-identity shows it, and a test alert email arrives.
  2. Read the bill (easy). Open Bills and Cost Explorer; list the services and regions you used this week. Done when: you can say what each line is and what you will delete.
  3. First server (easy). Launch an Ubuntu EC2 instance with a security group that allows SSH only from your IP, SSH in, install Nginx, and open the default page in a browser. Done when: you can explain each security group rule, and you then terminate the instance and check it is gone in all regions.
  4. Run your container (medium). On a new instance, install Docker, pull your Spring Boot image from GHCR, and run it with Compose on 127.0.0.1:8080. Done when: curl localhost:8080/actuator/health on the server returns UP, and port 8080 is unreachable from your laptop.
  5. Proxy, DNS and HTTPS (medium). Attach an Elastic IP, configure Nginx, create an A record for api.yourdomain, and run certbot. Done when: the browser shows a valid padlock and certbot renew --dry-run passes.
  6. Managed database (medium). Create an RDS PostgreSQL instance that is not public, allow 5432 only from the EC2 security group, create a shop_app user, and connect the app through environment variables. Done when: the app starts, Flyway runs, and a connection attempt from your laptop times out.
  7. S3 uploads (medium). Create a private bucket, an IAM policy limited to it, and an instance role. Add presigned PUT and GET endpoints to Spring Boot and a small React upload form with the bucket CORS rules. Done when: you upload from the browser and view the file through a presigned link, and the object URL without a signature returns AccessDenied.
  8. Update and roll back (medium to hard). Write the deploy.sh from the runbook, deploy a new tag, then deploy a deliberately broken one (for example a missing environment variable). Done when: the script detects the unhealthy app and returns to the previous tag by itself.
  9. Deploy Project 3 (hard). Deploy the React frontend on Vercel or Netlify and Spring Boot with PostgreSQL on EC2 (or Render), under your own domain with HTTPS, with CI/CD from Module 4. Done when: Project 3 is live on a public HTTPS address, and you can explain everything that happens between git push and the new version going live (CI, tests, image build, registry, deploy, health check, DNS and TLS in the request path, and how to roll back).
6

Module 6·After month 12Later

Monitoring and later topics

11 topics · about 1 h of reading

Deploying is only half the job. Once real users arrive, you need to know when the app is down, why it crashed, how slow it is, and how much room is left. This module covers the everyday skills first: reading logs on a server, finding out why an app died, health checks and uptime alerts. These are the Must skills for a backend developer.

The second half is Later material that you do not need for your first job but will meet soon after: Prometheus and Grafana for metrics, Kubernetes for running many containers, and Terraform for creating infrastructure from code. Learn the first half now and read the second half to know what exists and how to talk about it. It builds on Docker, CI/CD and Cloud and deployment, and on Actuator from Spring Boot · Module 6.

  • Read and search logs from Docker, systemd and files, and keep them from filling the disk.
  • Diagnose the usual crashes: out of memory, port in use, database refused, missing configuration.
  • Explain logs, metrics and traces, and the four golden signals.
  • Set up health checks, uptime monitoring and alerts to your phone.
  • Run Prometheus and Grafana with Spring Boot and write basic PromQL.
  • Explain Kubernetes objects, read a Deployment and Service YAML, and describe what Terraform does.

Reading your app's logs on a server

Run docker compose logs -f --tail 100 api (or journalctl -u shop -f for a systemd service) to follow the application log, search it with grep and less, and rotate logs so they cannot fill the disk.

Where logs live

A log is a time-ordered text record of what the application did and what went wrong. Where you find it depends on how the app runs:

How it runsWhere the log isCommand
Docker containerCaptured from stdout/stderr by Dockerdocker logs
Docker ComposeSame, grouped by servicedocker compose logs
systemd service (a JAR)The journaljournalctl -u shop
Plain process with file loggingA file such as /var/log/shop/app.logtail, less
Nginx/var/log/nginx/access.log and error.logtail, grep
PaaS (Render, Railway)The platform's log viewerDashboard or CLI

Spring Boot writes to the console (stdout) by default. In containers that is exactly right: the platform collects stdout, and the app does not manage files. This is one of the twelve-factor ideas: treat logs as an event stream.

Docker and Compose

$ docker logs shop-api                      # everything since the container started
$ docker logs --tail 100 shop-api           # the last 100 lines
$ docker logs -f --tail 100 shop-api        # follow new lines (Ctrl+C to stop)
$ docker logs --since 10m shop-api          # the last 10 minutes
$ docker logs -t shop-api | tail            # add Docker's timestamps

$ cd /opt/shop
$ docker compose logs -f --tail 100 api     # one service
$ docker compose logs -f                    # all services, interleaved
$ docker compose logs --no-color --since 1h api > api.log   # save for sharing

Note that docker logs shows logs of a container that still exists, even if stopped. When a container is removed (for example by docker compose down or a recreate), its json log file is removed with it. After a crash, look first, then restart.

systemd and the journal

$ journalctl -u shop -f                         # follow
$ journalctl -u shop -n 200 --no-pager          # last 200 lines
$ journalctl -u shop --since "1 hour ago"
$ journalctl -u shop --since "2026-11-11 09:00" --until "2026-11-11 09:30"
$ journalctl -u shop -p err                     # only errors and worse
$ journalctl -b -1                              # the previous boot, useful after a crash/reboot
$ journalctl -u docker --since today            # logs of the Docker service itself

Add sudo if your user is not in the systemd-journal group. The journal's size can be capped with SystemMaxUse=500M in /etc/systemd/journald.conf.

Searching logs: grep, less, awk

$ docker compose logs api | grep -i error                  # case-insensitive
$ docker compose logs api | grep -C 5 "NullPointerException"  # 5 lines around each match
$ docker compose logs api | grep -c "WARN"                 # count matches
$ grep -r "OrderService" /var/log/shop/ | tail             # search several files
$ zgrep "ERROR" /var/log/shop/app.log.*.gz                 # search rotated, compressed logs
$ less +F /var/log/shop/app.log                            # follow inside less; Ctrl+C to stop following

# Nginx access log: count responses per status code
$ awk '{print $9}' /var/log/nginx/access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn
# the busiest client IPs
$ awk '{print $1}' /var/log/nginx/access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head

Inside less: / then a word searches forward, n jumps to the next match, G goes to the end, g to the start, q quits. In the default Nginx log format the status code is the 9th space-separated field, which is why the awk line works; if you change the log format, adjust it.

Log levels and Spring Boot settings

Levels from quiet to loud are ERROR, WARN, INFO, DEBUG, TRACE. Production usually runs at INFO for your code and WARN for libraries. Set levels in properties or environment variables:

logging.level.root=WARN
logging.level.com.shop=INFO
# only if you must log to a file (not needed in containers):
logging.file.name=/var/log/shop/app.log
logging.logback.rollingpolicy.max-file-size=20MB
logging.logback.rollingpolicy.max-history=14

With Actuator's loggers endpoint exposed (and protected!), you can change a level on a running app without restarting, which is handy for a short debugging session:

$ curl -X POST -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
    -d '{"configuredLevel":"DEBUG"}' http://127.0.0.1:8080/actuator/loggers/com.shop.orders
$ curl -X POST -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
    -d '{"configuredLevel":null}' http://127.0.0.1:8080/actuator/loggers/com.shop.orders

Version note: structured logging

Spring Boot 3.4 and later (including 4.x) can write logs as JSON for log tools using properties such as logging.structured.format.console=ecs (other formats include logstash). On older 3.x versions use a Logback encoder library. JSON logs are far easier to search in a central system.

Stop logs from filling the disk

A full disk kills a server: the database cannot write, the app crashes, even SSH can misbehave. Docker's default json-file driver keeps logs forever unless you limit them. Set limits per service in Compose (as in the runbook), or for all containers in /etc/docker/daemon.json:

{
  "log-driver": "json-file",
  "log-opts": { "max-size": "10m", "max-file": "3" }
}

Restart Docker after editing (sudo systemctl restart docker); the new default applies only to containers created afterwards. For plain log files, logrotate renames, compresses and deletes old files on a schedule:

/var/log/shop/*.log {
    daily
    rotate 14
    compress
    missingok
    notifempty
    copytruncate
}

copytruncate copies the file and empties the original, so a running app that keeps the file open continues to write without a restart. Check space regularly with df -h, du -sh /var/log/* and docker system df.

Centralised logging, in one paragraph

With one server, SSH and grep are enough. With many servers or containers, logs vanish when a container is replaced and searching ten machines by hand is painful. A centralised logging system ships every log line to one searchable place. Two popular stacks are ELK (Elasticsearch stores and indexes, Logstash or Beats ship, Kibana searches and charts) and Loki with Grafana (an agent such as Promtail or Grafana Alloy ships logs; Loki indexes only labels, so it is lighter; you search in Grafana). On AWS, CloudWatch Logs does this for you. Use structured JSON logs and a correlation (request) ID in every line so one request can be followed across services.

Rule: never log secrets or personal data

Do not log passwords, JWTs, API keys, full card numbers or whole request bodies. Logs are copied, shared and kept for months, and many people can read them.

Mistakes

  • Running at DEBUG in production: huge logs, slower app, leaked data.
  • Restarting or recreating the container before reading the log of the crash.
  • Using System.out.println or e.printStackTrace() instead of a logger with levels and timestamps.
  • No limit on Docker log size, then "No space left on device" months later.

Interview: "How do you debug a production problem using logs?"

  • Find the time window, follow or filter the logs (--since, grep -C), find the first error (not the last), use a request ID to follow one request, check the stack trace's "Caused by".
  • Mention log levels, rotation, structured logs and centralised logging for many servers.

Why did my app crash? A step-by-step walkthrough

Check whether the container or service is running and how it exited, read the lines just before the death, then match the symptom to one of the usual causes: killed for memory (exit 137), port already in use, database refused, missing environment variable, or a full disk.

The method

Do not guess and do not restart blindly. Follow the same order every time:

$ docker compose ps -a                        # status and exit code (Exited (137) ...)
$ docker inspect -f '{{.State.ExitCode}} oom={{.State.OOMKilled}}' shop-api-1
137 oom=true
$ docker compose logs --tail 200 api          # the end of the log
$ df -h / ; free -h                           # disk and memory right now

Exit codes tell a lot. 0 means a clean exit. 1 means the application failed (an exception during startup). 137 means the process received SIGKILL (128 + 9), most often from the out-of-memory killer. 143 means SIGTERM (128 + 15): a normal stop request from docker stop or a deploy.

Cause 1: the process was killed for memory (exit 137)

Symptom: the container restarts at random times, exit code 137, OOMKilled is true, and the app log simply stops with no stack trace, because the kernel killed the process instantly.

$ sudo dmesg -T | grep -i -E "out of memory|killed process"
[Tue Nov 11 09:14:02 2026] Out of memory: Killed process 4218 (java) total-vm:3482112kB ...

Why: the JVM uses more than heap (metaspace, thread stacks, buffers), and the machine or container limit is smaller than the total. Fix: give the JVM less than the limit with -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75 (in JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS), move to a bigger instance, add swap as a safety net, stop other memory eaters, and look for a leak.

Do not confuse this with a Java-level java.lang.OutOfMemoryError: Java heap space, which is in the log with a stack trace: the heap itself was too small or leaking. To study it, run with -XX:+HeapDumpOnOutOfMemoryError -XX:HeapDumpPath=/tmp and open the dump in a tool such as Eclipse MAT or VisualVM (see Core Java · Module 7).

Cause 2: the port is already in use

***************************
APPLICATION FAILED TO START
***************************

Description:

Web server failed to start. Port 8080 was already in use.

Action:

Identify and stop the process that's listening on port 8080 or configure
this application to listen on another port.

Find the owner:

$ sudo ss -ltnp | grep ':8080'
LISTEN 0 100 *:8080 *:* users:(("java",pid=2311,fd=9))
$ docker ps --format '{{.Names}} {{.Ports}}' | grep 8080

Typical reasons: an old copy of the app (a forgotten manual java -jar), two containers publishing the same host port, or a new container started before the old one released the port. Stop the other process, or map a different host port.

Cause 3: the database refuses the connection

HikariPool-1 - Exception during pool initialization.
org.postgresql.util.PSQLException: Connection to localhost:5432 refused.
Check that the hostname and port are correct and that the postmaster is accepting TCP/IP connections.
Caused by: java.net.ConnectException: Connection refused

Read the host in the message. Check, in this order:

  1. Wrong host. localhost inside a container means the container itself. Use the Compose service name (postgres) or the RDS endpoint (see Docker networks).
  2. Database not ready or not running. docker compose ps; use a healthcheck and depends_on: condition: service_healthy.
  3. Network rule. For RDS, a timeout (not "refused") usually means the security group does not allow the app's group on 5432.
  4. Credentials. password authentication failed for user means the host is reachable but the user or password is wrong. database "shop" does not exist means a wrong name.
  5. Too many connections. Pool size times instances is above the server limit.
$ docker compose exec api sh -c 'getent hosts postgres'
$ docker run --rm --network shop_default postgres:16 \
    psql -h postgres -U shop_app -d shop -c 'select 1'

Cause 4: a missing or wrong environment variable

Caused by: java.lang.IllegalArgumentException:
Could not resolve placeholder 'JWT_SECRET' in value "${JWT_SECRET}"

The code expects a variable that the container does not have. See what the container actually got, and what Compose resolved:

$ docker compose config | grep -A 12 environment     # after variable substitution
$ docker compose exec api env | grep -E "SPRING|JWT|DB_"

Common reasons: the variable is missing from .env, a typo in the name, a value with a space or special character that needs quotes, an env_file path that is wrong, or the container was not recreated after the file changed (docker compose up -d recreates only when the configuration differs, so run it again after editing, and check).

Cause 5: the disk is full

java.io.IOException: No space left on device
ERROR: could not write to file "pg_wal/xlogtemp.123": No space left on device
$ df -h
$ sudo du -xh / --max-depth=2 2>/dev/null | sort -rh | head -15
$ docker system df
$ docker system prune -f              # removes stopped containers, dangling images, unused networks
$ sudo journalctl --vacuum-size=200M  # shrink the journal

Typical culprits: unlimited container logs, old images, a growing upload folder, a database dump left in place. Fix by cleaning, then add log rotation and a disk alert so it does not return.

More causes worth a quick check

  • Wrong Java version: UnsupportedClassVersionError means the JAR was compiled for a newer Java than the runtime image provides.
  • Migration failed: Flyway reports a checksum mismatch or a failed script; the app refuses to start. Read the migration name in the log.
  • The health check kills a slow starter: an orchestrator restarts the app before it finished starting; add a startup probe or a longer initial delay.
  • Permissions: the app cannot write to a mounted folder owned by root.
  • Certificates or DNS: UnknownHostException for an external API, or SSL handshake errors, point to network or trust problems.

Tip: write a post-mortem note

After fixing, write three lines: what happened, the cause, what you changed so it will not repeat (a memory limit, an alert, a test). This habit is what turns incidents into experience, and interviewers like to hear one real story.

Edge cases

  • With restart: unless-stopped, a crash loop can restart the app every few seconds and fill the log. Use docker compose ps to see the restart count, and stop the loop to investigate.
  • The first error in a log is usually the real cause. Later errors are often side effects.
  • A Java OutOfMemoryError for "unable to create native thread" is about the OS thread or memory limit, not the heap.

Interview: "The app keeps restarting in production. What do you do?"

  • Check status and exit code, read the log before the exit, check memory and disk, then match the cause (137 OOM, port conflict, DB connection, missing config). Fix, redeploy, verify the health endpoint, and add monitoring so you hear about it first next time.

Observability: logs, metrics, traces and the golden signals

Observability means you can understand what a running system is doing from its outputs: logs say what happened, metrics say how much and how fast, traces show the path of one request; watch the four golden signals (latency, traffic, errors, saturation) to know whether users are happy.

Monitoring vs observability

Monitoring watches known things: "is the site up, is CPU above 90%?". It answers the questions you thought of in advance. Observability is the ability to ask new questions about a live system ("why are orders from one city slow since 3 pm?") using the data it emits, without shipping new code first. You need both: monitoring tells you something is wrong, observability helps you find out why.

The three pillars

PillarWhat it isGood forTypical tools
LogsTimestamped events with details"What exactly happened in this request?"Docker logs, ELK, Loki, CloudWatch
MetricsNumbers sampled over time (counters, gauges, histograms)Trends, dashboards, alertsPrometheus, Grafana, CloudWatch
TracesThe timeline of one request across services, as nested spans"Which service made this slow?"OpenTelemetry, Zipkin, Jaeger, Tempo

Metrics are cheap and compact, so they power alerts. Logs are rich but heavy. Traces matter most in microservices, where one user click touches many services; see Spring Boot · Module 7 for Micrometer Tracing and distributed tracing. The three connect through shared identifiers: a trace ID printed in each log line lets you jump from a slow trace to its logs.

The four golden signals

Google's Site Reliability Engineering book names four signals that describe the health of almost any service. If you can watch only four things, watch these.

Latency
How long requests take. Look at percentiles (p50, p95, p99), not the average, because the slowest 1% of users matter. Separate the latency of failed requests from successful ones.
Traffic
How much demand: requests per second, messages per minute.
Errors
The rate of failing requests: HTTP 5xx, exceptions, timeouts, and wrong results.
Saturation
How full the system is: CPU, memory, disk, thread pool, database connection pool, queue length. Latency rises sharply as saturation nears 100%.

Two shortcuts help you choose metrics: the RED method for request-driven services (Rate, Errors, Duration), and the USE method for resources like CPU, disk and network (Utilisation, Saturation, Errors). RED tells you how users feel; USE tells you which machine part is the bottleneck.

Golden signalSpring Boot / JVM example
Latencyhttp.server.requests timer (p95), database query time
TrafficRequest count per second per endpoint
ErrorsCount of responses with status 5xx; exceptions per minute
SaturationJVM heap used vs max, GC pause time, Hikari active connections vs max, Tomcat threads busy, CPU, disk

What to alert on

Alert on symptoms users feel, not on every cause. "Error rate above 5% for 5 minutes" and "p95 latency above one second for 10 minutes" are good alerts. "CPU above 80% for a minute" often is not: it may be fine, and it wakes you for nothing. Too many alerts cause alert fatigue, and people begin to ignore them. Every alert should be actionable, and link to a runbook that says what to check first. Terms you will hear: SLI (service level indicator: a measured number such as the percentage of successful requests), SLO (the target for it, such as 99.9% over 30 days) and the error budget (how much failure the SLO still allows, used to decide between shipping features and fixing reliability).

The path you will take in this module

Mistakes

  • Looking at average latency only; a few very slow requests hide inside it.
  • Metrics with unbounded labels (a user ID or full URL as a label): the number of time series explodes and memory runs out (high cardinality). Use the URI template /orders/{id}, not /orders/42.
  • Dashboards nobody looks at, and alerts nobody acts on.

Interview: "What is observability? What are the golden signals?"

  • Logs, metrics and traces help you explain system behaviour from outside. The golden signals are latency, traffic, errors and saturation. Use percentiles; alert on user-visible symptoms; link alerts to runbooks.
  • Mention RED for services, USE for resources, and OpenTelemetry as the vendor-neutral standard.

Health checks: Actuator, liveness vs readiness, Docker and load balancers

Spring Boot Actuator exposes /actuator/health (UP returns 200, DOWN returns 503); use it for Docker HEALTHCHECK, load-balancer target checks and uptime monitors, and split it into liveness ("restart me?") and readiness ("send me traffic?") for orchestrators.

Why health checks

A running process is not always a working application. The JVM can be alive while the database pool is exhausted or startup is not finished. A health check is a cheap URL or command that answers "is this instance OK?". Docker, load balancers, Kubernetes and monitoring tools call it on a schedule and act: stop sending traffic, restart the container, or alert you.

Actuator basics

Add spring-boot-starter-actuator to the project. The health endpoint combines many health indicators (database connectivity, disk space, Redis and so on) into one status.

<dependency>
  <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
  <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-actuator</artifactId>
</dependency>
management.endpoints.web.exposure.include=health,info
management.endpoint.health.show-details=when-authorized
management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled=true
GET /actuator/health HTTP/1.1

HTTP/1.1 200 OK
Content-Type: application/vnd.spring-boot.actuator.v3+json

{"status":"UP"}

HTTP/1.1 503 Service Unavailable

{"status":"DOWN"}

Without extra properties only the overall status is shown, which is what you want on the public internet. show-details=when-authorized shows which component failed only to authenticated users; never expose details publicly because they reveal your internals. Only the endpoints listed in exposure.include are reachable over the web, so list the minimum.

A custom health indicator

Add checks for things your app really depends on, such as a payment gateway. Keep them fast and time-limited: a slow health check can itself cause outages.

@Component
public class PaymentGatewayHealthIndicator implements HealthIndicator {

    private final PaymentClient client;

    public PaymentGatewayHealthIndicator(PaymentClient client) {
        this.client = client;
    }

    @Override
    public Health health() {
        try {
            client.ping();                      // must be quick, with a short timeout
            return Health.up().withDetail("gateway", "reachable").build();
        } catch (Exception e) {
            return Health.down(e).build();
        }
    }
}

Version note: Actuator packages in Spring Boot 4

Spring Boot 4 split Actuator into smaller modules, and some health classes moved to new packages. The idea (implement HealthIndicator, return Health) is the same, but check the Boot 4 migration notes for the exact imports if you upgrade.

Liveness vs readiness

Two different questions, and mixing them causes outages.

LivenessReadiness
QuestionIs the process stuck or broken beyond repair?Can it serve requests right now?
If it failsThe orchestrator restarts the containerThe instance is removed from the load balancer; no restart
Should includeOnly the app's own state (not external services)Dependencies the app needs to work, such as the database
Spring URL/actuator/health/liveness/actuator/health/readiness

If liveness included the database and the database had a short outage, an orchestrator would restart every app instance in a loop and make the incident worse. Readiness is the right place for the database: the instance stops receiving traffic until the database returns, then rejoins. Spring Boot enables these two groups automatically when it detects Kubernetes; elsewhere turn them on with management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled=true. Choose what the readiness group contains:

management.endpoint.health.group.readiness.include=readinessState,db
management.endpoint.health.group.liveness.include=livenessState

Spring also publishes the state during shutdown: when the app is stopping, readiness turns REFUSING_TRAFFIC first, so a load balancer stops sending new requests (pair this with server.shutdown=graceful).

Docker HEALTHCHECK and Compose

Docker can run a command inside the container and mark it healthy or unhealthy. The image needs the tool you call; slim images may lack curl, so install it or use another tool that exists there.

HEALTHCHECK --interval=30s --timeout=3s --start-period=60s --retries=3 \
  CMD curl -fsS http://localhost:8080/actuator/health || exit 1
services:
  api:
    image: ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:${IMAGE_TAG}
    healthcheck:
      test: ["CMD", "curl", "-fsS", "http://localhost:8080/actuator/health"]
      interval: 30s
      timeout: 3s
      retries: 3
      start_period: 60s        # failures during startup do not count
  worker:
    image: ghcr.io/asha/shop-worker:${IMAGE_TAG}
    depends_on:
      api:
        condition: service_healthy   # start only after api is healthy

Plain Docker marks a container unhealthy but does not restart it by itself (Docker Swarm and orchestrators do). Use docker ps to see (healthy), and combine with an uptime monitor.

Load balancers and platforms

  • AWS ALB: each target group has a health check path (/actuator/health or the readiness URL), an interval, and thresholds. Instances failing the check are taken out of rotation.
  • Render, Railway and others: set the health check path in service settings; a new deployment receives traffic only after it passes, which gives zero-downtime deploys.
  • Kubernetes: liveness, readiness and startup probes (next articles).

Security of health endpoints

  • If you use Spring Security, permit /actuator/health/** without login, and protect other Actuator endpoints with a role. See Spring Boot · Module 4.
  • Better still, put Actuator on a separate port that is never published: management.server.port=8081. Then probes and Prometheus use the internal network, and nothing sensitive is reachable through Nginx. (If you move the port, point your Docker or Kubernetes checks to it.)
  • For the uptime monitor, expose only a simple public health URL, or a separate small endpoint.

Mistakes

  • Health checks that call slow external services, so one slow partner marks every instance DOWN.
  • Putting the database in liveness (restart storms).
  • Exposing * endpoints or full health details publicly.
  • A start period that is shorter than your app's startup time, so it gets killed while starting.
  • Health only checks "process is up" (a static 200) and hides a broken database.

Interview: "Liveness vs readiness probe?"

  • Liveness decides if the container should be restarted; readiness decides if it receives traffic. Keep external dependencies out of liveness. Spring Boot provides both through Actuator health groups.

Uptime monitoring and alerts to email, Slack and Telegram

An external service calls your public health URL every few minutes from outside your infrastructure and alerts you by email, Slack or Telegram when it fails, so you hear about an outage before your users tell you.

Why monitor from outside

If your monitoring runs on the same server as the app, it dies with it. An uptime monitor is a service on someone else's infrastructure that requests your URL from outside, through the same path as a real user: DNS, the internet, TLS, Nginx, and the app. If any link breaks, it notices. Popular services are UptimeRobot and Better Stack (formerly Better Uptime); both have free plans at the time of writing, with limits on check interval and number of monitors that change, so check the current terms. Healthchecks.io works the other way round for scheduled jobs: your cron job pings it after each run, and it alerts if a ping is missing (a "dead man's switch", good for nightly backups).

Set up a monitor, step by step

  1. Choose the URL: https://api.example.com/actuator/health through your real domain, over HTTPS. Add a second monitor for the frontend URL.
  2. Choose type "HTTP(s)". Better still, use a keyword check that expects "status":"UP" in the body: a custom error page with status 200 would otherwise look healthy.
  3. Set the interval as short as your plan allows (often one to five minutes).
  4. Add alert contacts: email first, then a chat tool. Test them with the service's test button.
  5. Require two failed checks in a row before alerting, so one network blip does not wake you.
  6. Add monitors for the TLS certificate expiry and, if available, the domain expiry.
  7. Optionally publish a public status page.

Alert channels

  • Email: the baseline. Put the address on your phone with push notifications.
  • Slack or Discord: create an incoming webhook for a channel; most monitoring tools already have a ready integration.
  • Telegram: create a bot with BotFather (it gives you a token), start a chat with the bot, find your chat ID, and paste both into the tool's Telegram integration. It is free and reaches your phone quickly, which suits solo developers.

You can also send your own alerts from scripts, for example at the end of a failed deployment. Treat the webhook URL and the bot token as secrets: keep them in environment variables or CI secrets.

# Slack incoming webhook
$ curl -fsS -X POST -H 'Content-type: application/json' \
    --data '{"text":"Deploy of sha-9b1d4e7 failed on production"}' "$SLACK_WEBHOOK_URL"

# Telegram bot
$ curl -fsS "https://api.telegram.org/bot${BOT_TOKEN}/sendMessage" \
    -d chat_id="${CHAT_ID}" -d text="Deploy of sha-9b1d4e7 failed on production"

Add this to the failure branch of your deploy workflow (Module 4), so the pipeline tells you when it breaks.

What to monitor

TargetCheckCatches
Public health URLHTTPS + keyword UPApp down, DNS or TLS problems, Nginx down
Frontend URLHTTPS 200Failed deploy on Vercel/Netlify, DNS errors
Certificate expiryDays leftFailed certbot renewal
Nightly jobHeartbeat pingBackups that silently stopped
Server resourcesDisk, memory (via Prometheus/CloudWatch)Problems before they become outages

Good alerting habits

  • Alert on user-visible failure, not on every internal blip.
  • Make each alert say what failed, since when, and where to look first (link to the runbook).
  • Do not alert your whole team on a learning project, and do not mute alerts permanently because they are noisy; fix the noise.
  • Test the whole path by stopping the app once and checking that your phone receives the alert.

Mistakes

  • Monitoring only the home page of the frontend and never the API.
  • Alerts going to an email address nobody reads (compare the billing alert advice in Module 5).
  • Pinging a free, sleeping service every few minutes to keep it awake: it can break the platform's rules or use up its allowance. Read the platform terms.
  • Checking the root URL / that serves a cached or static page and stays 200 when the API is dead.

Interview: "How do you know your service is down before customers do?"

  • External uptime checks on a real health URL with keyword assertion, alert routing to chat or phone, metrics-based alerts on error rate and latency, and an on-call or at least a runbook.

Prometheus and Grafana: metrics from Spring Boot with Docker Compose

Spring Boot (Actuator plus Micrometer) exposes metrics at /actuator/prometheus; Prometheus pulls and stores them on a schedule defined in prometheus.yml, and Grafana draws dashboards from Prometheus.

The pull model

Prometheus is a time-series database and monitoring system. It uses a pull model: instead of your app sending data, Prometheus visits each target's metrics URL every few seconds ("scrapes" it) and stores the numbers with timestamps. The advantages are simple targets (they only expose text), and Prometheus itself notices a dead target, because the scrape fails (the metric up becomes 0). Grafana is the dashboard tool; it reads from Prometheus (and from Loki, PostgreSQL, CloudWatch and others) and draws graphs.

Expose metrics from Spring Boot

Micrometer is the metrics facade inside Spring Boot (like SLF4J for logging). The Prometheus registry turns its metrics into Prometheus text format. Add the dependency (the version is managed by Boot):

<dependency>
  <groupId>io.micrometer</groupId>
  <artifactId>micrometer-registry-prometheus</artifactId>
</dependency>
management.endpoints.web.exposure.include=health,info,prometheus
management.metrics.tags.application=shop-api
management.metrics.distribution.percentiles-histogram.http.server.requests=true
# optional: serve Actuator on a private port that is never published
management.server.port=8081
  • management.metrics.tags.application adds a label to every metric so you can tell apps apart in one Prometheus.
  • percentiles-histogram…http.server.requests=true publishes histogram buckets for request time. Without them you cannot calculate p95 with histogram_quantile (see the PromQL article).
  • management.server.port keeps metrics off the public port. Prometheus reaches api:8081 inside the Docker network; Nginx does not proxy to it.

Open the endpoint to see the format: lines of name{labels} value.

# HELP http_server_requests_seconds Duration of HTTP server request handling
# TYPE http_server_requests_seconds histogram
http_server_requests_seconds_count{method="GET",status="200",uri="/api/products"} 1542
http_server_requests_seconds_sum{method="GET",status="200",uri="/api/products"} 61.4
http_server_requests_seconds_bucket{le="0.1",method="GET",status="200",uri="/api/products"} 1390
jvm_memory_used_bytes{area="heap",id="G1 Eden Space"} 5.2428800E7
process_cpu_usage 0.031
hikaricp_connections_active{pool="HikariPool-1"} 2.0

Metric types

Counter
A number that only goes up (requests served). You almost always look at its rate of change, with rate().
Gauge
A value that goes up and down (heap used, active connections).
Histogram / timer
Counts observations in buckets (_bucket), plus a total (_sum) and a count (_count). Used for latency.

The scrape configuration

global:
  scrape_interval: 15s            # how often to scrape
  evaluation_interval: 15s        # how often to evaluate alert rules

rule_files:
  - /etc/prometheus/alerts.yml

scrape_configs:
  - job_name: shop-api
    metrics_path: /actuator/prometheus
    static_configs:
      - targets: ["api:8081"]     # service name and management port on the Compose network

  - job_name: prometheus          # Prometheus monitors itself too
    static_configs:
      - targets: ["localhost:9090"]

A job is a group of targets with the same purpose; every sample gets the labels job and instance (the address). Spring Boot's default path is not Prometheus's default path, so metrics_path is essential. Use static_configs for fixed hosts; on Kubernetes or AWS Prometheus finds targets through service discovery.

Run Prometheus and Grafana with Docker Compose

services:
  prometheus:
    image: prom/prometheus
    volumes:
      - ./prometheus/prometheus.yml:/etc/prometheus/prometheus.yml:ro
      - ./prometheus/alerts.yml:/etc/prometheus/alerts.yml:ro
      - prometheus-data:/prometheus
    ports:
      - "127.0.0.1:9090:9090"       # only reachable from the server itself
    restart: unless-stopped

  grafana:
    image: grafana/grafana
    environment:
      GF_SECURITY_ADMIN_PASSWORD: ${GRAFANA_PASSWORD}
    volumes:
      - grafana-data:/var/lib/grafana
    ports:
      - "127.0.0.1:3000:3000"
    depends_on:
      - prometheus
    restart: unless-stopped

volumes:
  prometheus-data:
  grafana-data:
$ docker compose -f compose.yaml -f compose.monitoring.yaml up -d
$ ssh -i shop-key.pem -L 3000:127.0.0.1:3000 -L 9090:127.0.0.1:9090 ubuntu@203.0.113.25
# then open http://localhost:3000 (Grafana) and http://localhost:9090 (Prometheus)

Use the pinned image versions you have tested rather than the default latest tag in real setups. Both ports are bound to the loopback address and reached through an SSH tunnel, so no extra security-group rules are needed. If you want Grafana on a public domain later, put it behind Nginx with HTTPS and a strong admin password.

  1. In Prometheus open Status → Targets: the shop-api target should be UP. If it is DOWN, read the error message (wrong port, wrong path, network name).
  2. In Grafana log in as admin with the password you set (Grafana asks you to change a default password if you never set one).
  3. Connections → Data sources → Add data source → Prometheus, URL http://prometheus:9090 (the Compose service name, not localhost). Save and test.
  4. Dashboards → New → Import. Search grafana.com/grafana/dashboards for a "JVM (Micrometer)" or "Spring Boot" dashboard, enter its ID or JSON, pick the Prometheus data source and import. Community dashboards expect specific label names, such as the application tag set above, so adjust the dashboard variables if panels are empty.
  5. Build your own panel for the golden signals using the queries in the next article.

For machine-level metrics (CPU, memory, disk of the server) add node_exporter as another scrape target; for per-container metrics, cAdvisor. Together with application metrics, they cover saturation.

Rule: metrics endpoints are not public

Metrics reveal your endpoints, versions and load. Expose /actuator/prometheus only on an internal port or protect it. Never publish Prometheus or Grafana with default passwords.

Mistakes

  • Target DOWN because Prometheus uses localhost:8081 inside its own container; use the service name.
  • Forgetting metrics_path and getting 404.
  • Not persisting data (prometheus-data volume), so history disappears on recreate.
  • Labels with unbounded values (user IDs), which explode memory.

Edge cases

  • Prometheus stores data locally for a limited time (the retention setting); it is not a long-term archive. Long-term storage needs remote write to another system.
  • Short-lived jobs may finish between scrapes; for those a Pushgateway exists.
  • A scrape interval of 15s with rate(...[5m]) works well; the range should hold several samples.

Interview: "How do you monitor a Spring Boot app with Prometheus and Grafana?"

  • Actuator plus the Micrometer Prometheus registry expose /actuator/prometheus; Prometheus scrapes it (pull model) per prometheus.yml; Grafana queries Prometheus; alert rules go through Alertmanager.
  • Mention metric types, the application tag, and keeping the endpoint private.

PromQL basics: rate, error rate, p95 latency and alert rules

PromQL selects time series by name and labels; use rate(counter[5m]) for per-second speed, divide two sums for an error ratio, and histogram_quantile(0.95, …) over buckets for p95 latency; alert rules turn a query into a notification.

Selectors and ranges

PromQL is Prometheus's query language. A name alone returns the latest value of every series with that name; braces filter by labels.

http_server_requests_seconds_count
http_server_requests_seconds_count{uri="/api/products"}
http_server_requests_seconds_count{status=~"5.."}        # regex match: any 5xx
http_server_requests_seconds_count{status!="200"}        # not equal
http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m]                   # a range: the last 5 minutes of samples

The operators are =, !=, =~ (regex) and !~. A selector with [5m] is a range vector and can only be passed to functions such as rate; one without a range is an instant vector.

The golden signals as queries

Rate (traffic)
rate(x[5m]) gives the per-second increase of a counter averaged over 5 minutes. It handles counter resets after restarts. Never graph a raw counter; it only climbs.
sum / sum by
Adds series together, optionally keeping some labels: sum by (uri) (...).
increase
increase(x[1h]) gives the total increase over the range (requests in the last hour).
histogram_quantile
Estimates a percentile from histogram buckets.
sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m]))

# per endpoint, busiest first
topk(5, sum by (uri) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m])))
sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{status=~"5.."}[5m]))
/
sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m]))
# average response time
sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_sum[5m]))
/
sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m]))

# 95th percentile (needs the histogram buckets enabled in Spring Boot)
histogram_quantile(0.95,
  sum by (le) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket[5m])))

For p95, you must keep the le label ("less or equal", the bucket bound) while summing; that is what sum by (le) does. If you group also by uri, add it: sum by (le, uri). The result is an estimate based on bucket sizes, not an exact number.

# heap usage as a fraction
sum(jvm_memory_used_bytes{area="heap"}) / sum(jvm_memory_max_bytes{area="heap"})

# database connection pool: busy connections vs the maximum
hikaricp_connections_active / hikaricp_connections_max

# process CPU usage (0 to 1 of the CPU available to the process)
process_cpu_usage

# is every target up? (1 = scrape succeeded)
up{job="shop-api"}

Alert rules

An alert rule is a query plus a condition and a duration. When the condition holds for the whole for period, the alert fires. Prometheus then sends it to Alertmanager, which groups, de-duplicates and routes alerts to email, Slack, Telegram (via a webhook receiver) or paging tools. Grafana also has its own alerting if you prefer to manage everything there.

groups:
  - name: shop-api
    rules:
      - alert: ApiDown
        expr: up{job="shop-api"} == 0
        for: 2m
        labels:
          severity: critical
        annotations:
          summary: "shop-api is not reachable by Prometheus"

      - alert: HighErrorRate
        expr: |
          sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count{status=~"5.."}[5m]))
          / sum(rate(http_server_requests_seconds_count[5m])) > 0.05
        for: 5m
        labels:
          severity: warning
        annotations:
          summary: "More than 5% of requests fail"

      - alert: SlowRequests
        expr: |
          histogram_quantile(0.95,
            sum by (le) (rate(http_server_requests_seconds_bucket[5m]))) > 1
        for: 10m
        labels:
          severity: warning
        annotations:
          summary: "p95 latency is above one second"

The file is loaded through rule_files in prometheus.yml. Check rule syntax with promtool check rules alerts.yml (the tool ships with Prometheus) before you restart. In the Prometheus UI, the Alerts page shows pending and firing alerts. Remember a catch: an alert that depends on the app's own metrics cannot fire if the whole server is down, which is why the external uptime monitor from the previous article remains necessary.

Reading and building a dashboard

  • Make one row per golden signal: traffic (requests per second), errors (percentage), latency (p50 and p95 lines), saturation (heap, CPU, pool usage).
  • Show the same time range for all panels and annotate deployments, so a spike can be linked to a release.
  • Use the panel's unit settings (seconds, percent 0.0–1.0, bytes) to make numbers readable.

Mistakes

  • Graphing a counter without rate(): a line that only goes up.
  • Using rate() on a gauge, or a range shorter than twice the scrape interval (no data).
  • Dividing two queries without sum, so the labels do not match and the result is empty.
  • Averaging percentiles across instances. Sum the buckets first and then use histogram_quantile.
  • p95 returns "no data": the histogram buckets were never enabled in Spring Boot.

Tip: build queries step by step in the Prometheus UI

Start with the plain metric name, add a label filter, wrap it in rate, then in sum. Open the Graph tab at each step and check that the result makes sense.

Interview: "Write a query for the error rate and p95 latency."

  • Error rate: sum of 5xx request rate divided by the sum of all request rate. p95: histogram_quantile(0.95, sum by (le) (rate(..._bucket[5m]))).
  • Explain counters vs gauges, why rate, and why alerts need a for duration.

Kubernetes basics: why, architecture and the main objects

Kubernetes runs and manages many containers across many machines: you declare the state you want (three copies of this image, exposed on this port), and the control plane keeps reality equal to it by scheduling, restarting, scaling and rolling out updates.

The problem it solves

Docker Compose runs containers on one machine. Real systems need more: place containers on whichever machine has room, replace a container that crashed, start more copies when load rises, update without downtime, and survive the loss of a whole machine. Doing that by hand with scripts does not scale. Kubernetes (also written K8s) is an open-source container orchestrator, originally from Google, that does these jobs. Its core ideas:

  • Scheduling: it chooses a node with enough CPU and memory for each container.
  • Self-healing: failed containers are restarted; containers that fail health checks are replaced; work moves away from a dead node.
  • Scaling: change the number of replicas by hand or automatically by load (the Horizontal Pod Autoscaler).
  • Rolling updates and rollback: new versions replace old ones gradually; one command returns to the previous version.
  • Service discovery and load balancing: stable names and addresses for changing containers.

The model is declarative: you write YAML describing the desired state and submit it. A controller compares the actual state with the desired state in a loop and fixes any difference. You do not say "start a container"; you say "there should be three".

Architecture

Control plane
The brain. The API server is the front door for every command; etcd stores the cluster state; the scheduler assigns new pods to nodes; the controller manager runs the loops that match actual state to desired state.
Node
A worker machine (a VM or physical server) that runs pods.
kubelet
The agent on each node. It talks to the API server, starts the containers for pods assigned to the node, and runs the health probes.
Container runtime
The software that actually runs containers on a node (containerd, for example). kube-proxy handles Service networking on each node.

The main objects

ObjectWhat it is
PodThe smallest unit: one or more containers sharing a network address and storage. Pods are temporary; you rarely create them directly.
ReplicaSetKeeps a stated number of identical pods running. Created and managed by a Deployment.
DeploymentDeclares an app: image, replicas, update strategy. Manages ReplicaSets so updates are rolling and reversible.
ServiceA stable name and virtual IP in front of a changing set of pods, chosen by labels. Load-balances between them.
IngressHTTP(S) routing from outside by host and path to Services; needs an ingress controller (for example Nginx).
ConfigMapNon-secret configuration (environment values, files).
SecretSensitive values (passwords, tokens). Stored base64-encoded, which is not encryption; restrict access and consider encryption at rest and external secret stores.
NamespaceA named partition of the cluster (dev, staging, shop) for organising and limiting resources.

Others you will meet: StatefulSet (pods with stable identity and disks, for databases), Job and CronJob (run to completion, scheduled), PersistentVolumeClaim (a request for storage), HorizontalPodAutoscaler and DaemonSet (one pod per node).

Labels and selectors: how objects find each other

Objects carry labels, simple key/value tags such as app: shop-api. A Deployment's selector says which pods it owns; a Service's selector says which pods it sends traffic to. If the labels do not match, the Service has no backends: the most common beginner problem.

Service types

TypeReachable fromUse for
ClusterIP (default)Inside the cluster onlyBackends, databases, anything behind an Ingress
NodePortEach node's IP on a high port (30000–32767)Quick tests, local clusters
LoadBalancerA cloud load balancer with a public addressExposing one service on a cloud cluster

On a cloud cluster, one Ingress with a single load balancer usually fronts many Services, which is cheaper than a LoadBalancer Service per app.

Request path

Where Kubernetes fits

Kubernetes shines with many services, several teams and the need for automated scaling and updates. It brings real complexity: networking, storage, security, upgrades and YAML. For one Spring Boot app, a single server with Compose, or a PaaS, is simpler and cheaper. Learn Kubernetes to read and operate it, because many companies run it; do not use it to run your first project.

Mistakes

  • Running a database as a plain Deployment with no persistent storage plan. Use managed databases, or StatefulSets with care.
  • Believing a Secret is encrypted. It is only base64 by default.
  • Using the latest image tag, so a pod restart can pull a different version.
  • Setting no resource requests or limits; one greedy pod starves the others.

Interview: "Explain Pod, Deployment and Service."

  • Pod: the unit that runs containers, temporary. Deployment: declares replicas and image, handles rolling updates and rollback through ReplicaSets. Service: stable address and load balancing across matching pods by labels.
  • Add the control loop idea: desired state versus actual state.

Deploying Spring Boot to Kubernetes: YAML, probes, kubectl and local clusters

A Deployment (image, replicas, resources, probes pointing at Actuator's liveness and readiness URLs) plus a Service gives you a self-healing, rolling-updating Spring Boot app; apply it with kubectl apply -f and manage it with get, describe, logs and rollout.

The complete manifest

A manifest is a YAML file describing objects; --- separates several in one file. This one creates a namespace, configuration, a Deployment with two replicas, and a Service. The names and image are examples.

apiVersion: v1
kind: Namespace
metadata:
  name: shop
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: ConfigMap
metadata:
  name: shop-config
  namespace: shop
data:
  SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE: "prod"
  JAVA_TOOL_OPTIONS: "-XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75"
---
apiVersion: apps/v1
kind: Deployment
metadata:
  name: shop-api
  namespace: shop
  labels:
    app: shop-api
spec:
  replicas: 2
  selector:
    matchLabels:
      app: shop-api                  # which pods this Deployment owns
  strategy:
    type: RollingUpdate
    rollingUpdate:
      maxSurge: 1                    # at most one extra pod during an update
      maxUnavailable: 0              # never go below the desired count
  template:
    metadata:
      labels:
        app: shop-api                # must match the selector above
    spec:
      terminationGracePeriodSeconds: 40
      imagePullSecrets:
        - name: ghcr-pull            # only needed for a private registry
      containers:
        - name: api
          image: ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:sha-3f9c2ab   # always a specific tag
          ports:
            - containerPort: 8080
          envFrom:
            - configMapRef:
                name: shop-config
            - secretRef:
                name: shop-secrets   # DB password, JWT secret (created with kubectl)
          resources:
            requests:                # what the scheduler reserves
              cpu: "250m"
              memory: "512Mi"
            limits:                  # the hard ceiling
              memory: "768Mi"
          startupProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /actuator/health/liveness
              port: 8080
            periodSeconds: 5
            failureThreshold: 30     # up to 150 seconds to start
          readinessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /actuator/health/readiness
              port: 8080
            periodSeconds: 10
            failureThreshold: 3
          livenessProbe:
            httpGet:
              path: /actuator/health/liveness
              port: 8080
            periodSeconds: 10
            failureThreshold: 3
---
apiVersion: v1
kind: Service
metadata:
  name: shop-api
  namespace: shop
spec:
  type: ClusterIP
  selector:
    app: shop-api                    # sends traffic to pods with this label
  ports:
    - port: 80                       # the Service's port
      targetPort: 8080               # the container's port

Create the sensitive objects from the command line, not from files in Git:

$ kubectl create secret generic shop-secrets -n shop \
    --from-literal=SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL='jdbc:postgresql://db.example.internal:5432/shop' \
    --from-literal=SPRING_DATASOURCE_USERNAME='shop_app' \
    --from-literal=SPRING_DATASOURCE_PASSWORD='change-me' \
    --from-literal=JWT_SECRET='change-me-too'

$ kubectl create secret docker-registry ghcr-pull -n shop \
    --docker-server=ghcr.io --docker-username=asha --docker-password="$GHCR_TOKEN"

The pieces explained

replicas: 2
Run two identical pods. If one dies, the ReplicaSet creates another.
selector and labels
matchLabels must equal the pod template's labels. The Service selects the same label.
RollingUpdate
With maxSurge: 1 and maxUnavailable: 0, Kubernetes starts one new pod, waits until it is Ready, then stops an old one. This is zero-downtime when readiness is correct.
envFrom
Turns every key in a ConfigMap or Secret into an environment variable. Spring Boot reads SPRING_DATASOURCE_URL by relaxed binding, as in the earlier modules.
requests and limits
Requests are what the scheduler reserves for the pod; limits are the ceiling. Exceeding the memory limit gets the container killed (OOMKilled). CPU over its limit is only throttled. Set the JVM heap below the memory limit (MaxRAMPercentage=75).
terminationGracePeriodSeconds
How long Kubernetes waits after SIGTERM before killing. Match it to spring.lifecycle.timeout-per-shutdown-phase with server.shutdown=graceful.

Probes mapped to Actuator

ProbeFailing meansSpring Boot URL
startupProbeStill starting; the other probes wait. If it never passes, the container is restarted./actuator/health/liveness
readinessProbeNot ready; removed from the Service endpoints, not restarted/actuator/health/readiness
livenessProbeBroken; the container is restarted/actuator/health/liveness

Spring Boot turns on these probe endpoints automatically when it detects it runs in Kubernetes; if you deploy elsewhere, set management.endpoint.health.probes.enabled=true. The startup probe protects a slow-starting JVM from being killed by the liveness probe. If you moved Actuator to its own port (management.server.port=8081), point the probes at that port. The reasons for keeping the database out of liveness are in Health checks.

An Ingress for HTTP from outside

apiVersion: networking.k8s.io/v1
kind: Ingress
metadata:
  name: shop-api
  namespace: shop
spec:
  ingressClassName: nginx
  rules:
    - host: api.example.com
      http:
        paths:
          - path: /
            pathType: Prefix
            backend:
              service:
                name: shop-api
                port:
                  number: 80

TLS certificates in Kubernetes are commonly handled by cert-manager, which obtains Let's Encrypt certificates for Ingress hosts, the cluster version of certbot.

kubectl essentials

kubectl is the command-line client for the API server.

CommandPurpose
kubectl apply -f file.yamlCreate or update objects to match the file
kubectl get pods -n shopList pods (also deploy, svc, ing, all)
kubectl describe pod NAME -n shopDetails and the Events at the bottom (why it fails)
kubectl logs -f NAME -n shopFollow a pod's logs (--previous for the crashed container)
kubectl exec -it NAME -n shop -- shOpen a shell inside a container
kubectl rollout status deploy/shop-api -n shopWait for an update to finish
kubectl rollout undo deploy/shop-api -n shopRoll back to the previous revision
kubectl scale deploy/shop-api --replicas=4 -n shopChange the replica count
kubectl port-forward svc/shop-api 8080:80 -n shopReach a Service from your laptop for testing
kubectl delete -f file.yamlDelete what the file describes
 
$ kubectl apply -f k8s/shop-api.yaml
namespace/shop created
configmap/shop-config created
deployment.apps/shop-api created
service/shop-api created

$ kubectl get pods -n shop
NAME                        READY   STATUS    RESTARTS   AGE
shop-api-6d9c7b8f5d-4kq2x   1/1     Running   0          48s
shop-api-6d9c7b8f5d-v7m9p   1/1     Running   0          48s

$ kubectl port-forward svc/shop-api 8080:80 -n shop
$ curl localhost:8080/actuator/health

# release a new version
$ kubectl set image deploy/shop-api api=ghcr.io/asha/shop-api:sha-9b1d4e7 -n shop
$ kubectl rollout status deploy/shop-api -n shop
$ kubectl rollout undo deploy/shop-api -n shop          # if something is wrong
$ kubectl rollout history deploy/shop-api -n shop

In a pipeline, the deploy step runs kubectl set image or kubectl apply with the new SHA tag (or a GitOps tool such as Argo CD does it from Git), exactly like the SSH deploy in Module 4.

Reading failures

StatusMeaningLook at
ImagePullBackOffCannot pull the image: wrong name or tag, private registry without credentialsdescribe pod Events; imagePullSecrets
CrashLoopBackOffThe container starts and exits repeatedlylogs --previous, as in the crash walkthrough
PendingNo node has enough free CPU or memory, or a volume is unavailabledescribe pod: scheduling events
OOMKilled (last state)Memory limit exceededRaise the limit, lower the heap
Running but 0/1 ReadyReadiness probe failingProbe URL, port, startup time

Where to run a cluster

  • Local learning: minikube (minikube start), kind (Kubernetes in Docker: kind create cluster; load a local image with kind load docker-image NAME:TAG), or the Kubernetes option in Docker Desktop.
  • Managed in the cloud: EKS (AWS), GKE (Google) and AKS (Azure) run the control plane for you; you pay for it and for the worker nodes. Learn locally first; a managed cluster left running is a surprise cost.
  • Helm: a package manager for Kubernetes. A chart is a templated bundle of manifests, installed with helm install and configured through a values file.

Docker Compose vs Kubernetes

Docker ComposeKubernetes
MachinesOne hostA cluster of nodes
Self-healing and schedulingRestart policy onlyFull: reschedules on other nodes
Rolling updatesRecreate (brief downtime)Built in, with rollback
ScalingManual, single hostManual or automatic
Learning costLowHigh
Best forLocal development, small single-server appsMany services, teams, high availability

Rule: every container gets probes, requests and a pinned image tag

Without readiness probes, rolling updates can send traffic to a pod that is still starting. Without requests and limits, scheduling and memory behaviour are unpredictable. Without a pinned tag, you cannot say what is running.

Mistakes

  • Selector and template labels that do not match (the apply is rejected), or a Service selector that matches nothing (no endpoints; check with kubectl get endpoints).
  • Liveness probe with a short delay on a slow JVM: pods are killed while starting. Use a startup probe.
  • Memory limit equal to the heap size: the JVM also needs non-heap memory, so it gets OOMKilled.
  • Committing Secrets with real values to Git.

Interview: "How do you deploy a Spring Boot app on Kubernetes and do a zero-downtime release?"

  • Image with a SHA tag; Deployment with replicas, resources and probes on Actuator readiness and liveness; Service and Ingress; rolling update with maxUnavailable: 0 and graceful shutdown; kubectl rollout status and rollout undo.

Terraform basics: infrastructure as code

Terraform lets you describe cloud resources in code, preview the changes with terraform plan, create them with apply, and remove them with destroy, keeping a state file that records what exists, so environments are repeatable and reviewable like source code.

Why infrastructure as code

Clicking in the console is fine for learning, but it cannot be reviewed, repeated or undone cleanly. If your production server was clicked together by hand, nobody can say exactly how, and a second copy for testing is guesswork. Infrastructure as code (IaC) keeps the description of servers, networks and databases in text files in Git. You get history, code review, repeatable environments, and a one-command cleanup (a real help against surprise bills, see Module 5).

Terraform (by HashiCorp; a community fork named OpenTofu also exists) is the most widely used IaC tool. It is declarative: you state the desired end result, and Terraform works out what to create, change or delete. It works with any platform that has a provider.

Core concepts

Provider
A plugin that talks to a platform's API: aws, google, azurerm, kubernetes, github. Downloaded by terraform init.
Resource
A thing to create: aws_instance, aws_security_group, aws_s3_bucket. Written as resource "type" "local_name" { ... }.
Data source
Read-only information looked up from outside, such as the latest Ubuntu AMI.
Variable
An input (region, instance type) so code can be reused. Set with defaults, -var, or a .tfvars file.
Output
A value printed after apply (the server's public IP).
State
A file (terraform.tfstate) mapping your code to the real resources. Terraform compares code, state and reality to produce a plan.
Module
A reusable folder of Terraform code, called with inputs.

A tiny example: an EC2 instance, a security group and an Elastic IP

This creates the web server from the Module 5 runbook. Put it in a folder as main.tf. It uses the default VPC of the region. Pin the provider version range you have tested; the range below is only a starting point.

terraform {
  required_version = ">= 1.5"
  required_providers {
    aws = {
      source  = "hashicorp/aws"
      version = ">= 5.0"
    }
  }
}

provider "aws" {
  region = var.region
}

variable "region" {
  type    = string
  default = "ap-south-1"
}

variable "instance_type" {
